The test match is `tests/test_c01_route_limits.py:27` (patch line 75 of the rebuilt `C-01.patch`, and line 54 of `C-01-price-only.patch`; the earlier ':28' was a line of the first-version file) (`match="zero or negative price"`, read in C-01.patch). Closed on reviewer words and the runner's passing tests only (said this was a weaker DECLINE than the others because the two lines it rests on were patched-copy lines I did not read; both lines are now read and explained above, the runner's passing tests are still reviewer-side evidence I did not re-run). |
| C-6 | Nit | reviewer finding | PROPOSE FIX | Add a rollout note to the README: any pilot or production config with a price of 0 now refuses to start (the max_tokens-above-the-ceiling part of the note is moot while the ceiling is cut, like C-2 and X-1; the exact note, including the self-hosted model case, is in C-8). Demos are on disk and the ask-your-data demo builds `Route` from its own config (`askdata/config.py:64-69`) with whatever prices its config file holds. (grep over `demos/`, src): `usd_per_mtok` appears only in the demo's `.py` files (`askdata/config.py:42-43,66-67`, `fixtures.py:25` with prices 2 and 10, `meter.py:53-54`); no YAML, JSON or TOML file in the demo carries it, so the demo's prices come from code (`fixtures.py:25`, a fake model) or from whatever config a client passes in; no demo price for a kind other than `fake` was found, and none was run. |
| C-7 | Minor | reviewer finding, split off from C-3 | PROPOSE FIX | UNCONDITIONAL (does not depend on the Route-config condition; not part of patch C-01) and ONE CHANGE WITH C-3 (the same places: `llm/common.py:164-171`, `llm/fake.py:51-54`, `Limits.charge`), but NOT PRESENT as a seen defect: a MISSING GUARD at the sinks, not a defect that a known producer reaches. Producers read: `llm/anthropic.py:142` (typed SDK field), `llm/openai_compat.py:142` and `llm/bedrock.py:176` (`usage.get(...)` from the response body; the openai_compat endpoint is client-configured, the only plausible hostile source), `llm/fake.py:54` (a recording). Reachable only through a misbehaving openai_compat endpoint or a bad recording. It is counted apart from the defects present in today's code. The same spend meter also fails on a negative or zero TOKEN COUNT and on a negative usd, Read from source: `llm/common.py:164-165` takes `tokens[0] or estimate_tokens(...)`, so a zero count is replaced by an estimate but a NEGATIVE count is truthy and passes; `llm/common.py:166-169` (the multiplication is at 167-168, the division at 169) and `llm/fake.py:51-52` multiply it by the price, so the cost comes out negative; `Reply` (`models.py:294-302`) has plain `int` and `Decimal` fields with no `ge=0` (`Verdict.cost_usd`, `models.py:310-312`, the same), and `fake.py:54` builds `Reply(**data, cost_usd=cost, model_id=r.model_id)` from a recorded file whose counts are whatever the file says; the sinks accept it: `Limits.charge` (`limits.py:17-18`), `MemoryStore.add_spend` (`store/memory.py:196-198`) and `add_cost` (`:76-81`), `PostgresStore.add_spend` (`store/postgres.py:416-430`, an upsert that adds `usd`) and `add_cost` (`:276-277`), and `k1b/loop.py:47-48` `Budget.charge` (`self.spent += usd`), so a negative amount moves spend DOWN and a cap never fires. I found no real provider path that returns a negative count (the three provider lines above were read; none can be shown to yield one), so this is a hardening of the choke point, not a seen bug. Fix : FIRST, on a count that is not greater than zero, do NOT fall back to `estimate_tokens(...)` alone (read in `llm/common.py:59-62` and `:164-165`: the estimate is `max(1, len(text) // 3)` of what was sent or received, so a broken or hostile endpoint that returns a bad count with a short reply would be charged LESS than the call really cost, which fails open on a money path). Charge the WORST CASE for the call instead: `(max_tokens x output price + estimate_tokens(sent) x input price) / 1,000,000` (`reply()` already divides the same way at `:169`), log one line (route, which count was bad, no text) and raise an alert a person sees; this also changes today's zero case (zero is replaced by the estimate now), so say so in the CHANGELOG and test it; keep the `ge=0` backstop below. The reason the check must come before the `Reply(...)` call: a `ge=0` that raises inside `llm/common.py:171` fires AFTER the provider call is billed and before any charge (`limits.py:17`), so the failure points toward UNDER-counting spend. THEN keep, as a backstop where every consumer routes: `ge=0` on `Reply.input_tokens`, `output_tokens`, `cost_usd`, `latency_ms` and `Verdict.cost_usd` (the producer of every reply), plus a refusal of a negative `usd` in `Limits.charge`, both stores' `add_spend` and `add_cost`, and `Budget.charge` (the sinks), because a `model_copy(update=...)` skips field validators (see (b); `tests/test_k5_tools.py:720` builds a Reply that way). Not traced: how the callers of the llm kinds handle the `ValidationError` a negative count would now raise inside `llm/common.py:171` (the `Reply(` call; a reply that fails to build must end the call in a visible error, not a crash loop); the test must cover that. Tests: negative input count, negative output count and negative usd each refused, in `Limits.charge` and in both stores. Cross-reference: the same refusal of a lowered spend must exist at the database (see the spend trigger in X-6), so the code check in `Limits.charge` and the stores gives a clear error first and the database trigger is the backstop. Rating Minor. Reason: the row itself says NOT PRESENT as a seen defect: no producer read returns a negative (`llm/anthropic.py:142` typed SDK field; `llm/openai_compat.py:142` and `llm/bedrock.py:176` `usage.get(...)`; `llm/fake.py:54` a recording). THREAT MODEL (T2 in 'Words and scales', stated once there; repeated here): the only plausible source is a hostile or compromised openai_compat endpoint (client-configured) or a tampered recording file; an endpoint like that already sees every prompt, so the added harm is that spend caps stop firing. If the studio head puts such an endpoint in the threat model (a client may point the openai_compat kind at a host it does not control), C-7 goes back to Major. |
| C-8 | Minor | reviewer finding | PROPOSE FIX | A self-hosted model priced at zero is refused too. C-01 hunk 2 (`C-01.patch` lines 14-27 (the `raise ValueError` is at 27): `if self.backends.llm.kind != "fake":` then `min(input, output) <= 0` raises `ValueError`) exempts ONLY the kind `fake`. A model you run yourself (kind `openai_compat`, registered as neutral at `backends/table.py:529-535`; its settings accept plain http to a loopback address with no key, `table.py:71-85`, comment at `:73`: 'a local Ollama needs none') with a price of 0 would fail to load: 'routes with a zero or negative price'. That is the intended money rule (at a price of 0 no dollar limit can fire), but this file did not say it covers the self-hosted model, which is the neutral default under the no-single-dependency rule. Read: K1b replay already refuses a zero price for every kind except `fake` (`k1b/replay.py:85-90`), so the patch widens an existing rule to config load. DECISION RECORDED: the only exemption is kind `fake`. No exemption for a loopback address or a missing key, because the same kind `openai_compat` also points at paid hosted endpoints, and an address or flag test would reopen the bug for them. A self-hosted model needs a POSITIVE NOTIONAL price: a number the operator sets to stand for what the machine costs per million tokens (for example the machine's hourly cost divided by the millions of tokens it handles in an hour), so the per-run, daily and monthly limits still fire; the number is the studio head's (Decision 19). EXACT README ROLLOUT NOTE to add: 'Rollout note: after this patch every route used with a model kind other than fake needs both prices above 0, a self-hosted model included (kind openai_compat on your own server). Set a notional price that stands for what the machine costs per million tokens, so the spend limits still fire. A config with a price of 0 now refuses to start with: routes with a zero or negative price: [...]. Only kind fake may cost nothing.' ONE TEST TO ADD (not written into the patch, not run) to `tests/test_c01_route_limits.py`: `test_a_self_hosted_model_priced_zero_is_refused_and_a_notional_price_loads`, with `LOCAL_LLM = {"kind": "openai_compat", "base_url": "http://127.0.0.1:11434/v1"}` (the keyless loopback shape that `OpenAICompatSettings` accepts): `_load(cfg_dir, llm=LOCAL_LLM, input_usd_per_mtok="0")` must raise `ConfigError` matching 'zero or negative price' (this half fails on the old code), and `_load(cfg_dir, llm=LOCAL_LLM, input_usd_per_mtok="0.05", output_usd_per_mtok="0.05")` must load (this half passes on old code too and only guards the rule). Cost of the notional price: the dollar figure is not real money, so any report that adds up spend mixes real and notional dollars unless the route is labelled; say so in the README. Not checked: whether the fixture config loads with this llm kind for other reasons (host allowlist, environment table); the test was not run. Rating Minor: no defect in today's source or in the patch's behaviour; a rollout and lock-in note with one test (rating rule). ALSO READ IN SOURCE: the kind `fake` is in `DEMO_ONLY` (`backends/table.py:595`) and is refused when live (`config.py:394-396`), so in a pilot or production environment NO kind is exempt: every route there needs both prices above 0. The patch checks EVERY route in `self.routes`, not only the routes in use (`C-01.patch` lines 18-26: `for n, r in self.routes.items()`), so an unused route priced 0 also stops the start. The notional price is a number the operator sets: a price of 0.000001 passes the check, and the `budget_usd` limits must be in the same notional unit, or the limits compare different things. The test above was not run. |

## Patch A-01 (tick drains the audit outbox, logs a failed write): DEFERRED by the lead's default of 2026-11-16 (the studio head may veto it). No action now. If it returns: fix X-2 and the per-row guard A-6 first
| id | sev | merged from | decision | what to change |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| A-1 | Minor | reviewer finding | PROPOSE FIX | The docstring and README say the step drains events held "in memory". Checked: the memory list `_pending` is a plain attribute of the sink object (src `audit/chain.py:173`; `audit/local_dir.py:32` not re-read), so it lives only in the process that wrote it. The reviewer's reason, that the tick is a different program from the agent (the reviewer cited an order-file question id), could NOT be verified: that id appears only in review files, not in any source or design file I could open. The tick is also itself an audit producer (src `runtime/tick.py:133` and `:162` call `write_outcome`), so a tick-owned sink can hold memory events the same tick drains; the claim is therefore "agent-held memory cannot be drained by a separate tick process", not "memory is never drained". Reword docstring and README to say what the step drains (the shared outbox table or directory, plus this process's own memory list), and make the ERROR line say "lost if this process exits". The same in-process loss hits every short-lived program: the other `write_outcome` callers (src `limits.py:46` the emergency stop, `approvals/requests.py:82`, `runtime/agent_loop.py:241`, `k1b/audit_sink.py:39` and the tick itself at `runtime/tick.py:133`/`:162`) and the ask-your-data demo outcome (`demos/ask-your-data/askdata/audit.py:60`, `write_outcome`; src, opened by me) and the K1b CLI (`cli.py:268` `audit.emit_outcome`; `cli.py:262`, `:273`, `:275` are `audit.emit` = `write_intent`, which raises instead of queueing). Sibling checked: the other K1b `audit.emit` callers `k1b/replay.py:185`, `:198`, `:260` and the demo's `audit.py:45` and `:78` all go through `write_intent`, which raises `AuditUnavailable` and so loses nothing quietly; they need no drain. On the lossy path (if the database and the outbox both fail, the event goes only to `_pending` (src `audit/chain.py:229`) and is lost when the command exits). A tick in another process can never drain those. Rating Minor. Reason: the finding is about wording and one log message (what the step drains, what the ERROR line says), not a defect that loses or doubles an event; the drain does what its code says. Check of the claim 'the docstring and README say in memory': half wrong. The step docstring does (`patch-copies/A-01.patch:98`, the tick hunk: 'wait there (or in memory)'; the first-version file had it at line 86); the patch README does not say it for the drain (its section 'A-01: the tick drains the audit outbox' describes the step without it, and says the error text reads 'held in memory only' for the ERROR log line). So fix the docstring; the README needs no change for this. |
| A-2 | Minor | reviewer finding | PROPOSE FIX | In plain words: the FIRST-version step quietly skipped an audit sink that has no `reconcile` instead of turning red; the rebuilt copy `patch-copies/A-01.patch` already raises, so the fix is to use the copy. Rating (rule): Minor. SHOWN: the first-version patch used `getattr(audit, "reconcile", None)` and a test pinned the skip (`test_a_kind_without_reconcile_is_skipped`; read in the first-version file, which I can no longer open); the rebuilt copy fetches the method with `getattr` but raises `RuntimeError` when it is missing (`patch-copies/A-01.patch:102-104`) and its test is renamed `test_a_kind_without_reconcile_fails_the_step_instead_of_recording_a_success` (`:198-207`, asserting `step=audit_reconcile error_kind=RuntimeError` at `:207`; read, not run); the Protocol requires `reconcile` (`audit/protocol.py:27`). NOT shown: any sink the tick can hold that lacks `reconcile` (both registered kinds define it; `K1bAudit` lacks it but is never the tick's sink, `app.py:161`), so no loss or bypass is shown; the K1bAudit docstring (`audit_sink.py:38`) says the SINK keeps a failed event for `reconcile`, which is true of the sink; the real gap, that no K1b command calls the sink's `reconcile()`, is recorded under X-4. The wanted behaviour, a missing method turns the step red in tick_state and not green because the Protocol requires `reconcile` (src `audit/protocol.py:27`, returns int; `app.audit` is typed AuditSink), is what the rebuilt copy does; a direct call `ctx.app.audit.reconcile()` would give the same result with an AttributeError instead of the RuntimeError text. Dependency (checked): the tick's `app.audit` is built by `make("audit", ...)` at `app.py:161`, so it is a ChainAudit or a LocalDirSink and never a `K1bAudit` (`k1b/audit_sink.py:26` is a wrapper for the K1b CLI around such a sink; it is not an AuditSink, and the tick does not hold one). So the tick-side direct call does NOT depend on the K1bAudit decision, and a tick can not "point at" a K1bAudit. Separate real defect: the docstring of `K1bAudit.emit_outcome` (`audit_sink.py:38`) says that on failure the sink keeps the event "for `reconcile`", but `K1bAudit` has no `reconcile()` and nothing in the K1b CLI calls its sink's `reconcile()` (a short-lived process, see A-1), so those events are not drained there either (this is the same gap as X-4). Give K1bAudit a `reconcile()` that calls `self.sink.reconcile()` and call it at the end of each k1b command, or reword the docstring so it does not suggest that a K1b command drains. That choice stays with the studio head and is not a blocker for the tick step. |
| A-3 | Minor | reviewer finding | PROPOSE FIX | In plain words: a stuck backlog and an empty one write the same log line. Rating (rule): Minor. SHOWN (source read): `_drain` and the database-down path swallow errors (`audit/chain.py:238-255`, `audit/local_dir.py:126-156`), so `drained=0` looks identical for both. NOT shown: any loss: the waiting rows stay in the outbox (A-6) and are drained once the cause is gone; the harm is a hidden backlog, which is also why the patch is deferred. Rule part (a) needs a loss or a bypass, so Minor. A stuck backlog logs `drained=0`, the same as an empty outbox, because `_drain` and the database-down path swallow errors (src `audit/chain.py:238-255` and `audit/local_dir.py:126-156`, both read). Make reconcile report how many are left; log WARNING `audit_reconcile remaining=<n>` when above 0, or fail the step when work remains and nothing drained. CONTRACT CHANGE: `AuditSink.reconcile()` is declared `-> int` ("how many events were written", `audit/protocol.py:27-29`) and implemented by ChainAudit, LocalDirSink and any client sink. Returning a pair or a result object changes the Protocol and every implementation and caller, so it needs its own decision and a Protocol test; the smaller option is a separate method `pending_count()` that leaves `reconcile()` unchanged. Either option touches every sink, including sinks written by clients, and must work for the kinds that have no database (the local-directory kind): see the single-dependency check below. (src, opened by me; not run): (a) (demo-only, lower weight: the `local_dir` kind is in `DEMO_ONLY`, `backends/table.py:593-601`, and refused when live, `config.py:394-397`) For the local-directory kind the count must cover `outbox/corrupt/`: `reconcile` moves a file that fails to parse there (`audit/local_dir.py:143-147`) and its docstring says it is 'counted nowhere' (`:127-128`); the drain loop globs only `outbox/*.json` (`:141`), so a corrupt file is never retried and never counted. Give `pending_count()` two numbers, files still waiting in the outbox and files in `corrupt/` (and the memory list), and log a WARNING with the corrupt count on EVERY tick while it is above 0 (it stays above 0 until a person clears the folder, which is the point); the chain kind has no such folder, so its corrupt count is 0 and the unparsable-row case is A-6. (b) What changing the `reconcile()` return type would break (src, grep over `tests/`, `demos/`, `src/`; no other caller exists): 9 assertions on the int, not about 8 as the reviewer counted: `tests/test_cuj.py:202`, `tests/warden_port/test_audit_chain.py:514`, `:517` and `:677`, `tests/test_s2_audit.py:51`, `tests/test_review_fixes.py:105`, `tests/contracts/test_capabilities.py:45` and `:59`, and `tests/test_review_pr3_round2.py:321`; the three `reconcile` definitions (`audit/chain.py:232`, `audit/local_dir.py:126`, the Protocol `audit/protocol.py:27`) and every client sink. A separate `pending_count()` breaks none of the 9 and adds one Protocol method (a default for sinks without it, as above), which is why it is the smaller change. |
| A-4 | Minor | reviewer finding | PROPOSE FIX | Neither sink logs a failed last resort today (src, both opened by me). `audit/local_dir.py` has no logging import or log call at all: the write path is `write_outcome` at `local_dir.py:110-124` (its `except Exception` at `:116-117`, `os.replace` into the outbox at `:121`) and the drain is `reconcile` at `:126-156`; the reviewer cite `local_dir.py:130` does not hold in this tree. In `audit/chain.py:220-230` the last resort is a bare `self._pending.append(event)` with NO log line. So whether either kind has the ERROR line depends on A-01 adding it: both kinds need it, with a test each. Read in the rebuilt copy `patch-copies/A-01.patch` (the first-version file lacked both, so for the copy the claims 'no ERROR on the local-directory last resort' and 'no local-directory test' do not hold): the copy adds a WARNING (`:60`) AND an ERROR (`:72`, 'audit outbox not written ... held in memory only') to `LocalDirSink.write_outcome`, an ERROR to the chain kind (`:25`) and its own local-directory test `test_local_dir_sink_logs_a_warning_and_then_an_error_when_the_outbox_fails_too` (`:233`, builds `LocalDirSink` at `:236`; read, not run). The chain-kind tests build `audit.ChainAudit(audit._Db.memory())` (`:183`, `:211`, `:221`). So the log-line gap for the two sinks is closed by the copy and this row keeps only the points below. Also not said in the patch README: events drained by the new step are appended to the audit chain under the tick's own database login (see X-2 and X-5.1). Extra point (a Nit, checked: `models.py:196` `event_id` is a free `str`, so an agent-planted outbox row can carry any text): the new log lines write `event.event_id` (`patch-copies/A-01.patch` lines 12 and 25 for the chain kind, 60 and 72 for the local-directory kind); neutralise control characters and cap the length before logging it (for example escape non-printable characters and cut to 64 characters), and the same for `seq` or any other value read from an outbox row, and add a test with an id holding a newline and an ANSI escape. |
| A-5 | Minor | evidence run | PROPOSE FIX | Checked against evidence-13.md:99 and the first-version A-01.patch lines 190-205 (in the rebuilt copy the tick-test hunk is `patch-copies/A-01.patch:248-264`, the assertion that the step ran is at `:261`): it is NOT true that removing the step from STEPS leaves every test green. The evidence runner's mutation (c) removed the step from STEPS and two tests failed, `test_the_step_is_in_the_tick_list` and `test_steps_order_and_summary_line_is_not_a_step` (evidence-13.md:99), and the patch's own edit of `tests/test_p7_tick.py` runs `run_tick(app)` and asserts `get_tick_state("audit_reconcile").last_ok_at is not None`. What is still open, and small: (1) no test seeds an outbox row and shows that `run_tick` drains it (the five new tests call the function directly; the tick-path assertion runs against an empty outbox); (2) a sink without `reconcile` turning the step red belongs to A-2, not here. Add test (1). Rating Minor: one missing test remains. |
| A-6 | Minor | reviewer finding | PROPOSE FIX | No event is lost ("this is audit loss" does not follow from the code). Checked (src, opened by me): chain kind, `audit/chain.py:243`: `AuditEvent.model_validate_json(body)` runs OUTSIDE any try, so one bad outbox row raises out of the drain (audit `reconcile()`) BEFORE the DELETE at `:244`. The row stays in the table (nothing is lost) but every row after it, ordered by event_id (`chain.py:239`), is never drained on any later tick: head-of-line blocking. It is LOUD only if the A-01 step exists (today nothing calls `reconcile()`, see X-4 and the paragraph 'Present consequence of deferring A-01' at the end of 'Counts (by script)'): then the exception escapes the step, `runtime/tick.py:315-317` logs it at ERROR and `tick.py:319-324` stores it in `tick_state.last_error`, so the step goes red. Local-directory kind, `audit/local_dir.py:143-147`: a file that fails to parse is moved to `outbox/corrupt/` and the loop continues; the event is KEPT but silent: nobody is told (no logging at all in that file, see A-4). Other raise points in the same functions: `chain.py:244` DELETE unguarded (a database error escapes, same red step); `local_dir.py:144` catches only `ValueError`, so an `OSError` on read escapes; `local_dir.py:146` `os.replace` can raise; `local_dir.py:154` `path.unlink()` can raise if another process already removed the file (the practical form of X-3). Fix at the shared choke point (both sinks): catch the validation error per row, log the event id and a WARNING with the count of quarantined rows, keep the row or file for a person, and continue; cap the batch (about 100) in config. Add one test with a bad row between two good ones, per kind. (about a planted row, X-5.2): A-6's Minor holds for what it describes, a bad row that appears by accident (a bug, a half-written body), and needs no new row. It does NOT cover a bad row planted on purpose: the agent keeps INSERT on the outbox even after X-5 is applied, so head-of-line blocking can be created at will, and a planted row with a chosen `event_id` can replace the real event's content (X-5.2 (ii)); that second effect is a loss, so the "no event is lost" reason below is true only for accidental rows. I keep A-6 at Minor (the severity of the accident) and carry the planted case in X-5 (Major); the per-row catch and quarantine fix in A-6 is also the control that limits the planted case, so apply it whether or not X-5 is applied. Reason for Minor: no event is lost on either kind, the chain kind is loud once A-01 is applied, only the local-directory quarantine is silent. (`audit/chain.py:243` `model_validate_json` is outside any try): once A-01 is applied this unguarded line runs on every tick, so one bad outbox row stops every row behind it on every tick and turns the step red each time. The per-row guard (catch, quarantine, log the id, continue) is therefore a PRECONDITION of applying A-01, not an optional extra. A-6 stays Minor with or without the guard (delay, no loss shown); the guard is a precondition of A-01 anyway. |
| A-7 | Nit | reviewer finding | PROPOSE FIX | Log INFO only when the count is above 0. The new step name `audit_reconcile` sits next to the existing step `reconcile` (the approvals step; step names must match `[a-z_]+`, `tick.py:59-60`: the `fullmatch` is at `:59`, the raise at `:60`; the list is `STEPS` at `tick.py:249-266`; the log label is `tick.py:97`), so a person reading `reconcile` in a log or in `tick_state` can take it for the audit step. Suggest the name `audit_drain`, or at least a docstring line saying the two differ (a naming choice, not a defect). |
| A-8 | Nit | reviewer finding | PROPOSE FIX | README: name the docstring hunk (it also adds `reprobe_sources` to the contract line) and the extra assertion in `test_p7_tick.py`. |
| A-9 | Nit | reviewer finding | DECLINE | Test style (direct assignment instead of monkeypatch) is safe for plain classes; reviewers found the fixtures valid and the runner confirms the tests run. |

## Patch G5-wording (comments and docstrings only): APPLY AFTER THE G-1 FIX AND ONE REBASE (test-file hunk; exit 1 on this checkout)
| id | sev | merged from | decision | what to change |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| G-1 | Minor | reviewer finding | PROPOSE FIX | The new header in `approvals/gate.py` (patched copy `/tmp/core-b4p` lines 7-8, b4cce5b base; read) says "nothing in this repository re-scans text before it leaves". The approval channel does run one final redaction (src `approvals/channel.py:29`; the Slack channel also redacts once more). Say: "there is no BLOCK verdict and no independent second detector; the channel runs one final redaction pass." Use the same words in the `guard/redact.py` docstring. Wording check against `redact_text`: `approvals/channel.py:29` calls `runmap.redact_text(text, mapping, neutralise=_neutralise)` on the channel text, `channel.py:36` calls it again on a joined view, and `slack_channel.py:81` calls it for Slack. "The same patterns" is accurate only if the mapping and patterns are the same set the earlier redaction used: not compared. The wording above therefore says "one final redaction pass" and avoids "same patterns" until compared. Rating Minor. Reason: comment-only, no behaviour change, and the error is on the safe side: the sentence tells a reader there is LESS protection than there is. The earlier word 'over-claims' was imprecise and is replaced by 'may understate', which says only that a final pass exists; whether that pass uses the same patterns as the earlier redaction was NOT compared. G2 CHECK (read in this checkout and in `/tmp/smfull`): the header's second claim, that rule G2 (unverifiable claims) is not built, is true: `approvals/gate.py:354` defines the regular expression `_UNVERIFIABLE` but nothing calls it (grep over `src`: one hit, the definition) and no `hedge` function exists (grep: none); the sentence G-1 questions is only 'nothing in this repository re-scans text before it leaves'. Scope the wording to the approval channel (the text people read). Cites checked: `approvals/channel.py:29` and `:36` and `approvals/slack_channel.py:81` call `redact_text`; the patched header text is at `/tmp/core-b4p` `approvals/gate.py:7-8` (b4cce5b base). |

In the ORIGINAL text (this checkout, `/tmp/smfull`, and b4cce5b for these files) the G5 mentions in `approvals/gate.py` are `:42` (the G5 bullet), `:59` (chat sinks: `enforce`, with G5), `:63` (Verdicts ... In the PATCHED tree `/tmp/core-b4p` the same lines are `:45`, `:62`, `:66` and `:206`, and its lines `:7-10` are the patch's own new header, not original design (line 10 ends 'design only.'; the earlier cites ':7-9' and ':9' fit only part of it). In `guard/redact.py` the comment 'the gate re-scans with a fresh one' EXISTS (original `:947`, patched `:948`; read): it is a comment about a past bug in the original gate and it still says the gate re-scans, so reword it as history ('the original WARDEN gate re-scanned ...'). ## Missed risks in today's code (found by a reviewer after the first reading; each is a PROPOSE FIX)
| id | sev | decision | what to change |
|---|---|---|---|
| X-1 | Minor | PROPOSE FIX | No product bound. The ceiling limits tokens and the price check limits the price separately, but nothing bounds their product (max_tokens times output price per call). A 200_000-token ceiling at a high output price can still exceed any sensible per-call spend. Add a per-call cost bound in config, checked at load: max_tokens x output price must not exceed the per-run budget. Not run. This row rests on the ceiling (C-01 extra) that the lead's default 2026-11-16 item 4 CUT, so it is MOOT while the ceiling is cut, like C-2; kept for the day the ceiling returns. |
| X-2 | Major | PROPOSE FIX | In plain words: the new step writes to the audit trail and deletes waiting rows, and the patch does not say under which database login it runs. DECISION FIRST: either the step runs under the runtime login and the patch says so (the over-breadth of X-5 is then accepted in writing by the studio head), or it runs under a separate drain login (X-5.1); everything below follows from that choice. RATING BASIS: rating rule part (b), a broken least-privilege rule in the text of the patch (no identity, no per-action reason, no negative test; the rule demands these for each write function, so the missing test is part (b), not a plain missing test); counted only if A-01 returns. Least privilege of the new `audit_reconcile` step (the least-privilege rule). It is a new write function: a new caller of the existing audit `reconcile()`, which appends to the audit chain and deletes outbox rows. It adds no new write path (every right it uses is already granted, see 'Every table the tick can write, by step' in this row). The patch does not say which identity runs it, nor ship the per-action reason and a negative test (reviewer words, confirmed by reading A-01.patch and its README: neither names an identity for the step, ships a per-action reason or a negative test for it). What the code needs and what is already granted (src, opened by me; the single grant map is `store/migrate.py:46-68`): (1) the audit chain append `ChainAudit._append` (`audit/chain.py:180-206`) does SELECT and INSERT on `audit_entries`; granted to the runtime role as `_APPEND` = SELECT, INSERT (`migrate.py:57`; triggers refuse UPDATE, DELETE and TRUNCATE to the runtime, portal and purge roles, `migrate.py:42-45` comment; the table owner or a superuser can disable a trigger, so it is not 'any role'); (2) the drain, audit `reconcile()` (`chain.py:232-246`), does SELECT on `audit_outbox` (`:239`) and DELETE on it (`:244`); both ALREADY granted: `audit_outbox` is `SELECT, INSERT, DELETE` for the runtime role (`migrate.py:59`), so the grant map needs NO new line for the drain (but that same grant is the over-breadth recorded as X-5: "add nothing" holds only while the agent and the tick share one runtime role, and is not an endorsement of the grant); (3) INSERT on `audit_outbox` is needed by `write_outcome`'s last resort (`chain.py:223-227`), which the tick's stuck step already calls (`runtime/tick.py:133` and `:162`) and which the audit step's own `_drain` does not call; it is why INSERT stays; (4) the inline checkpoint inside `_append` (`chain.py:203-204`, `checkpoint()` at `:295-320`) INSERTs into `audit_checkpoints` (`migrate.py:58`, `_APPEND`), and the existing `checkpoint` step (`tick.py:177`) does the same; (5) the tick as a whole also needs UPDATE where its other steps write: `tick_state` (`migrate.py:56`, `_RW`), `runs` (`migrate.py:47`, `_RW`) and `approval_requests` (`migrate.py:50`, `_RW`). TRACE (src, opened by me; only what I traced): the approvals step `reconcile` (`tick.py:85-97`) calls `runtime.on_decided` (`agent_loop.py:799-804`); REJECTED and EXPIRED go to `_to` only; APPROVED goes to `execute` (`:806-`). In `execute` the store and limits calls I found are: reads (`get_run`, `get_plan`, `get_gate_decision`, `get_redaction_map`, `audit.events`), `_recheck` which calls `limits.blocked` (reads only: `get_kill_switch`, `spend_totals`, `limits.py:20-28`), `audit.write_intent` and `audit.write_outcome` (audit append, outbox as last resort), `advance` for state changes (`runs` UPDATE through `Postgres.transition`, `postgres.py:262`), the heartbeat `store.touch` (`runs` UPDATE, `postgres.py:280`), and the client hooks `execute`, `verify` and `rollback`, which are outside this repository and whose own writes I did not read. I did NOT find `limits.charge` or `add_spend` in this path: the lines `agent_loop.py:485-486` and `limits.py:17-18` are the model-call path in `handle` (starts `:352`), not `on_decided`, so the reviewer's chain `on_decided -> limits.charge -> add_spend` is NOT confirmed, and `spend`, `spend_month` and `plans` are NOT shown to be written by the tick (they stay `_RW`, `migrate.py:53-54` and `:48`, for the agent path). Every table the tick can write, by step (src; each store method opened by me in `store/postgres.py`): `sweep` and `resend_unsent` (`runtime/tick.py:79-82`; `approvals/requests.py:126` via `_send` and `:210` in `sweep`): `approval_requests` UPDATE, plus audit; NO `approval_decisions` INSERT from the tick: the only `INSERT INTO approval_decisions` is `store/postgres.py:345`, reached through `record_decision` at `requests.py:170` inside `ApprovalService.decide()` (`:146-180`), which is the receiver/click identity, not the tick (checked src; that receiver path also does an UPDATE on `approval_requests`, `store/postgres.py:350` inside `record_decision` (defined at `:337`; `:335` is the last line of the method before it, `update_approval_request`; the INSERT is at `:345`), plus an audit append through `_audit`, `approvals/requests.py:173`); approvals step `reconcile`: `approval_requests` is read, `runs` UPDATE and audit append as traced above (the channel update only talks to the channel); `stuck`: `runs` UPDATE through `advance` and audit append; `checkpoint`: `audit_checkpoints` INSERT; the new `audit_reconcile` step: `audit_entries` INSERT, `audit_outbox` SELECT, INSERT and DELETE; `purge_evidence`: DELETE on `tool_calls` and `redaction_maps` through `purge_store` (the purge role; `app.purge_store or app.store` means that if no purge store is configured the runtime role is used, and that role has no DELETE there, so the purge fails; not run); `reprobe_sources`: no store write; the loop itself: `tick_state` upsert (`tick.py:319`, `postgres.py:470-471`). So the "do NOT deny UPDATE" list for the tick identity is: `runs`, `approval_requests`, `tick_state` (UPDATE via upsert) (the INSERT on `approval_decisions` belongs to the receiver identity, not the tick). CODE FACT: only the receiver path (`ApprovalService.decide()`, `approvals/requests.py:146-180`, through `record_decision`, `store/postgres.py:337-345`) writes decisions in this repository. DATABASE FACT: the grant map gives that INSERT to the one runtime role (`migrate.py:51`), so any process that holds the runtime connection string can write it; that fact and its fix are X-6.3; this list is complete only for the repository's own code, NOT for the client's execute, verify and rollback hooks, which run under the same identity today and may write anywhere that identity can. Widening, said plainly: the tick also runs the client's `execute`, `verify` and `rollback` hooks (approvals step `reconcile` -> `runtime.on_decided` -> `execute`, `tick.py:95` and `agent_loop.py:806-915`: `hooks.execute` at `:886`, `hooks.verify` at `:901`, `hooks.rollback` at `:909`), so every extra write right on the tick's identity (an audit append path with outbox DELETE) is a right that client code runs next to; this is one more reason for X-5. Kill switch: read only. So "no UPDATE anywhere" is true ONLY for the new audit step's own call (the drain: audit `reconcile()` and `_drain`); the tick process as a whole does hold UPDATE on those three tables. Today the tick uses `app.store` and `app.audit` on the runtime role, and `app.purge_store or app.store` (`tick.py:196`) for deleting evidence: that is the precedent for a separate deleting identity (the purge role, `purge="DELETE"` grants in `migrate.py:62-65`; roles are `runtime_role`, `portal_role`, `purge_role`, `migrate.py:286-288`). The portal role is read-only; the purge role deletes only `tool_calls` and `redaction_maps`. The patch and the design do not state a separate tick identity (not stated in any file I read), so the decision is: either the tick keeps the runtime role (the existing grants above already cover the audit step; nothing is added) and the patch must say so and X-5 is recorded as accepted over-breadth with the studio head's yes, or a new tick or drain role (see X-5) is created and its grants added to the grant map in `store/migrate.py`. Do not add rights beyond what the drain needs. The dead-man SQL in DEAD-MAN-CHECK.md only reads `tick_state`, so it needs a separate read-only role (SELECT on `tick_state` only), not the tick's write role (not read: DEAD-MAN-CHECK.md). Ship: the policy text, the per-action reason above, a negative test that matches the corrected list, and a masked list of the identity's rights. Negative test (matches the list above): with the identity that runs the audit step, UPDATE on `audit_entries` and on `audit_checkpoints` is refused, DELETE on `audit_entries`, `audit_checkpoints` and every table except `audit_outbox` is refused (the runtime role has no DELETE on any other table; evidence deletion belongs to the purge role), and INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE on `tick_state` are NOT part of the audit step's reason (they belong to the tick's step bookkeeping, `tick.py:319-324`); the read-only dead-man role is refused INSERT. Wording of these negative tests: the claim 'UPDATE on `audit_entries` is refused' only tests the grant if the assertion names the exact class `InsufficientPrivilege` (the privilege check runs before the append-only trigger, which raises a different error; this is how `tests/test_k2_migrations.py:230-246` asserts it); listed in X-5.6 (a). Do NOT write a negative test that denies UPDATE on `tick_state`, `runs` or `approval_requests` to the tick's identity: the other tick steps need it. The checker `store/migrate.py:193` `effective_problems` already reports rights above the grant map for the named logins; use it, and extend it as listed in the paragraph 'Over-breadth checks still missing' (in 'Departures from the least-privilege rule in the end-state', after the login table). The migration comment at `store/migrate.py:43` says the drain (audit `reconcile()`) deletes only outbox rows: the test must pin that. |
| X-3 | Minor | PROPOSE FIX | Concurrent reconcile is untested. Two ticks (or a tick and an agent) can drain the same outbox row at once. Two outcomes in src (opened by me): (a) `_append` runs SELECT-then-INSERT inside one transaction (`chain.py:184-186`); if the second drainer's SELECT sees the first one's row it raises `DuplicateEvent`, `_drain` (`chain.py:248-255`) treats it as done, returns True, and BOTH drainers then DELETE and add 1 to `done`, so the count (`drained=`) is double counted; (b) NOT possible as first written (src, opened by me): every append takes the Postgres advisory lock BEFORE its SELECT (`chain.py:120`, key `_PG_LOCK` at `:47`), the in-process lock first (`chain.py:117`), and SQLite uses `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` (`chain.py:123`), so two appends can not both SELECT before either INSERTs; a second drainer gets `DuplicateEvent`, which is case (a). Only (a), the double count, is real. Add a two-thread or two-connection test that asserts each event appears once in the chain and the count adds up (case (a) only; no Postgres-only test for (b)). Practical form for the local directory: the unguarded `path.unlink()` at `audit/local_dir.py:154` (src: if another drainer already removed the file, `unlink()` raises `FileNotFoundError`, which is an `OSError`, not caught by the `except DuplicateEvent` or `except Exception` blocks above it because it sits after them, so it escapes the drain (audit `reconcile()`) and ends the whole loop: the files after it are not drained in that call; `unlink(missing_ok=True)` would be the one-line fix, not run); for the chain: the unguarded DELETE at `audit/chain.py:244` (see A-6). |
| X-4 | Major | PROPOSE FIX | In plain words: nothing ever empties the outbox, and a record written while the database is down exists only in memory and is lost when the program exits. RATING BASIS (rating rule part (a)): SHOWN by reading source: no caller of the audit `reconcile()` exists in this repository (grep by me), and in the chain kind a record written while the database is down is kept only in `_pending` (`audit/chain.py:229`) and is lost at exit. NOT shown: how often a record reaches the outbox or the database is down (not measured), and a program outside this repository that calls `reconcile()` could not be checked. So the rating rests on the missing caller and the read loss path, not on measured loss. PRESENT today (src, opened by me): no caller of the audit `reconcile()` exists in this repository (only tests call `.reconcile`; a client program outside this repository could not be checked). The only definitions are `audit/chain.py:232`, `audit/local_dir.py:126` and the Protocol `audit/protocol.py:27`; the tick step named `reconcile` (`runtime/tick.py:85`) is the approvals step. `K1bAudit` (`k1b/audit_sink.py:26-49`) has no `reconcile()` at all although its docstring (`:38`) says the sink keeps a failed event "for `reconcile`". This is the defect that A-01 (deferred) would fix; while A-01 stays deferred it is a Major present in today's code that had no row of its own until now. What is lost, read from code (not run): in the chain kind, `write_outcome` (`audit/chain.py:215-230`) writes to `audit_outbox` through the SAME database handle (`self._db`) as the chain, so if the database is down the outbox INSERT fails too and the event goes only to the in-memory list `_pending` (`:229`, comment `:172`): it is never in the outbox table, is lost when the process exits, and no other process can drain it (A-1 says the same). Rows reach the outbox table only when the chain append fails while the separate outbox INSERT (`chain.py:223-227`) still succeeds, for example a failed `_append` transaction (`chain.py:180-206`) with the database otherwise reachable. How often that happens was not measured (not run). The inline `checkpoint()` (`chain.py:203-204`) can NOT be that cause: it "Never raises" (it catches every `Exception` and returns None, `chain.py:295-317`) and it runs after the chain INSERT has committed. Those rows, and in the local-directory kind the files in the outbox folder, wait until someone calls `reconcile()` by hand, with no log line and no red tick step. Note (src `audit/chain.py:240-241`, `:233-237`): an exit-time `reconcile()` call recovers ONLY rows already in the outbox table (and, in the same process, its own `_pending`); events that sit only in `_pending` because the database was down (`chain.py:229`) are lost at exit anyway, because `reconcile()` first tries to drain them through `_append`, which needs the same down database, and returns early at `chain.py:240-241` when the outbox SELECT fails. So this fix narrows the loss, it does not close the DB-down case; that case needs a durable fallback that does not use the database (for example the local-directory kind as a second sink), which is a design choice for the studio head; only an outline exists (Decision 2, unreviewed, unbuilt). Fix: either un-defer A-01 (the tick calls `reconcile()`), or give every caller of the short-lived commands a `reconcile()` call at exit. Decider: the studio head (Decision 2; the deferral is the studio head's, decision 2026-11-16 item 4). A-01 would narrow this problem, not close it: the database-down case needs a durable fallback that does not use the database. WORKED EXAMPLE (src, opened by me; not run): the emergency stop. `Limits.stop` (`limits.py:43-46`) switches first and audits second: `store.set_kill_switch(...)` then `audit.write_outcome(...)`; the CLI (`cli.py:383-393`) then prints 'kill switch ON' and returns 0 (`cli.py:396-397`; `cli.py:383-388` is the docstring), and the process exits. The switch itself persists (the stored row holds `on`, `by` and `ts`, `models.py:256-259`), so the stop WORKS; what can be lost is its audit record. If the audit append fails after the switch write succeeded (for example the database drops between the two calls, or the append waits on the audit lock, see X-5.4) and the outbox INSERT fails as well, the event exists only in `_pending` (`chain.py:229`) and is gone when the CLI exits; if only the append failed, the event waits in the outbox for a hand call of `reconcile()`. Why 'a stop during a database outage is recorded nowhere durable' is too strong: with the Postgres store and the chain audit on the same database, a full outage makes `set_kill_switch` raise FIRST, the CLI reports the store unreachable and says the switch was NOT changed (`cli.py:383-388` is the docstring; the unreachable-store message and exit code 4 are `cli.py:370-374`), and no event exists to lose; the loss needs the audit write to fail while the store write succeeded. The reset is safer: it audits first and leaves the switch on if that fails (`limits.py:48-51`). |
| X-5 | Major | PROPOSE FIX | In plain words: the one database login the agent uses can delete audit records that wait in the outbox, and can also forge, plant and block audit records. PRESENT today, independent of A-01. RATING BASIS: rating rule parts (a) and (b), shown by the cited grants (`store/migrate.py:57-59`); Postgres behaviour UNVERIFIED (nothing was run). SHOWN: the grants in `migrate.py:46-68` and the code paths below. NOT shown: that a hijacked agent was ever tried against a real database. Who can use the login: see item 8 of the plain summary. The finding is split into six parts, X-5.1 to X-5.6, in the table directly below, each rated by the rating rule: X-5.1 Major (a: pending audit events can be erased), X-5.2 Major (a: forged rows get around tamper evidence; sub-point (i) alone is Minor), X-5.3 Major (a: signing silently stops), X-5.4 Minor (availability only, nothing lost), X-5.5 Minor (fail-closed), X-5.6 not rated (a work list for X-5.1). The counted row is Major because its worst parts are Major (what is wrong, who can do it, what accepting costs, what the fix costs, who decides); the old labels (a) to (j) of the long text are mapped to the parts in History, section 'X-5 full working text'. The studio head decides (Decisions 3 to 6). |
| X-6 | Major | PROPOSE FIX | In plain words: one database login (the runtime login) serves the agent, the scheduled tick and the approval receiver, so the agent's login can switch off its own kill switch, set its own spend counters to zero, add approval decisions, and set an approval request to APPROVED directly, after which the tick runs the plan. PRESENT in today's source, independent of A-01; read from source, not run; every Postgres statement is UNVERIFIED. The studio head decides. The finding is split into nine parts, X-6.1 to X-6.9, in the table directly below, each rated by the rating rule: X-6.1, X-6.2, X-6.3 and X-6.4 Major (a: a limit, an approval or the emergency stop can be got around), X-6.6 and X-6.8 Major (b: the least-privilege and masking rules), X-6.5 Minor (a gap in a proposal, not in today's source), X-6.7 Minor (rollout care), X-6.9 Minor (approved-plan integrity: a swapped plan is stopped at `execute`, source read, not run). The counted row is Major because its worst parts are Major. VERDICT: the database guard on approval requests proposed earlier must NOT be built as written; the gaps found in it are in part X-6.5. RATING BASIS: Major because parts X-6.1 to X-6.4 are defects shown in today's source (the cited grants) and because the shared login breaks the least-privilege rule (agents never hold approver or executor credentials); the gaps in the proposed guard are gaps in a proposal, not new defects in today's source or in the three patches. SHOWN: the grants and the code paths. NOT shown: how far a self-approved plan gets, which depends on the client's hook. (Whether a deployment really uses one login is settled by source: see item 8 of the plain summary.) The full working text (every cite, test and Postgres statement) is kept unchanged in History, section 'X-6 full working text'; the mapping to its paragraphs is under the parts. |

### X-5 in parts
These parts are not counted by the script (their ids do not match its row pattern); the counted row is X-5 above. Every Postgres statement here is UNVERIFIED (nothing was run). "Size" is a relative judgement (small, medium, large), not a measured effort; no hours are estimated.

| part | what is wrong (plain words, source cite) | who can do it | what accepting costs | what the fix costs (size) | who decides |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| X-5.1 Delete waiting audit records (Major) | The runtime login holds DELETE on the outbox (`store/migrate.py:59`: `SELECT, INSERT, DELETE`). Outbox rows are audit events not yet in the chain; the chain table itself is protected by triggers that refuse UPDATE, DELETE and TRUNCATE to the runtime, portal and purge logins (`migrate.py:42-45`), the outbox is not. Only the drain needs DELETE (`audit/chain.py:244`, and the file removal in the local-directory kind); if Decision 2 option (b) is chosen, the `k1b` login deletes outbox rows too (its commands call `reconcile()` at exit), so two logins then need DELETE. | Any code under the runtime login: agent loop, tick, the client's hooks, a stolen connection string. | Pending audit events can be erased with no trace. | A separate drain login: `SELECT, DELETE` on `audit_outbox`; `INSERT` plus column `SELECT (seq, hash, event_id)` on `audit_entries`; column `SELECT (alg, key_label)` on `audit_checkpoints`; built with `signer=None` and `anchor=None` (so no signer or anchor secret). PREREQUISITE (re-grepped: `config.py:419-420` refuses every pair in `NOT_PROD`, `backends/table.py:604` holds `("signer", "none")` and `("anchor", "none")`, and `stages.py:18-23` makes `is_prod` true for `prod` and for any value that is not a stage name; only `demo` and `pilot` are spared, so a pilot is spared): in production a drain built this way is REFUSED today. It needs the new setting 'this process does not sign' plus a start-up rule that only the signing process needs a signer: the same setting as X-5.3 option (i), designed and sized once there (Decision 5). The agent keeps `SELECT, INSERT` on the outbox. IMPORTANT LIMIT: while the drain is done by the tick drain step next to the client's hooks, client code can reach any handle the tick holds, so a second handle there protects nothing; the drain must be done by a separate process, the drain program. The purge store (`app.py:151-160`, `tick.py:196`) is the precedent for a second login, and it has the same weakness (same process as the hooks). Size: medium (grant map, new role, start-up check for a pilot or production environment, tests; see X-5.6), plus the 'does not sign' setting of Decision 29. | Studio head, Decisions 3 and 5. |
| X-5.2 Forged and planted audit rows (Major) | The runtime login keeps INSERT on `audit_entries` (`migrate.py:57`) and on the outbox (`:59`). Rewriting the chain is blocked by triggers, appending is not. (i) (Minor alone) An outbox row with an invalid body makes `AuditEvent.model_validate_json` raise unguarded (`chain.py:243`) so every later row (ordered by `event_id`, `chain.py:239`) waits. (ii) An outbox row with a chosen `event_id` makes the real event's later outbox insert do nothing (`ON CONFLICT (event_id) DO NOTHING`, `chain.py:225`), so the planted body is drained under the real id; this is narrow: the real chain append must fail first. (iii) Forged rows can be appended directly. | Any code under the runtime login. | A hijacked agent or a buggy hook can add false audit rows, delay the drain on purpose, and in the narrow case replace a real event's content. Removing outbox DELETE (X-5.1) does not stop any of this; the policy text must say so. | Option 1: accept in writing (the runtime login, and after a split every login that writes audit events, keeps INSERT on the audit tables). Option 2: one audit-writer function in the database, owned by a login nobody connects with, that computes the chain hash itself (X-6.4; Postgres only; byte-exact hash agreement with Python needed; audit path, uncapped review). The per-row guard of A-6 (catch, quarantine, log the id, continue) limits case (i) and is cheap. Size: option 1 none, option 2 large. | Studio head, Decision 4. |
| X-5.3 Planted checkpoint row stops signing (Major) | The runtime login holds `SELECT, INSERT` on `audit_checkpoints` (`migrate.py:58`); the table's only key is `seq BIGINT PRIMARY KEY`; `hash` and `signature` are `TEXT NOT NULL` (`migrations/0002_audit_chain.sql:15-17`), but nothing links a row to `audit_entries` or checks its signature. `checkpoint()` decides from `signed_through()`, which is `SELECT MAX(seq)` (`chain.py:290-293`); `if self.signed_through() >= seq: return seq` (`chain.py:307`) returns without signing, and the signing insert is `ON CONFLICT (seq) DO NOTHING` (`chain.py:310-314`). One junk row at or above the head silences signing and anchoring; the tick step `checkpoint` stays green; the row cannot be removed (append-only trigger); `verify()` reports it (through `_check_checkpoints`, `chain.py:413-436`) but nothing in this repository runs `verify()` on a schedule (searched only as far as `cli.py`). | Any code under the runtime login. | No new head is signed or anchored, with no error, until the chain grows past the planted seq (never, for a very large seq). | (i) Only a login that holds the signer may INSERT checkpoints: agent and tick use `signer=None` and the `checkpoint` step runs in a separate signing process with its own login and secret (the only option that stops the junk row at its source; the signer must not sit in the tick next to client hooks). FOUND IN ROUND 19 (src read, not run): in a production environment `signer: none` is refused for EVERY process, because `config.py:419-420` refuses each (capability, kind) pair listed in `NOT_PROD` (`backends/table.py:604`, `NOT_PROD` holds `("signer", "none")` and `("anchor", "none")`). So agent and tick with `signer=None` cannot start in production under today's rule, and option (i) needs a new setting (for example a process role that says 'this process does not sign') plus a start-up rule that production needs a signer in exactly the signing process. Not designed; it stays inside the 'large' of (i). (ii) Count only valid rows in `signed_through()`/`checkpoint()`: new code, needs a signer to hold the public key, closes only the above-head case. (iii) A foreign key `(seq, hash)` to `audit_entries` plus a unique index: a weak second guard (the attacker can append the entry it needs, `chain.py:187` gives it the true hash), and it breaks one test, `test_audit_checkpoints_migration_adds_alg_and_label_and_old_rows_get_defaults` in `tests/test_p2_postgres.py`, which makes three inserts into `audit_checkpoints` with no matching entry (`:289`, `:296`, `:299`). (iv) A `(seq, signature)` key lets junk rows coexist and needs `_check_anchors` (`chain.py:440`) rewritten. The head-seq case stays OPEN until option (i) or option (iv) is chosen. A test that works now: plant a junk row at head plus 1000, call `checkpoint()`, assert a valid row exists at the head seq and `signed_through()` equals the head seq (these two assertions fail on the current code; the return-value assertion passes on it). Size: (i) large, (ii) medium, (iii) small, (iv) medium. | Studio head, Decision 5. |
| X-5.4 Audit lock held (Minor) | Every append takes `pg_advisory_xact_lock(_PG_LOCK)` before its SELECT (`chain.py:120`); the key is a constant derived from the bytes `hadvnaud` (`chain.py:47`), public in the source. As far as I know any login may call that function (default privilege of PUBLIC; not checked). A hijacked session can hold the lock in an open transaction, and every audit write of every writer waits; with no timeout set the wait is unbounded, and `write_outcome` falls back to the outbox only when `_append` raises (`chain.py:219-229`). | Any code under the runtime login. | A hang of every audit write (availability loss, not data loss; fail-closed `write_intent` raising `AuditUnavailable` is the safe side). | `lock_timeout`, `statement_timeout` (writers' connection strings or `ALTER ROLE ... SET`) and `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` on the agent login protect against ACCIDENTS only: a hostile session can `SET` them back to 0 (from my knowledge of Postgres, not run). They must be set only once a drain runs (X-4), otherwise a short timeout turns a hang into an outbox that grows without a signal. The control against a hostile session needs the audit writer as its own login (X-6.4) and `REVOKE EXECUTE` on the advisory-lock functions from PUBLIC, then a grant back by name to the logins that append audit entries (the EXECUTE revoke probably needs a superuser, because those functions belong to the built-in catalog; UNVERIFIED; it belongs to Decision 9b; the agent and tick login stays on the allowlist, so this control does not stop hostile agent code until the audit writer is its own login, X-6.4; Decision 6 covers the timeouts only), or a row lock on a one-row table the agent cannot lock; neither was checked against a real Postgres database. Size: small (timeouts), large (the real control). | Studio head, Decision 6 (timeouts) and Decision 9 (the revoke). |
| X-5.5 One unreadable audit row blocks the reads of a chosen run (Minor) | The runtime login can INSERT into `audit_entries` (`migrate.py:57`). `ChainAudit.events()` (`chain.py:279-286`) parses every stored body with `AuditEvent.model_validate_json` and no guard, so a row for run R with a body that is not a valid event makes `events(R)` raise. `verify()` flags a non-JSON body through the event-id check (`chain.py:397-400`, `:406-410`), but a body that is valid JSON, not an `AuditEvent` and carries the matching `event_id` is flagged by neither check. The only caller in `src` is `agent_loop.py:847` inside a `try` whose `except Exception` (`:853-854`) hands the run to a person with the reason `audit_unavailable`. | Any code under the runtime login. | A chosen run is stopped (fail-closed) with a misleading reason; what a person then sees was not run. | Our own code, no studio head decision: in `events()` and `reconcile()` (`chain.py:243`) parse each row in its own `try`, skip a row that fails to parse, count it and log one ERROR line naming the `seq` (never the body); `verify()` keeps reporting it; a row that parses is never skipped. Tests: an unparseable body and a valid-JSON non-event body for run R, then a valid `gate.decide` event; `events(R)` must return the valid event, not raise, and the log must hold the seq and not the body; plus an `execute`-level test that the recheck finds the bound hash. Size: small. | Nobody (our fix); listed for completeness. |
| X-5.6 What the drain login breaks and needs (work list, not rated) | (a) `tests/warden_port/test_audit_chain.py:643-679` builds the chain as the RUNTIME login and asserts `reconcile() == 1` and `signed_through() == 2`; both fail once the runtime login loses outbox DELETE (and, with X-5.3 option (i), checkpoint INSERT), so split it into a runtime-login test and a drain-login test. (b) `migrate()` accepts only three logins (`migrate.py:283-292`, name check `:298-303`, not-members check `:333-339`, per-login block `:340-355`); a fourth optional parameter (default `None`) must join `named` and get its own `_apply_grants` and `effective_problems` block; `Grant` (`migrate.py:23-37`) needs a `drain` field defaulting to nothing; the callers listed by grep all pass fewer arguments, so an optional default breaks none. (c) On an existing deployment `_apply_grants` runs `REVOKE ALL` per table (`migrate.py:179`) then re-grants, so the next migrate takes DELETE away from the runtime login: the drain login and its secret must exist first, and the tick must show a missing drain handle as a red step. (d) A start-up check for a pilot or production environment, like `app.py:86-89`, must refuse a start with no drain secret name. (e) A second `ChainAudit` object has its own `_pending` list (`chain.py:173`), so the tick's own `write_outcome` must go through the object the drain drains. (f) The local-directory kind (`audit/local_dir.py`) has no database login; the same split there is a directory-permission question, not designed. (g) KEEP `SELECT` on the outbox for the agent until `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` is proved on a real database not to need it. | The builder and the deployer. | If the work is skipped: the split in X-5.1 cannot ship safely. | See the left cell. Size: medium. | Our code; timing is Decision 15. |

### X-6 in parts (what is wrong, who can do it, what it costs)
These parts are not counted by the script (their ids do not match its row pattern); the counted row is X-6 above. Every Postgres statement here is UNVERIFIED (nothing was run). X-6.8 sits after X-6.7 and X-6.9 after X-6.8 in this table. "Size" is a relative judgement, not a measured effort; no hours are estimated. Each part also says what accepting today's state costs.

| part | what is wrong (plain words, source cite) | who can do it | what accepting costs | what the fix costs (size) | who decides |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| X-6.1 Kill switch (Major) | The runtime login may INSERT and UPDATE the kill switch row (`store/migrate.py:55`), so it can switch the emergency stop off, with no audit record. The only code that writes it is the operator command (`cli.py:383-408`, `limits.py:43-52`); the agent loop only reads it (`agent_loop.py:252`, `:817`). | Any code under the runtime login. | The emergency stop can be switched off by the thing it is meant to stop, silently. | A new `operator` login with write rights on the kill switch only (plus the narrow audit rights in the login table); the runtime login keeps read only. Needs its own secret setting, changes to `migrate()` and `cli.py`, a start-up profile for the `operator` process, and two releases (add, then narrow). WHO EMPTIES THE EVENTS THE `operator` LOGIN PARKS: it may INSERT into the outbox but not DELETE; the drain empties them under Decision 2 option (a) or (b); under (c) nobody does. The extra call at the end of a command in option (b) is for the K1b commands only, not for `operator`. Size: small to medium (one of the cheapest parts). | Studio head, Decision 7. |
| X-6.2 Spend counters (Major) | The runtime login may UPDATE the day and month spend rows and the run cost (`migrate.py:47`, `:53-54`), while the code only ever adds (`store/postgres.py:416-431`). It can set a counter to zero, change a key column (day, month, agent) so the cap reads an empty row, or lower the run cost. | Any code under the runtime login. | Every spend cap can be reset by the code it limits. | Option A: a database trigger that refuses a lower amount, freezes the key columns, refuses a negative amount, and never lowers the run cost; Postgres only (size: small, cheapest). Option B: append-only spend rows; rewrites `add_spend`, `spend_totals` and the memory store; money path, uncapped review (size: large). Neither proves real spend: an agent that never charges, or charges too little, is not caught; the reserve-before-call budget in code stays the guard. | Studio head, Decision 14. |
| X-6.3 Self-approval (Major) | The runtime login may INSERT approval decisions (`migrate.py:51`) and UPDATE approval requests (`migrate.py:50`): it can set a request to APPROVED, point it at another run, or reset an APPROVED request to PENDING and approve again. The tick's `reconcile` step runs every APPROVED request (`runtime/tick.py:85-95`), and `execute` does not demand a decision row (`agent_loop.py:806-866`). | Any code under the runtime login. | A request can approve itself and the tick then runs the plan; how far it gets depends on the client's hook (not run). | Remove INSERT on decisions from the agent login (receiver login only); a database guard on approval requests with an allowlist of changeable columns, status changes only from PENDING and only with a matching decision row, and a plain INSERT instead of today's upsert (`save_approval_request` calls `_insert(..., upsert_on="approval_id")` at `postgres.py:311`, read; the earlier wording read as if :311 were already a plain INSERT, which a citation check found wrong); one Python function for the allowed status changes that every store kind calls; known test breakage in `tests/contracts/test_store.py` and `tests/test_p2_postgres.py:289`, `:296` and `:299` (a `migrate` call and two inserts into `audit_checkpoints`; the earlier cite :290 held nothing, found by a citation check and re-read by me). The first release (new logins added, old rights kept) gives NO protection against self-approval. The guard must be built as in X-6.5, not as first written. Size: medium to large. | Studio head, Decisions 10 and 11. |
| X-6.4 Approval binding and audit rows (Major) | The runtime login keeps INSERT on the audit tables (`migrate.py:57`, `:59`; today it is the only login that does: the portal login has SELECT only and the purge login nothing on them, `migrate.py:57-59`) and can write the redaction map (`:65`). `execute` takes the approved digest of the restored values from the LAST `gate.decide` audit record (`agent_loop.py:845-867`), so a hijacked login can rewrite the map, append a forged record with a matching digest, and the changed values run. A status guard on approval requests does NOT close this. The agent also writes the approval summary and the approver list. | Any code under the runtime login: today it is the only login that can append audit records and write the map (after a split, every login allowed to append audit records). | Changed values can run under an approval given for other values. | Store the approved digest where the agent cannot write it (for example in the approval request at INSERT, frozen, compared at `execute`); optionally one audit-writer function in the database (Postgres only; see X-5.2). For the agent-written summary and approver list: accept in writing, or have the receiver recompute them from the stored plan (needs SELECT on `plans` and the detector `summary()` uses; not checked). Not designed further. DIGEST THREAT MODEL (read from source, not run): in a pilot or production environment the digest is an HMAC-SHA-256 under the deployment key named by `evidence.restored_hash_key_secret_name`, which `app.build` requires when live (`app.py:84-85`; `agent_loop.py:267-278`). An attacker who holds only the database connection string cannot compute a matching digest, so forging a `gate.decide` record needs that key as well; hostile code inside the agent or tick process can get it (`hooks.restored_key` lives in that process), so this part is rated against T1 (hostile code in that process), not against a stolen connection string alone. Outside a pilot or production environment no key is set and the digest is a plain SHA-256 that anyone who can read the values can compute. A frozen digest in the approval request still needs the process that computes it to hold the key (the agent process today), so freezing helps only if the request is written by a process that does not run client hooks (the receiver) and holds the key. Size: large. | Studio head, Decisions 4, 12 and 13. |
| X-6.5 The proposed database guard is unsafe as first written (Minor) | A temporary table named like `approval_decisions` is read BEFORE the real table (Postgres searches the session's temporary schema first; every login may create temporary tables unless that right is revoked), so the agent could forge the record the guard reads; a further claim of the first reviewer, that temporary functions and operators are found the same way, is NOT claimed as a fact here: I have not read the Postgres rules for function and operator lookup, a later reviewer thought it probably wrong, and it stays UNVERIFIED until a run on Postgres shows it (nothing in this file relies on it); triggers can be skipped through `session_replication_role` or by a member of the table owner's role; and the guard reads rows the same login can write. Reviewer knowledge, UNVERIFIED. | The runtime login, and any login holding the temporary-table right. | A guard built as first written can be bypassed: do not build it that way. | Trigger functions with schema-qualified names (`TG_TABLE_SCHEMA`) and a pinned `search_path` ending in `pg_temp`; `REVOKE TEMPORARY ON DATABASE ... FROM PUBLIC` (a deployment action that needs the database owner or a superuser and may refuse temporary tables to any other tool on that database; not checked); `ENABLE ALWAYS` on the new and the four existing audit triggers (a new migration, `0002` is released); extra checks in `effective_problems` (see the paragraph 'Over-breadth checks still missing' in 'Departures from the least-privilege rule in the end-state'); a start-up refusal in a pilot or production environment. Size: medium. | Studio head decides only the database-level `REVOKE` (Decision 9); the rest is our own code. |
| X-6.6 One login for several jobs (Major, rule part (b)) | `migrate()` knows three logins (`migrate.py:286-288`) and the app builds one store for everything (`app.py:146`). The agent loop also runs ALLOW-class actions and the client's hooks (`agent_loop.py:768`), so agent and tick are one login in practice unless that execution moves to the tick. | Any program that holds the runtime login: the agent, and the tick, which runs client hook code. | Items X-6.1 to X-6.4 stay open. | The login table below: eleven names (ten logins while agent and tick share one) and the migration owner, each with its own secret setting and grants; PER-PROCESS SECRET SCOPING comes first; `cli.py` changes (`:98`, `:138`, `:312`, `:330-343`, `:376-377`; its `operator` command writes audit events that the drain empties, see X-6.1); a start-up profile per process kind so the `operator` and `receiver` processes hold no signer or model credentials (PREREQUISITE: in production `signer: none` is refused for every process today, `config.py:419-420`, so these profiles need the setting 'this process does not sign' of X-5.3 option (i), Decision 5); PREREQUISITE and first piece of work (size: large; decider: the studio head, Decision 7; the builder writes it; the deployer sets up the processes): per-process secret scoping does not exist (see the paragraph 'PER-PROCESS SECRET SCOPING' in 'Departures from the least-privilege rule', after the login table), and without it a split protects nothing; moving ALLOW-class execution to the tick makes actions wait up to one tick interval. Size: large. | Studio head, Decisions 5, 7, 8, 21 and 22. |
| X-6.7 Rollout and rollback (Minor) | `migrate()` runs REVOKE ALL on each table before it grants again (`migrate.py:170-186`), so narrowing the map removes rights at the next migrate; there is no down-migration (`migrate.py:5`); new constraints can fail on rows already in a database in use; migration `0009` would break `ON CONFLICT (seq) DO NOTHING` (`audit/chain.py:312`) and stop signing silently unless the code change ships first. Migration numbers `0006` to `0009` are proposed and assigned when each change merges. | Nobody attacks here: the person deploying can lock the system out by narrowing too early. | A too-early narrowing locks the system out. | Two releases (A adds logins, secrets and database guards; B narrows the runtime map after an end-to-end proof); a data pre-check or NOT VALID constraints; rollback is the snapshot taken before `migrate`; CHANGELOG upgrade notes. Size: medium. | Studio head for timing (Decision 15); our code for the steps. |
| X-6.8 Reading the stored original values (Major, rule part (b)) | The runtime login holds `SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE` on `redaction_maps` (`store/migrate.py:65`, `_RW`), the table of plaintext originals of identifier kinds (`app.py:87-88` comment: 'redaction maps (plaintext originals) are kept for ever' when the purge login is missing; `postgres.py:496-510`), with no row or column limit: any code under the login can read every run's map. The masking rule (`.claude/rules/least-privilege-hard-rule.md`, item 4) asks for the map to be role-gated, and the portal never reads it (`migrate.py` comment). The same login also holds `SELECT, INSERT` on `tool_calls` (`migrate.py:62-64`, `_APPEND`); those rows are stored redacted (`0005_evidence.sql:3`), so they carry less weight. The agent itself legitimately reads its own run's map to restore values for the gate (`inputs_view`, `agent_loop.py`), and `execute` reads it again, so a read right cannot simply be removed. | Any code under the runtime login, which includes the client's hooks. | Hostile code in the agent or tick process can read the original identifiers of every run, not only its own. | Read rights cannot be narrowed until the process that restores values (the executor) is separate from the agent (Decision 8): then the agent login keeps `INSERT, UPDATE` on `redaction_maps` and no `SELECT`, and only the executor login reads. Row-level security keyed on a setting the agent can change is not a real control and is not proposed. Until then: accept in writing (the cost is the left cell). Needs a negative test per login (agent refused SELECT on `redaction_maps`; executor allowed; portal refused). Size: depends on Decision 8 (large). | Studio head, Decision 8. |
| X-6.9 Approved-plan integrity (Minor; source read, not run) | The runtime login holds `SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE` on `plans` (`store/migrate.py:48`, `_RW`) and `save_plan` is an upsert on `run_id` (`store/postgres.py:293-294`), so code under the login can change a stored plan after the approval. SOURCE READ (opened by me, nothing run): `execute` loads the plan with `get_plan` and the gate decision with `get_gate_decision` (`agent_loop.py:808-810`) and `_recheck` stops with `plan_changed` when `plan.hash()` differs from `decision.plan_hash` (`agent_loop.py:783`); `Plan.hash()` (`models.py:139-148`, opened by me) hashes the whole dumped plan (fields such as `inputs`, `target`, `idempotency_key` and `created_at` are `Plan` fields, `models.py:114-125`); it covers every field only while `_HASH_OMIT_IF_DEFAULT` (`models.py:137`) stays empty, and it is empty today; `gate_decisions` is append-only for the runtime login (`SELECT, INSERT` only, `postgres.py:301` comment, `_APPEND` in `migrate.py`) and a second row for the same run raises `StaleState` (`postgres.py:302-303`). So a swapped plan is stopped at `execute`, as long as the gate decision row cannot be replaced. NOT shown: that the primary key on `gate_decisions.run_id` (`migrations/0001_core.sql:37-38`, read) really refuses a second row on a real Postgres database (not run); that the hash is also checked on every path that runs a plan (only `_recheck` was read); what the approver was shown (the summary is written by the agent, X-6.4). THE MINOR RATING HOLDS ONLY for a run whose honest gate decision exists: code under the same login can INSERT a whole run, its plan, a gate decision and an APPROVED request (X-6.3, X-6.4), so this part stops the swap of an honest plan, not a forged run. | Any code under the runtime login. | A changed plan is stopped at `execute` (shown by source); the cost of accepting is a missing test and a dependency on `gate_decisions` staying append-only. | Add a test that a plan UPDATE after the gate decision makes `execute` stop with `plan_changed` and a test that the runtime login cannot UPDATE or DELETE `gate_decisions`; drop UPDATE on `plans` if no caller saves a plan twice for one run (`agent_loop.py:713` is the only caller read). Part of exit criterion (2). Size: small. | Our code; no studio head decision. |


### X-6.6 login table (proposed, not built; this is the table the edit list and X-6.1 to X-6.5 refer to)
Rule: each process holds exactly ONE login, built from the secret name of that login only; no process holds two. The names are `agent`, `tick`, `receiver`, `operator`, `k1b`, `drain`, `signer`, `dead-man reader`, `verifier`, `portal` and `purge` (eleven names; `portal` and `purge` exist today, the others are proposed) plus the migration owner (deploy time only). While agent and tick stay one login (Decision 8) that is ten logins and the migration owner. The three further identities the rule asks for (breakglass, CI deploy, humans) are in the departures table below. Every login has three things in this table: the exact rights, a reason for each right (the per-action reason the rule demands), and tests: a POSITIVE test (its own job works, because a negative test cannot notice a missing right) and NEGATIVE tests (what it must be refused). All tests are proposals, none exists, and every Postgres statement is UNVERIFIED. END-STATE WARNING: until Decision 8 is done the `agent` and `tick` login runs the client's `execute`, `verify` and `rollback` hooks, so the agent holds executor rights, and this table does NOT meet the rule 'agents never hold approver or executor credentials'. The rights in the first row that depart from the rule until then are: the executor rights themselves, `SELECT` on `redaction_maps` (X-6.8), `UPDATE` on `approval_requests` (needed by the tick's expiry; the guard of X-6.3 limits it), `UPDATE` on `spend` and `spend_month` (needed for the upsert; the trigger of X-6.2 limits it) and `INSERT` on the audit tables (departure X-6.6a). After Decision 8 the agent keeps `INSERT, UPDATE` on `redaction_maps` and loses `SELECT`; only the executor login reads it.

|---|---|---|---|---|
