### Departures from the least-privilege rule in the end-state (parts of X-6.6, not counted rows)
Rating rule: part (b), a broken written studio rule is Major; a missing design where no write function is added is Minor. Counted once, inside row X-6.6 (counting grain in 'Words and scales').

| id | departure | rating and reason | what closes it | decider |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| X-6.6a | Audit append rights (INSERT on `audit_entries`) for six names: `agent` and `tick` (one login while Decision 8 is open), `receiver`, `operator`, `k1b` and `drain`, which is five logins (the `signer` writes only `audit_checkpoints`). The rule asks for ONE append-only audit writer. | Major (part b). Present today for the one runtime login: it holds INSERT on the audit tables (`migrate.py:57`, `:59`); the portal login has SELECT only and the purge login nothing there. The end-state table gives INSERT to five logins, so the departure grows if the split is built before Decision 4 is answered. Same fact as X-5.2 and X-6.4. | the audit-writer function of X-5.2 option 2, or an acceptance note | Decision 4 |
| X-6.6b | Agent and tick hold executor rights (they run the client's `execute`, `verify`, `rollback`). The rule says agents never hold executor credentials. | Major (part b). Present today (one login). | Decision 8 (move ALLOW-class execution to the tick, then split the logins) or an acceptance note | Decision 8 |
| X-6.6c | Long-lived credentials: every login is one static connection string (item 2 of the rule asks for short-lived credentials only and for rotation of anything that exists). | Major (part b). Present today. | rotation lead and interval (stop-gap), then short-lived credentials | Decision 21 |
| X-6.6d | Breakglass, CI deploy and human identities are not designed (the rule asks for each). | Minor under the rating rule as written (no write function is added by their absence and no such account exists today); it becomes Major if a proposal or pilot offer claims the end-state table is complete. A design reviewer rates it higher because the rule's item 1 lists these identities as required; that is a reading of rule part (b) the studio head may take (Decision 20 and Decision 22), and it is disclosed here rather than settled. PROPOSAL TO THE STUDIO HEAD (not a change of any number): rate X-6.6d Major under part (b), because item 1 of the rule lists these identities and item 5 asks a policy, a negative test and an over-breadth review for every new role. EFFECT IF HE ACCEPTS: Major parts 9 to 10 and Major items 11 to 12; the counted rows stay 4 / 13 / 11 because X-6.6d sits inside X-6.6, which is already a Major part (the counting grain). I have not applied it and the script counts are unchanged. | a NEW design (no source document: `../ROLES-PROPOSAL.md` covers only five lead-intake database roles, see Decision 22) | Decision 22 |

Prerequisite NOT in this repository, and the first piece of work of any split (size: large; decider: the studio head, Decision 7; the builder writes the scoping and the start-up refusal; the deployer sets up separate processes and separate secret mounts): PER-PROCESS SECRET SCOPING. A login per job helps only if each process can read only its own secret. Today `SecretProvider.get(name)` (`actions/secrets.py:9`) returns ANY name, the app builds one `secrets` object (`app.py:82`, `:144-170`), and the environment kind (demo only) reads the whole process environment (`actions/secrets_env.py:17`, `:23`). So a new `operator` secret placed next to the agent's would be readable by the agent. A list of allowed names inside one process (precedent: `_own_secrets`, `app.py:254-261`, which limits a TOOL to its own `secret_names`) stops an honest caller asking for another name, but it is NOT isolation here: the tick runs client hook code in the same process (`agent_loop.py:886`, `:901`, `:909`), and that code can read `os.environ` itself, build its own provider, or use the process's own identity against a secrets manager. Real isolation needs separate operating-system or container processes with separate secret mounts or environments: the process that runs client hooks holds only its own login's secret, and the `operator`, `receiver`, `drain` and `signer` run as their own processes. Negative tests (run as each process, not only as a unit test of a wrapper): start the hook-running process with only its own secret present and assert that the other logins' names do not resolve and that its environment holds no other login's value; and a start-up refusal for a pilot or production environment like `app.py:86-89` when a process names the secret of a second login. A wrapper test that only shows a name being refused is a unit test of the wrapper and proves no isolation. Why it matters: any connection string in the tick process is reachable by client code; and "the drain gets its own login" protects nothing while the tick drain step does the drain inside the tick (X-5.1).
Credential lifetime (rule item 2, short-lived credentials): today each login is one static connection string read through the secrets kind, so it is long-lived. The store has an optional sign-in that mints short-lived tokens (`iam_auth: true`, `store/auth_aws.py`, `rds_iam`), but it is specific to one cloud (no-single-dependency: the neutral alternative is a rotating secret from the client's own secrets manager through the secrets kind; trigger to build it: the first client on a cloud without that sign-in). Which logins use it is not designed here; until it is, each login's secret needs a named rotation lead and a date. Decision 21 (the rotation lead and the interval need the studio head).
Audit-writer departure (rule item 1 asks for ONE append-only audit writer): this table gives audit append rights to `agent` and `tick` (one login), `receiver`, `operator`, `k1b` and `drain` (six names, five logins), so the end-state departs from the rule until the audit-writer function of X-5.2 option 2 exists (Decision 4). The departure is recorded here and needs the studio head's written yes (departure X-6.6a, Decision 4).
Identities the rule asks for that are not app logins (not designed here): `breakglass` (dormant, disabled, alarmed, outside the grant map), `CI deploy` (runs migrations as the migration owner through a short-lived federated sign-in, no stored key) and humans (viewer, operator, approver, admin; the approver uses a passkey and is never the requester or executor). Decision 22 (a new design is needed and no source document exists for it: `../ROLES-PROPOSAL.md` covers only five lead-intake database logins, which that file calls roles; see Decision 22).
Liveness per process and the dead-man reader (proposal; source read, nothing run). The step `checkpoint` in the tick is green with no signer (`tick.py:177-181`, `audit/chain.py:299-300`: `checkpoint()` returns None and does not raise), so the dead-man check gives false comfort while no checkpoint is signed. If the signer and the drain become their own programs (X-5.1, X-5.3), the tick no longer writes their sign of life and their `tick_state` row would stay old: a permanent false alarm. Fix: each process gets its own liveness row and its own right to write it, and the dead-man reader also reads two facts directly: the age of the newest signed checkpoint against the head (the head sequence number above `signed_through()` for longer than a limit) and the age of the oldest waiting row of `audit_outbox`. The exact columns and the exact grants for those two reads were not checked, and the SQL of those two reads is not written. The `dead-man reader` of this table and the role named `deadman_reader` in `patch-copies/DEAD-MAN-CHECK.md` are the same login; the login table uses the name with a space and that file the role name with an underscore. Until the advisory-lock revoke (Decision 9b) exists, even a login without write rights can call the public advisory-lock functions and stall every audit write, so 'read-only' does not mean it cannot hurt the audit (X-5.4).

Undeclared logins (criterion (8) and the refusal of a login that is not in the login table; design reviewer's gaps, each opened as far as stated): (1) on a managed database the administrator role is often not a real superuser, so a superuser flag check can miss it (unverified: not run on any managed service); (2) the check runs inside `migrate()`, so a login created after the last run is invisible until the next run; (3) where the declared list is kept and who may edit it is not said (proposal: one validated config place, edited only through a reviewed change); (4) the `dead-man reader` is in the table but under another name in its own file (see above); (5) no login has an expiry (`VALID UNTIL`) although Decision 21 asks for short-lived credentials. Each is a design item, none is built.

Over-breadth checks still missing (the paragraph, in the section 'Departures from the least-privilege rule in the end-state', that criterion (9) points to; restated after a source reading). WHAT `effective_problems` (`migrate.py:193-262`, opened by me, nothing run) ALREADY DOES: it asks Postgres what a login can really do, however it got the right (a direct grant, membership in another role, PUBLIC, a default privilege on an existing object, ownership; docstring `:194-198`; it calls `has_table_privilege`, `has_column_privilege` and `has_sequence_privilege`); it flags superuser, create-role, create-database and bypass-row-security (`:200-209`), CREATE on the schema (`:211`), owning a relation (`:219`), and any table, column or sequence right above the grant map on EVERY relation of the schema, including relations that are not in the grant map (`:213-217` and the loops after it). So a login that gets a right through an INHERITED membership in the table owner's role is already caught when that right exceeds the grant map. A membership that does not inherit (a NOINHERIT role; in Postgres 16 and later a membership granted with inherit false and set true) is NOT seen by these calls but still lets the member run `SET ROLE` into the table owner's role; gap (5) (`pg_has_role(..., 'MEMBER')`) is what covers it (Postgres behaviour from the manual, not run: UNVERIFIED). So a membership in the table owner's role can still slip past (1) to (8) when it is non-inheriting. WHERE IT IS CALLED (`migrate.py:340-356`): `effective_problems` for the runtime and purge logins only; the portal login gets the narrower `_excess_privileges` (`:265-280`: table and sequence rights only, no column, ownership, CREATE or superuser check); the new logins of the login table get no call at all. Membership is compared only between the runtime, portal and purge logins (`:333-339`). `effective_problems` and `_apply_grants` also look only at the schema that `current_schema()` returns (`migrate.py:210`, `:172`), so a relation in any other schema is not inspected. WHAT IS REALLY STILL MISSING: (1) EXECUTE on functions (for example the advisory-lock functions and any SECURITY DEFINER function): `migrate.py` has no `has_function_privilege` (grep, count 0); (2) the TEMPORARY right on the database (temp-table shadowing, X-6.5): no `has_database_privilege` (grep, count 0); (3) default privileges for the migration owner's FUTURE objects: no `pg_default_acl` read (grep, count 0; objects that exist today are already covered by the `has_*_privilege` calls); (4) a call of `effective_problems` for every new login (agent and tick, receiver, `operator`, `k1b`, drain, signer, dead-man reader) and for the portal; (5) memberships of the new logins in each other and in the table owner's role (`pg_has_role`, as at `:333` for three logins); (6) the schema scope: either refuse a deployment whose app tables sit in several schemas or run the checks over every schema the logins can reach (`migrate.py:210`). BASELINE AND ALLOWLIST (Postgres gives PUBLIC the right to CREATE temporary tables on a new database and EXECUTE on a new function by default, from the manual, UNVERIFIED, not run): the checks (1) and (2) therefore need the expected result written down for each login; it is in the text of criterion (9), which ties each expectation to Decisions 9a and 9b. PLUS one test: a shadowing negative test (a temporary table named like `approval_decisions`, created by the agent login, must not be read by the guards of X-6.3, or the login must be refused TEMPORARY if Decision 9a is yes). Each check needs a negative test per login. Size (assistant judgement, not measured): small to medium, about 1 to 2 days for the checks of today's three logins and the PUBLIC baseline; the per-login checks of the NEW logins wait until those logins exist and `migrate()` accepts them (Decisions 3, 5 and 7; see Decision 9). UNVERIFIED (Postgres behaviour not run).

Caveat on the purge precedent: `app.purge_store` (`app.py:151-160`) is a second login in the SAME process as the client hooks, so it is a precedent for the grant and migration mechanics, not for isolation.

## Single-dependency (lock-in) check
- Audit drain (A-01 and X-4): the two audit kinds REGISTERED in this checkout are `chain` (needs the in-memory store (an in-memory SQLite database, `chain.py:102-105`) or Postgres; there is no file SQLite option, `chain.py:523-543`; a chain over the memory store loses its events at exit, `k1b/audit_sink.py:21-23`) and `local_dir` (works with no database at all, `audit/local_dir.py:126-156`; it is in `DEMO_ONLY`, `backends/table.py:593-601`, and refused when the environment is live, `config.py:394-397`, so everything this file says about it (the `corrupt/` quarantine, the folder-permission split, the drain loop) concerns demo environments only and carries a lower weight than the chain kind) (`backends/table.py:502-511`). This is not a claim that only two exist: a client may add an audit kind (anything that fits the `AuditSink` Protocol), and `K1bAudit` (`k1b/audit_sink.py:26`) wraps a sink for the K1b CLI without being a kind; so the tick step must work through the Protocol for any of them. So the tick step must work only through the `AuditSink` Protocol (`audit/protocol.py:27`), with no SQL in the tick, or the local-directory kind loses its drain.
- A-3: a `pending_count()` or a changed `reconcile()` return is a contract change for every sink, including sinks that clients write; it needs a Protocol test and a default for old sinks (for example, a sink without the method is treated as unknown, not as empty).
- DEAD-MAN-CHECK.md (D-2): the SQL uses `make_interval`, which is Postgres only; the check has no equivalent for the local-directory kind or SQLite. Name that in the note and give the neutral way (read `tick_state` through the store kind) as the alternative.
- X-5: the separate drain identity is written for Postgres roles only (correction of the first wording): `store/migrate.py` imports `psycopg` and its `Grant` fields hold Postgres privilege strings, so a `drain` field is Postgres-specific; the local-directory kind and any other store kind have no grant map at all (for the directory kind the same split is a folder-permission question, not designed). The full list of logins is the login table under X-6.6 (eleven names). The `migrate()` change also needs `cli.py` changes that no earlier round listed: the `--runtime-role`, `--portal-role` and purge arguments (`cli.py:98` and its neighbours), the call at `cli.py:138`, `_migrate` at `cli.py:312` (its `migrate(` call is `cli.py:330-339`) and its warning at `cli.py:341-343`, and the message at `cli.py:376-377` ('check the runtime role's grants'), which names the wrong role once the kill switch is written by the `operator` login. Not designed for any other database kind. OUTLINE for a non-Postgres store (no-single-dependency): the login table in X-6 and its negative tests are written as an acceptance spec per Store Protocol method group (kill switch write: `operator` login only; approval decision write: receiver only; approval status change to APPROVED or REJECTED only with a decision on file; spend: increase only; audit outbox delete: drain only), so a store kind built on another platform must meet the same table with that platform's own access policy (for example one policy per identity and table; the transition rules also live in one shared Python function that every store kind calls, X-6.3); trigger to build it: the first client store kind that is not Postgres. NAMED DELIVERABLE (so the Postgres-only guards are not forgotten): the 'store acceptance spec', the login table plus its negative tests written per Store Protocol method group; every Postgres-only guard of X-5 and X-6 (triggers, column grants, `REVOKE TEMPORARY`, the advisory-lock `REVOKE EXECUTE`) must have a line in it. NOT BUILT: grep over `src`, `tests` and `docs` for 'acceptance spec' finds no file; it is a work item that belongs to Decision 5 and Decision 7. The `memory` store is in `DEMO_ONLY` (`backends/table.py:593-601`) and is refused when live, so it carries none of this.
- C-3: a check on `Route` is vendor-neutral. The K1b zero-price exemption keys on the fake kind (src `k1b/replay.py:88`), so the check must not name a vendor.
- C-8: a self-hosted model (kind `openai_compat` on your own server) is a neutral default, so the price rule must not make it unusable: the only exemption stays kind `fake`, and the self-hosted model gets a positive notional price (no new field, no vendor name in the check). A flag such as 'unmetered' would reopen the zero-price hole for hosted endpoints of the same kind. Not run.
- Short-lived credentials (X-6.6): `store/auth_aws.py` (`iam_auth: true`) is the one existing answer and is specific to one cloud; the neutral alternative is a rotating secret through the secrets kind (kinds registered in `backends/table.py:388-425`: `fake`, `env` (demo only), `aws_secrets_manager`, `vault`, `azure_key_vault`, `gcp_secret_manager`, `mounted_file`); trigger: the first client on a cloud without it; the person and the interval are Decision 21.
- Signer and anchor (X-5.3 option (i)): a signing key and an outside anchor place are outside things; option (i) puts the signer in its own process with its own secret. READ IN ROUND 19 (src `backends/table.py:427-490`, `audit/signer.py:1-12`, `audit/anchor_s3.py:9-11`, `:85`; names only, I did not open the cloud kind modules' code beyond their docstrings, so that vendor SDK imports stay inside them is carried by the repository's lock-in guard test `tests/test_lockin_guard.py`, not re-run by me). SIGNER: neutral default `ed25519_local` (a local key, Ed25519, checked with the public key alone; marked `neutral=True`); alternatives built: `aws_kms`, `azure_key_vault_key`, `gcp_kms`, `vault_transit`; the capability default when nothing is set is `none`, which is refused in production (`table.py:604`); trigger for an alternative: the client's own key store. ANCHOR: neutral default `s3_object_lock` (the S3 interface, also served by MinIO or Ceph through `endpoint_url`); alternatives built: `azure_immutable_blob`, `gcs_retention_lock`; default when unset `none`, refused in production; trigger: the client's own storage. So on the kind list the signer and anchor check passes, but the check as a whole does NOT pass for production: every process that must not sign (agent, tick, drain, `operator`, `receiver`) needs `signer: none`, which production refuses for every process today (`config.py:419-420`, `backends/table.py:604`), so the start-up rule must change first (X-5.3 option (i), Decision 5; this is a rule inside our code, not a vendor lock-in). NOT checked: whether a signing process can be configured with the signer kind alone.
- Dead-man alert channel (DEAD-MAN-CHECK.md, D-3 (3)): the place the alert goes (a chat tool, an e-mail service, a no-code flow) is a third party; the client chooses it under the client-chooses-tools rule, and the note names an export path and one alternative. NOT VERIFIED here: no alert kind or alert sender exists in `src` (grep for 'alert' outside `k1b/` finds nothing), so the claim that the note names an alternative rests on DEAD-MAN-CHECK.md, which I did not re-read; the alert path is outside this repository (an operator's own query and channel).
- Evidence base: the patch files, the patch README and DEAD-MAN-CHECK.md were read from scratch trees under `/tmp` (which may be cleared; see '`/tmp` paths' in 'Words and scales'). Copies of `C-01.patch`, `A-01.patch`, the patch README and `DEAD-MAN-CHECK.md` are kept in `patch-copies/` next to this file, with `evidence-13.md` already here. `G5-wording.patch` is NOT copied in the CURRENT tree: its context lines carry a personal repository handle (see G-2). That statement is true for the current tree only: the file was added once and pushed, and the handle stays in the branch history (see `../reviews/incident-g5-patch-copy.md`); only the lead or the studio head can decide to rewrite that branch or to accept it (Decision 23, part (b)). The handle is not printed in this file. Decision 23, part (a): the studio head decides whether a cleaned copy (which no longer applies to b4cce5b) may be kept.
Nothing here was run; all of it is read from source.

## Counts (by script)
Rule: every table row whose first cell is an id C-n, A-n, G-n, D-n or X-n is counted; severity is the second cell; decision is the first words of its decision cell (C, A and G rows have a 'merged from' cell, so their decision is in the fourth cell; D and X rows have none, so it is in the third cell; the script handles both layouts). C-1 was a Major in the first text and is a Nit after the citation check.

The only definitions of `reconcile()` for the audit are `audit/chain.py:232`, `audit/local_dir.py:126` and the Protocol `audit/protocol.py:27`, and nothing in `src/hadavin_sdk` calls them (only tests do): the tick step named `reconcile` (`runtime/tick.py:85`) is the approvals step (it calls `runtime.on_decided`), not the audit one. This is row X-4. An audit event that reached the outbox table (in the chain kind this happens only when the chain append fails but the outbox INSERT at `chain.py:223-227` succeeds, how often was not measured; the inline `checkpoint()` at `chain.py:203-204` can not cause it, it never raises, `chain.py:295-317`; if the database is down the outbox INSERT fails too and the event stays only in the in-memory `_pending`, `chain.py:229`, and is lost at exit, see A-1) stays there until someone calls `reconcile()` by hand, and no log line or red tick step says so; the A-6 wording "LOUD" holds only if A-01 is applied (the loudness is the step going red, which exists only with the step). ## Decisions for the studio head
Twenty-nine numbered decisions (Decisions 9 and 23 each have two parts: 9a and 9b, 23a and 23b), one question inside Decision 5 (5-K) and one question (Q1): 33 answers in all (29 + 2 + 1 + 1), each with a row in the table below. Decision 9 was split into 9a and 9b, and 5-K made its own question, in the batch logged in `../reviews/fix-review17.md`. Decisions 1 to 15, 21, 22 and 25 to 29 matter for today's code or for what may be sold today (they include the hold sentence, X-4, X-5 and X-6). Decisions 16 to 20, 23 and 24 matter only if a patch is applied, concern the rating, or are housekeeping. Each has: what to decide, what accepting today's state costs, and a recommended default (the assistant's recommendation; the decision is the studio head's, and an unanswered decision is NOT a yes, see the table below). The old numbering is mapped to these in History.

### Who does what, in what order, and what happens if nobody answers
ANSWER IN THIS ORDER (worked out from the 'decide after' column; there is no cycle): first three short questions: Q1 (a fact check and a reading; Decision 18 waits for it), 25 (one name: who is the deployer; the deployer part of Decisions 3, 5, 7, 9, 10, 15 and 21 waits for it) and 5-K (the one yes/no question inside Decision 5: do the K1b commands sign checkpoints themselves; asked here because Decision 2 option (b) cannot be built without it, so Decision 2 may not be answered 'b' before 5-K); then 7; then 26, then 27 (it needs the place named in 26), then 28 (it needs the binding of 27), then, in any order, 2 (its acceptance note needs 26 and 27), 4, 9a, 9b, 14, 19, 20, 22, 23a, 23b, 24; then the options of 5, and 8, 12, 21, 29; then 3, 6, 10, 13, 18; then 11 and 15; 16 and 17 only if their patch returns; 1 last (Decision 1 is the headline decision, but it closes only when 2 to 15, 21 and 25 to 29 are settled, so it is asked last). BECAUSE 1 IS ASKED LAST, a 'no' to Decision 7 (or to 3, 10 or 14) is recorded as 'no, and its effect on the hold is whatever Decision 1 then says' (option 1: the hold stays; option 2: a proposal may go with the open criteria stated). Decision 20 is a question about a word and a count: nothing about the hold changes with its answer, so it is no longer first.

Common to every decision: the DECIDER is the studio head. WHO DECIDES WHAT (a proposal to the studio head, not a rule): of the 33 answers, the studio head decides those that accept risk, name a person, spend money, change a rule or change a setting of a repository (1, 2, 5-K, 8, 9a, 9b, 12, 18, 20, 22, 25, 26, 27, 28, 29, Q1); the builder decides the engineering options (the parts of 5, 6, 11, 13 to 17) with a written reason that an independent reviewer checks, and the studio head can veto any of them. Reason for the proposal: 'if nobody answers nothing is built' lets the hold persist by inaction, and the project rule on deferring allows 'needs the owner' only for money, a login or a decision only he can make. Until he accepts the proposal, the sentence below stays in force for all 33. IF NOBODY ANSWERS (this replaces the earlier rule that the recommendation is the default): nothing is built or applied (except the work that needs no decision and changes no code of the repository: the designs, measurements and scratch-branch tries listed in 'Work that is possible now and still open'), the hold sentence stays, demos on made-up data go on, and the 'today' state continues but is NOT an accepted risk, because acceptance needs an acceptance note (Decision 1). A recommended default is the assistant's advice and never counts as a yes. DEADLINE: dates belong to the studio head; each decision has a trigger (before the proposal is sent, before the first pilot, before production, or before the patch is applied), and the work that is possible now but not yet done has a named doer and a PROPOSED date (assistant suggestion) in 'Work that is possible now and still open' below. None of the defaults, day figures or proposed dates rests on an outside source or a measurement; they are my judgement. SIZE and TIME: small, medium and large are relative judgements; the day figures are my estimate of agent work including one review round and the evidence run, NOT measured, and 'unknown because' means no honest figure exists yet.

### One plain block per decision (read these first; the numbered paragraphs further down keep the reasons and the sources)
Each block has six parts: Ask (the question), If yes, If no, Hold (what the answer does to the hold sentence), Size and by when, Who. The studio head is the decider of every one (see 'Common to every decision' above). 'Size' is agent work, estimated and not measured; it is not a price and not a calendar date unless a date is written (this review makes no paid model call; spending needs the studio head's yes with numbers). A date in a block is a UTC day and is written with its IST end (a UTC day ends at 05:30 IST on the next date). BUILD ORDER (this replaces the earlier sentence that Decisions 2, 3, 5, 7, 10, 12 and 14 can all be built at the same time; they cannot): WAVE 1, at the same time by different builders, each piece starting only once its own decision is answered in the answer order above (so Decision 5 starts after 7 is answered, and Decision 2 option (b) does not start before 5-K is answered): Decision 2 option (a) or (c) (with 6), run as the tick drain step under the runtime login that already holds the outbox rights today (`migrate.py:57`, `:59`); X-2 is met in two stages: PART 1 in wave 1, together with this decision (the patch NAMES the runtime login, ships the per-action reason and a negative test, and the studio head accepts the X-5 over-breadth in writing, which is the 'DECISION FIRST' of the X-2 row); PART 2 (a login of its own for the drain, criterion (6)) comes with Decision 3 in wave 2, when the tick drain step is switched off; Decision 5; Decision 7 secret scoping (not its start-up profiles); Decision 9a (the TEMPORARY revoke); and Decision 14; WAVE 2, once the 'this process does not sign' setting (Decision 29) and the scoped secrets (Decision 7) exist: Decision 2 option (b) (it needs the `k1b` login and the answer to 5-K), Decision 3, Decision 10 (then 11), Decision 12 (then 13, because freezing the digest needs the receiver as its own program) and the start-up profiles of Decision 7; WAVE 3: the criterion (9) checks for each login once that login exists, then Decision 9b (the revoke of EXECUTE on the advisory-lock functions; it breaks every audit writer not on the allowlist, so it waits until the positive tests of every login on the list pass; the earlier order, which ran Decision 9 alongside, could not meet criterion (9) in its mode A for the lock functions), then Decision 15. Decision 8 runs alongside. No total of days is computed because the waves overlap; the first dated figures are proposed for the end of 2026-11-19 UTC (05:30 IST on 2026-11-20; see the hold sentence). Terms used in the blocks: the HOLD is the rule that no proposal is sent and no pilot starts until the nine exit criteria are met; 'MET' means built and proved by a passing test for criteria (1), (6) and (9), and either that or an acceptance note for the criteria that may end on a note (criterion (2) only for its X-6.4 part, criterion (8) only for its departures); the OUTBOX is the table where an audit record waits when it cannot be written into the audit chain, and the DRAIN is the job that moves those records into the chain; a LOGIN is one database account plus its secret; a CONNECTION STRING (DSN) is the line that names the database and the login; SECRET SCOPING means each program is started so that it can read only its own login's secret, so that separate logins are real; the TICK is the scheduled job that runs the housekeeping pieces of work in a loop; the KILL SWITCH is the stored on/off row that stops agent runs (the emergency stop); the `operator` LOGIN is the proposed database account of the command that stops and resets the kill switch; the RECEIVER is the program part that takes an approver's click; K1B is the family of command-line replay and score tools, and the `k1b` LOGIN is the proposed database account they would use; a CHECKPOINT (a signed head) is a signed note of the audit chain's latest row, and to SIGN is to add that signature; an ALLOW-CLASS action is one the policy lets run without asking a person; a DATABASE TRIGGER is a rule inside the database that refuses certain changes; REVOKE TEMPORARY is a database command that removes the right to create short-lived scratch tables; the CEILING is the largest `max_tokens` number a model route may set (a limit on how long one model reply may be).

- **Question Q1 (which program makes the model call in the recorded take?).** Plain words: the recorded take is the saved run used for the video. One program or flow step in it asks the AI model a question. Ask: which program or flow step is that, and does it read its price list and model settings from the config file by calling `load_config` (in which case the settings are checked in one place), or does it write the settings straight into its own code as `Route(...)`? WHERE TO LOOK: `inputs/DECISIONS-2026-11-16.md`, item 1 (the lead's decision to keep the price check and cut the ceiling) and item 7 (the recorded take); the run sheet for the take; the flow files it names. A builder can then search those files for `load_config(` and `Route(`. THREE ANSWERS. YES (it reads the config with `load_config`): the price check applies to the take and Decision 18 can be asked. NO (the settings are written in code): the check inside `Config` never sees the take, so patch C-01 is NOT applied as written; this answer alone does not drop the price check: Decision 18 must put the check where cost is computed (C-3) or drop the patch (the ONE STATEMENT in 'Words and scales' says the same). I DO NOT KNOW (it is safe to say this; nobody can name the program, or there is no recorded take yet): this counts as no answer, and nothing is lost. Then C-01 stays 'do not apply now', Decision 18 cannot be asked, and the builder cannot run the search because no file is named. The studio head also confirms or vetoes my reading of item 1 (keep the price check, cut the ceiling). Hold: none; C-01 is not an exit criterion. Size and by when: one search that takes minutes once the file is named; before Decision 18 is asked. Who: the studio head names the file or says 'I do not know' (unknown: no file I read names it).
- **Question 5-K (inside Decision 5; asked first: do the K1b commands sign checkpoints themselves?).** Ask: do the K1b commands (`replay` and `score`, the replay and score tools) sign checkpoints themselves? K-A (yes): they hold a signing secret and may add checkpoint rows, so there are TWO signing logins (`signer` and `k1b`), the K1b process also loads the detector and model code of replay (a signing key sits next to that code), and criterion (3) needs a third test that no login except those two can add a checkpoint row. K-B (no): they use the setting 'this process does not sign' and the signing process signs their events later, so there is exactly one signing login (assistant note, not a recommendation: K-B keeps exactly one; unknown: the studio head answers). If unanswered: the `k1b` row of the login table cannot be finished and Decision 2 option (b) cannot be built. Hold: none by itself (it shapes criteria (3) and (6)). Size and by when: none (one answer); before Decision 2 is answered 'b' and before the proposal is sent. Who: the studio head.
- **Decision 1 (the hold).** Ask: which hold? OPTION 1 (recommended): KEEP the hold until all nine exit criteria are met. OPTION 2: SPLIT it into a proposal hold (acceptance notes plus a written statement in the proposal of which criteria are open) and a pilot hold (all nine met); needs the studio head's yes and changes the hold sentence; not applied. OPTION 3: CHANGE a criterion itself (for example let (1), (6) or (9) end on an acceptance note); only the studio head can; not recommended. A plain 'no' to option 1 is not an answer: it must name option 2 or 3, because no acceptance note can end criteria (1), (6) and (9). If option 1: no proposal and no pilot until then; demos on made-up data go on. If option 2: a proposal may go out with the open criteria named; it cannot truthfully promise that agents never hold approver or executor rights; the pilot stays held until all nine are met; each open criterion that may end on a note needs an acceptance note (four parts). If option 3: the studio head rewrites the criterion and this file is edited again. If unanswered: option 1 is in effect. WHY IT IS ASKED LAST: a 'no' to Decision 7, 3, 10 or 14 has the effect that Decision 1 gives it, so those answers are recorded as 'no, effect per Decision 1'. Hold: this decision IS the hold. Size and by when: no size of its own; it ends when the longest chain is done: Decision 7 code part, then the Decision 5 setting, then 3 and 10 (with 11), 12 (with 13), 14 option A, then the criterion (9) checks; Decision 8 and the database-down design of 2 are on the path only if the studio head refuses a note for (7) or (4); asked last, before the proposal is sent. Who: the studio head decides; the builder builds per criterion; an evidence runner runs the tests.
- **Decision 2 (who empties the outbox).** Ask: (a) a scheduled job empties it every tick, (b) the same plus every short K1b command empties it as it ends, or (c) nobody does? Option (b) may be chosen only after question 5-K is answered (the `k1b` login depends on it). Choosing (a) or (b) UN-DEFERS patch A-01, which the lead deferred by default 4 of 2026-11-16 (the studio head may veto that default); A-01 can return only after X-2 part 1 (the patch names the login that runs the new step, which for now is the runtime login, with the per-action reason, a negative test and the studio head's written acceptance of the X-5 over-breadth; part 2, the drain's own login, is Decision 3 in wave 2 and is not a condition of returning) and then the per-row guard A-6, in that order. If (a) or (b): audit records stuck in the outbox reach the audit chain by themselves; (b) makes the `k1b` login a second login that deletes outbox rows, although a K1b command parks an event in the outbox on ONE path only (the failure of the score report, `cli.py:262-270`; `emit` raises and never parks, `k1b/audit_sink.py:31-35`; replay never parks), so (b) drains only when a K1b command happens to run (unknown: studio head answers whether that is worth a second deleting login). If (c): they wait for a hand call, with no log line. The database-down case stays open in all three until the fallback outline is reviewed and built. Hold: criterion (4) is met by (a) or (b) built plus the database-down fallback built; with (c), or until the fallback exists, only an acceptance note ends (4), and it must say that a record written while the database is down is lost if the program exits and that the client was told. Size and by when (ONE trigger time for the whole of Decision 2, the same as in the table: before the proposal is sent, because criterion (4) is an exit criterion of the hold): (a) about 1 day (after X-2 part 1 and A-6), (b) about 2 days, the fallback about 2 to 3 days after its design is reviewed; the design is proposed to reach review by the end of 2026-11-18 UTC (05:30 IST on 2026-11-19); the fallback is built AFTER X-2 part 1, because it is a new write path and must pass that test first; all before the proposal is sent. Who: builder.
- **Decision 3 (a separate login for the drain).** Ask: give the job that empties the outbox its own login, the only one allowed to delete outbox rows? If yes: the agent's login can no longer erase pending audit records; this needs Decisions 5 and 7 first and the drain program. If no: the agent's login can delete pending audit records with no trace. Hold: criterion (6) names Decisions 3 and 7 BUILT and cannot end on a note, so a 'no' leaves (6) unmet and the hold on, unless the studio head changes criterion (6) or splits the hold (Decision 1). Recommended default (assistant suggestion): yes, but only together with running the drain program, with Decisions 5 and 7. Size and by when: about 2 to 4 days plus Decision 5; before the proposal is sent. Who: builder (rights, login, tests); deployer (the drain program).
- **Decision 4 (forged audit rows).** Ask: accept in writing that the runtime login keeps the right to add audit rows (after a split, every login that writes audit events), or build one audit-writer function in the database? If accept: false audit rows can be added; for a pilot only through criterion (5). If build: the audit trail has one append-only writer. Hold: criterion (5) may end on an acceptance note (accept) or on the refused-INSERT test (build), so either answer lets (5) end; 'accept' needs a note with the client told. Recommended default (assistant suggestion): accept in writing for demos; for a pilot only through criterion (5); design the function before production. Size and by when: accept none; build about 1 week or more and needs a real Postgres to prove; note before the proposal, function before production. Who: builder.
- **Decision 5 (who writes signed-head rows).** Ask: which option closes the junk-row problem? There are two kinds of option, and they may be combined: (i) and (iv) each close it (choose one of them as the main option); (ii) and (iii) are add-ons that may be added to either. (i) ONLY a separate signing program, with its own login and secret, may write signed-head rows (checkpoint rows), so no other program can write a fake one; it needs the setting 'this process does not sign'. (ii) the program that looks for the last signed head ignores rows whose signature does not check out; on its own it does NOT close the problem, because the table allows one row per audit position (`seq` is its only key, X-5.3) and a junk row at exactly the head position still blocks the signing insert, which is `ON CONFLICT (seq) DO NOTHING` (`chain.py:310-314`). (iii) the database refuses a signed-head row that matches no audit entry (a weak extra guard: an attacker can append the entry it needs). (iv) change the table's key so that a junk row and a valid row for the same position can both exist, and the program trusts only the row with a valid signature (needs `_check_anchors` rewritten, `chain.py:440`). Question 5-K has its own block above and is asked first. If (i): only the signing login (under K-A also `k1b`) can write a fake signed head. If none (accept today's state): one junk row silences checkpoint signing with no error. THE SETTING 'THIS PROCESS DOES NOT SIGN' is needed whichever option is chosen, because without it the `operator`, receiver and drain programs cannot start in production (`signer: none` is refused there today, `config.py:419-420`), so criteria (1) and (6) cannot be met; today it is sized only inside (i); if (i) is not chosen its cost is a part of the (i) figure that nobody has split out (unknown; the builder reports it). Hold: criterion (3) may end on an acceptance note, so accepting today's state is covered by one; but criteria (1) and (6) need the setting, so unless it is built those two cannot be met. Recommended default (assistant suggestion): (i), with (iii) added later. Size and by when: (i) about 3 to 6 days, (ii) medium, (iii) small, (iv) medium; before the proposal is sent. Who: builder; deployer (the signing program and its secret).
- **Decision 6 (lock and statement timeouts).** Ask: make the database cancel any audit write that waits too long for a lock or runs too long? If yes: one stuck program cannot freeze every audit write (accidents only). If no: a held lock hangs every audit write. Hold: none; no exit criterion names it (the revoke that stops a session NOT on the allowlist from holding the lock is Decision 9b; it does not stop the agent and tick login until X-6.4). Size and by when: under 1 day; together with Decision 2, never before a drain runs. Who: builder.
- **Decision 7 (split the one login, with secret scoping first).** Ask: split the shared login into separate logins, or accept the shared login in writing? If yes (recommended): secret scoping first, then two releases (one adds logins, one narrows rights), starting with `operator` and `receiver`. If no: the kill switch can be turned off, spend zeroed and an approval self-given, silently; what that does to the hold is what Decision 1 says (asked last): option 1 keeps the hold on, option 2 lets a proposal go with (1) and (6) open and stated. Hold: criteria (1) and (6) cannot end on a note, so accepting the shared login in writing does NOT end the hold; it leaves (1) and (6) unmet. Only the split hold of Decision 1 (proposal allowed with the open criteria stated, pilot still held) lets a proposal go out with a 'no'. Size and by when: large; code part about 3 to 5 days (excludes Decisions 3 and 5); deployer part unknown until Decision 25 names the deployer; before the proposal is sent; first measured figure proposed for the end of 2026-11-19 UTC (05:30 IST on 2026-11-20). Who: builder; deployer (separate programs and secret mounts).
- **Decision 8 (run ALLOW-class actions in the tick, not in the agent).** Ask: move them, so agent and tick can be separate logins? If yes: ALLOW-class actions wait up to one tick interval; the agent loses the right to read stored original values. If no: agent and tick stay one login that runs the client's hooks, which departs from the rule 'agents never hold executor credentials'; accept that in writing. Hold: criterion (7) (the agent login cannot read the stored originals) may end on a note until Decision 8 is done, and the departure X-6.6b in criterion (8) may be covered by a note; so a 'no' does not keep the hold on, PROVIDED both acceptance notes exist (one for criterion (7), one for the departure X-6.6b in criterion (8)); until both exist, criteria (7) and (8) are unmet. A 'yes' that is not yet built is in the same state: unmet until built or noted. Recommended default (assistant suggestion): yes if Decision 7 is yes; otherwise the departure is accepted in writing. Size and by when: large; no figure; first piece of work is a try on a scratch branch, report by the end of 2026-11-19 UTC (05:30 IST on 2026-11-20; proposal); before production. Who: builder.
- **Decision 9a (database-level revoke of TEMPORARY from PUBLIC).** Ask: take away from every program except the migration owner the right to create scratch tables in the database (TEMPORARY)? If yes (the outcome is my reading of the Postgres manual, UNVERIFIED, not run): a scratch table can no longer hide a real table; other tools on that database may be refused scratch tables. If no: the guards rely on schema-qualified names and a pinned `search_path` being right everywhere, the TEMPORARY departure needs an acceptance note, and criterion (9) is checked in its mode B for TEMPORARY. A 'no' counts only when it names an acceptance note that already exists for this part (bound as in criterion (8)); until then the answer counts as unanswered, not as a 'no'. The mode (A or B) is written into the stored result of criterion (9), and a later change from yes to no needs a new note. Mode B is a setting plus a note, not a protection. Hold: a 'no' does not end criterion (9); it changes what (9) expects. Recommended default (assistant suggestion): yes where only our app uses the database; on a database shared with other tools, ask who else needs scratch tables first; answered per deployment. Size and by when: the revoke under 1 day; before production on a shared database; the checks with 9b. Who: deployer (the database owner or a superuser; UNVERIFIED); builder (the checks).
- **Decision 9b (database-level revoke of the advisory-lock functions from PUBLIC).** Ask: take away from every program the right to run the advisory-lock functions (the lock whose hold stops every audit write; all eight lock and try-lock functions, listed in criterion (9)) and give back only the one-number form `pg_advisory_xact_lock(bigint)`, the only lock call in `src` today, and only to the migration owner and the logins that append audit entries (the agent and tick login, receiver, `operator`, `k1b` and drain; `signer` is NOT on the list from the start: my reading of `checkpoint()` is that it calls no lock function)? If yes (the outcome is my reading of the Postgres manual, UNVERIFIED, not run): a program that is NOT on the list can no longer hold the audit lock; the agent and tick login IS on the list (it appends audit entries and runs the client's hooks, which are hostile under T1), so the claim 'a hostile program can no longer hold the audit lock' is STRUCK for that login and stays false until the audit writer is its own login (X-6.4); every audit writer not on the list stops working, so this revoke is the last piece of work (wave 3). If no: any login can hold the audit lock (X-5.4); this departure needs an acceptance note, and criterion (9) is checked in its mode B for EXECUTE. VALUE STATEMENT (honest): the revoke does little for the main threat today, because the agent and tick login stays on the list, the revoke needs a database administrator, and every audit writer left off the list breaks at run time; its value starts when the audit writer is its own login (X-6.4). Cheaper protection against an accident is Decision 6 (lock and statement timeouts), which does not wait for X-6.4. The head may therefore answer 9b 'no, for now' with a note and criterion (9) in its mode B. A 'no' counts only when it names an acceptance note that already exists for this part (bound as in criterion (8)); until then the answer counts as unanswered. The mode is written into the stored result, and a later change from yes to no needs a new note. Mode B is a setting plus a note, not a protection. Hold: a 'no' does not end criterion (9). Recommended default (assistant suggestion): yes, but only after the positive tests of every login on the list pass; until then no, with the note. Size and by when: the revoke under 1 day (one deployment action) after wave 3; before production on a shared database. Who: deployer (a superuser: these functions belong to the built-in catalog, whose owner is the bootstrap superuser, so a plain database owner probably cannot revoke them; UNVERIFIED, not run; on a managed host without a superuser it may be impossible, which is exception (a) of the fix-do-not-defer rule: then mode B and a note apply); builder (the checks).
- **Decision 9, both parts: what the four answers do, and where the answer is recorded.** (9a yes, 9b yes): both in mode A: the checks demand that no login holds TEMPORARY and that EXECUTE on the lock functions is held only by the allowlist. (9a yes, 9b no): TEMPORARY in mode A, EXECUTE in mode B (every login holds the lock functions only through PUBLIC), one note for the lock departure. (9a no, 9b yes): TEMPORARY in mode B (a note, schema-qualified names, a pinned `search_path`, the shadowing test), EXECUTE in mode A. (9a no, 9b no): both in mode B, two notes. THE ANSWER IS RECORDED AS A SETTING WITH NO DEFAULT (proposal, not built): two config values, one per part (the builder chooses their names in the one validated config place), each `yes` or `no`; when either is absent, `migrate()` and the start-up check refuse to run in a pilot or production environment, so a deployment can never fall back silently to the weaker mode; each deployment records its own answers (a client database shared with other tools may answer differently from our own). WHO DOES THE REVOKE: the deployer, once Decision 25 names one (UNVERIFIED which database right each revoke needs, see 9a and 9b); if nobody is named, the revoke has no doer and criterion (9) stays open.
- **Decision 10 (agent login loses the right to add approval decisions in the first release).** Ask: yes or no? If yes (recommended): only the receiver can record a decision; approvals stop until the receiver has its own connection string; needs the receiver as its own program and the 'does not sign' setting. If no: no protection against self-approval in the first release. Hold: criterion (1) names 'record an approval decision' as refused, built, and cannot end on a note; a 'no' only moves the removal to a later release, and (1) stays unmet until then. Size and by when: about 2 days; before the proposal is sent. Who: builder; deployer (the receiver's secret).
- **Decision 11 (receiver may change only the status column).** Ask: yes or no? If yes (recommended): a hijacked receiver cannot change what an approval points at. If no: the receiver keeps UPDATE on every column of `approval_requests`; only the database guard limits it. Hold: no criterion names it alone; criterion (1) still needs the guard that refuses APPROVED without a decision row. Size and by when: about 1 day, with Decision 10. Who: builder.
- **Decision 12 (bind the approved values where the agent cannot write).** Ask: store a frozen digest (a fingerprint of the approved values) in the approval request and compare it before the action runs? If yes (the assistant's suggestion, not a default): a forged approval record cannot make changed values run. COST OF YES: a new stored field and a comparison on the money path, a migration of existing approval requests (those without a digest must be refused or re-asked), and a test that a changed plan stops at `execute`; it also adds a place that can wrongly block a valid action (a false refusal, handled by asking a person again). If no: a forged approval record can make changed values run. Hold: criterion (2), its X-6.4 part, may end on a note, so a 'no' is covered by one; the X-6.9 test (a changed plan stops at `execute`) is still needed. Size and by when: about 3 to 5 days; money path, no round cap; before the proposal is sent. Who: builder.
- **Decision 13 (who closes the agent-written approval summary).** Ask: the receiver rebuilds the summary and approver list from the stored plan, or nobody does? If rebuild (recommended): the approver sees text the agent did not write. If nobody: a hijacked agent can show an approver text that differs from the stored plan. Hold: as Decision 12: the X-6.4 part of criterion (2) may end on a note. Size and by when: about 1 to 2 days, with Decision 12. Who: builder.
- **Decision 14 (spend counters).** Ask: option A (a database trigger that refuses lowering the counters) or option B (each spend stored as an added row, totals added up)? If A (recommended first): cheapest; Postgres only. If B: stronger but a rewrite of the money path. If neither: counters can be zeroed. Neither proves real spend. Hold: criterion (1) names 'refused any lowering of spend' as built and cannot end on a note, so 'neither' leaves (1) unmet and the hold on. Size and by when: A under 1 day, B several days (money path); before the proposal is sent. Who: builder.
- **Decision 15 (when to narrow the shared login's rights).** Ask: run the narrowing release after one end-to-end proof on a scratch database and once the logins' secrets exist? If yes (recommended): low lock-out risk. If earlier: a lock-out is possible. Hold: none; timing only. Size and by when: under 1 day plus the scratch proof; when the release is planned. Who: deployer with builder.
- **Decision 16 (`max_tokens` ceiling number; only if it returns).** Ask: what number, if the ceiling (the largest `max_tokens` a route may set) comes back? If left cut (recommended): no cost today. If it returns with a wrong number: a valid call is blocked or a ten-times typo passes. Hold: none. Size and by when: small; when the ceiling returns. Who: builder.
- **Decision 17 (A-3 return contract; only if A-01 returns).** Ask: change what `reconcile()` returns, or add a separate `pending_count()`? Recommended: the separate one. If no decision: a stuck backlog shows as nothing. Hold: none. Size and by when: small; when A-01 returns. Who: builder.
- **Decision 18 (shape of patch C-01; answer Q1 first).** Ask (after Q1 is answered; the ONE STATEMENT in 'Words and scales' lists what each Q1 answer allows): with Q1 yes, price-only inside `Config`, price-only where cost is computed (recommended), or drop the patch; with Q1 no, price-only where cost is computed, or drop the patch. The per-row guard A-6 is NOT part of this decision: it belongs to A-01 and is dealt with in Decision 2 (the 'it' of the earlier wording meant A-01, not C-01). If price-only: a zero price is refused everywhere cost is computed. If no decision: no price check exists and a zero price disables every spend cap for a route built outside `load_config`. Hold: none; C-01 is not an exit criterion. Size and by when: about 1 day; before any pilot or production configuration exists. Who: builder.
- **Decision 19 (price of a self-hosted model).** Ask, in two parts. (1) Accept that only the `fake` kind may cost nothing, and that a self-hosted model gets a positive stand-in price? (2) If yes, which number? Answer as one of: 'yes, the number is N', where N is a decimal price in US dollars per million tokens, greater than 0, for example 0.05 (the config holds the two prices as `Money`, a `Decimal`, `config.py:56` and `:64-68`, read, so it is not a whole number; give two numbers if input and output differ; a tiny number such as 0.000001 passes the check but makes the limits useless, so give a real estimate; whether this field should follow the studio rule 'money is integers' was not checked); 'yes, the builder proposes a number and I confirm it before the patch is applied'; or 'no'. If yes: C-01 can refuse every zero price. THE TWO BAD RESULTS, named: BAD 1: a self-hosted model priced at zero cannot load, so a keyless local-model configuration does not start; BAD 2: the exemption is widened beyond kind `fake`, so that a zero price reaches a hosted endpoint and switches every spend limit off for it. If no: the design as written produces bad 1 (C-01 refuses the zero price); bad 2 happens only if the studio head asks for it by name, and I advise against it. Hold: none. Size and by when: none (a README note and one test); with Decision 18, and the number is needed before C-01 is applied. Who: the studio head gives the number or confirms the builder's; builder (the note and the test).
- **Decision 20 (the Major rating rule).** Ask: which rule decides what counts as Major, and should defects of today's code of this size carry the word Blocker? Options, with the counts as Major / Minor / Nit: (1) keep the rule as written (recommended): 4 / 13 / 11; (2) a conditional or deferred row is Minor (X-2 becomes Minor): 3 / 14 / 11; (3) any gap in a deferred patch is Major (A-2 and A-3 become Major): 6 / 11 / 11; (4) a hostile model endpoint is in the threat model (T2; only C-7 becomes Major, because a recording or endpoint supplies token counts and the route price comes from the config file): 5 / 12 / 11; (5) a defect of today's code that breaks the least-privilege rule is called Blocker (the nine Major parts of X-5 and X-6 carry the word; the counted rows stay Major, X-4 stays Major): 4 / 13 / 11; (6) a money limit that one bad number switches off is Major (C-3 and C-7 both become Major; this is not T2): 6 / 11 / 11. If you answer differently: the assistant re-counts and the script output changes; counts for two options at once are not computed. Hold: none; nothing about the hold changes with this answer. Size and by when: none; before the next review round. Who: the assistant re-counts.
- **Decision 21 (credential lifetime and rotation).** Ask: (a) name a rotation lead and an interval for changing each login's secret now, (b) build short-lived credentials? If (a): a stop-gap (assistant suggestion for the interval: 90 days, no source). If (b): the real fix, for the first client on a cloud that supports it. If neither: a stolen connection string works until someone changes it. Hold: the departure X-6.6c in criterion (8) may be covered by an acceptance note, so a 'no' does not keep the hold on PROVIDED that note exists; criterion (8) is unmet until option (a) is done, option (b) is built, or the note exists. Size and by when: (a) small, (b) medium to large and unknown; before the proposal is sent. Who: a rotation lead named by the studio head (he may name the deployer once Decision 25 names one, or another person; there is no default person); builder only for (b).
- **Decision 22 (breakglass, CI deploy and human identities).** Ask: write a new design for the emergency account, the deploy identity and the human accounts before the first pilot? If yes: about 3 hours of agent time (order of magnitude only). If no: the login table (database logins only) stays incomplete against the least-privilege rule, which also asks for these three identities and the proposal must not call it complete. Hold: none; not an exit criterion. Size and by when: about 3 hours of agent time; proposed date for the design: the end of 2026-11-18 UTC (05:30 IST on 2026-11-19), only if the studio head answers; latest moment: before the first pilot. Who: the assistant.
- **Decision 23a (cleaned copy of the G5 patch).** Ask: may a cleaned copy be kept in the repository? (Why it is asked: the patch text carries a personal repository handle in two context lines; a cleaned copy would not show it.) If yes: it would no longer apply to b4cce5b. If no (recommended): whoever applies G5 rebuilds the patch from this checkout. Hold: none. Size and by when: none; before the patch is applied. Who: the assistant.
- **Decision 23b (the personal handle already in the history of branch `cloud/flows-audit`).** Ask: rewrite the branch history, or accept the exposure in writing? Rewrite only if nobody else has fetched the branch; if others already fetched it, accept in writing instead. Nobody can tell from this file whether others fetched it: the studio head asks the people who have access and answers on their word (a statement, not a measurement); if nobody can say, he decides which risk he takes. If rewrite (assistant suggestion, before any pull request is opened from the branch): the handle is gone from the branch; every commit from `34540bfa2` to the tip gets a new id and anyone who fetched the branch must fetch it again. If accept: the handle stays readable in the history of a repository that may be shared; an acceptance note is needed. Hold: none; housekeeping. Size and by when: one rewrite and one push, time not measured. Who: the studio head names who rewrites (himself, or the lead if he hands it over); the assistant that edits this file will not rewrite a shared branch.
- **Decision 24 (licence-file conflict).** Ask: which document wins? OPTION A (the heavy-lane summary, `../heavy-test-lane/SUMMARY.md`, section 'What the studio head must decide'): fix the files, including putting the studio licence text in the 10 LICENSE files (or moving those inputs out of the repository if they are copies of other projects' files). ITEM 11 (`inputs/DECISIONS-2026-11-16.md`): licence copies under `handover/promotion-inputs` are never overwritten or re-licensed. Recommended: item 11. If no answer: nothing changes today (the licence test passes). Hold: none. Size and by when: none; before any licence text changes. Who: the assistant.
- **Decision 25 (who is the deployer).** Ask: who is the deployer, the person who sets up separate programs and secret stores at a client or on the studio's machines? Two readings, not facts: on the studio's own machines the only person the repository describes is the studio head; at a client it may be a client person (unknown: studio head answers). If answered: Decisions 3, 5, 7, 9, 10, 15 and 21 get a named doer for their deployer part, and the hold can get a date. If unanswered: that deployer work has no doer and no size, so criteria (1), (6) and (8) cannot get an end date. RULE FOR THE ANSWER (least privilege, item 1 of the rule: CI deploy has its own identity, breakglass is dormant): the deployer is a human or a CI job that gets short-lived credentials by federation (OIDC); it is NEVER an agent session, and no agent session may hold a deployer, migration-owner or breakglass credential. Hold: stays on; criteria (1) and (6) need the deployer's separate programs and secrets. Size and by when: none (one name); before the deployer part of those decisions is scheduled and before the proposal is sent. Who: the studio head.
- **Decision 26 (where acceptance notes live).** An acceptance note is a short signed text that says: this open criterion is accepted as a risk, by whom, until when, and why. Ask: where do notes live? The rule that matters: a note about a client belongs in that client's own repository or in an outside store, filed by client code, never in this shared public repository (`no-lost-messages-isolation-load.md` item 7 and R373: one repository per client; a client name in a note here would break it). Options: (a) a folder named by the studio head in the client's own repository, with an empty four-part template created by the builder; (b) an outside store (a private folder or document store) the studio head names, one sub-folder per client code; (c) `handover/acceptance-notes/` in this repository, only for notes that name no client (about the core itself). Recommendation (assistant suggestion): (a) for client notes and (c) for core notes. If answered: the builder creates the template within one working day, and the start-up check can name notes. If unanswered: no place exists, no note counts, and nothing can close a criterion by a note. Decisions 27 and 28 wait for this answer (order: 26, then 27, then 28; Decision 2 needs 26 and 27 for its acceptance note). Changing the rule in item 2 of that rule file (a known gap is a defect, not an accepted exposure) needs the studio head's explicit yes; an acceptance note does not change it, it only records a yes given by him. Hold: unchanged. Size and by when: under 1 day of builder work (assistant judgement, not measured); before the proposal is sent. Who: the studio head names the place; the builder creates it.
- **Decision 27 (how notes and evidence are bound to the studio head).** Needs Decision 26 first (the place must exist). Terms: the SIGNED SUBTREE is the set of files under `src/` and `tests/` whose combined hash is signed; its hash names the exact program being approved (not a commit, which also carries files that do not run). Ask: which binding? OPTION (i): a detached signature over the signed subtree hash, the criterion and the result hash, with the public key pinned outside the repository and a dedicated approval key. Cost: a key to keep and a check to build, about 3 to 6 days of builder work (assistant judgement, not measured). Risk: a lost or leaked key; the key must be held by the studio head on his own device (or a hardware key) and NO agent session may hold it or reach it (least-privilege rule: agents never hold approver credentials). The check reuses the existing `Signer` Protocol and `verify_signature` in `audit/signer.py` and the signer kinds (aws, azure, gcp, vault) instead of new signing code. OPTION (ii): the notes folder under CODEOWNERS with the studio head as the only code owner plus a branch rule that requires his review. Cost: a repository-setting change (needs his explicit yes) and, with one person, a possible deadlock where nobody else can approve; how it works on the host was not checked (UNVERIFIED). Risk: the host, not our code, enforces it, and it binds nothing outside that repository. Recommended (assistant suggestion): (i). The recommendation is not a yes. If unanswered: a note is only typed text and counts for nothing. Hold: unchanged; criterion (8) cannot be met without a binding. Size and by when: see the cost above; the binding must exist before the first pilot (a choice with a consequence: if it exists only before the pilot, the proposal may go out with criterion (8) open and stated; if the head wants it before the proposal, the proposal waits). Who: the studio head chooses; the builder builds the check.
- **Decision 28 (is a start-up refusal wanted).** Needs Decision 27 (the refusal checks the signature and the signed subtree hash). Ask: should a pilot or production start be refused by the program when a criterion is open without a signed note, or is claimed met without evidence for the program being started (its signed subtree hash, Decision 27)? If yes: the check of criterion (8) is built and tested. If no: the check is not built and criterion (8) stays open for its check part. Under Decision 1 this means: with option 1 the hold stays; with option 2 the proposal may go with criterion (8) listed as open, and the only protection is that the studio head himself does not start a pilot without a note (a person, not the program, stops it). Hold: a 'no' does not end criterion (8). Size and by when: small to medium (assistant judgement, not measured); the check must exist before the pilot starts (before the proposal if the head wants the refusal promised in it). Who: the studio head; the builder.
- **Decision 29 (the setting 'this process does not sign', and its size).** This is the one place where this setting is decided (Decision 5 only points here). Today `signer: none` is refused only in production (`config.py:419-420`, `NOT_PROD` at `backends/table.py:604`, `is_prod` in `stages.py:18-23`; read), so the problem is for production starts only; other stages already accept it. Ask: build a setting that says a process does not sign checkpoints, so the drain program and the K1b commands can run in production without a signing secret (X-5.3 option (i))? One size: small to medium, about 3 to 6 days of builder work including the start tests (assistant judgement, not measured). If yes: the builder builds it; criterion (6) can then be met. If no: criterion (6) cannot be met in production, so the drain program and the K1b commands would need the signing secret (a larger exposure, see Decision 5 option (ii)) and Decision 1 says whether the proposal may go. Hold: criterion (6) stays open until built. Before the pilot; before the proposal if the head wants criterion (6) closed in it. Who: the studio head; the builder.

### Matter today
1. **Proposal and pilot while one login is shared (X-5, X-6).** Decide whether the sales proposal may be sent and a pilot started while the one shared login stays. The hold sentence (Words and scales) is this file's Blocker-equivalent: it stops the proposal and the pilot the way a Blocker stops an apply, and it ends only on the exit criteria below. EXACT EXIT CRITERIA (the hold ends only when ALL nine are met; the table right below gives, per criterion, who does the work, the size, the proof file and whether it may end on an acceptance note; the text of each criterion follows the table). Every test runs on a scratch database (defined in 'Words and scales') and its raw output is saved by an evidence runner, who does not judge; the test file names below are proposals, none exists yet.

| Criterion | What must be true | Who does the work | Size (estimate, not measured) | Proof file | May it end on an acceptance note? |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | The agent login cannot switch the emergency stop off, lower spend, record an approval decision or make a request APPROVED alone; the secret-scoping proof of (6) also passes | builder; evidence runner runs the tests; deployer for the separate secrets | not added up: Decision 10 about 2 days, Decision 11 about 1 day, Decision 14 option A under 1 day, plus the code part of Decision 7 (about 3 to 5 days, which excludes Decisions 3 and 5) | `evidence-<n>.md` (raw output and commit) | NO: needs the built guards and passing tests |
| 2 | A forged approval record cannot make changed values run (X-6.4); a changed plan stops at `execute` (X-6.9) | builder; evidence runner | Decision 12 about 3 to 5 days (money path, no round cap); Decision 13 about 1 to 2 days; the X-6.9 test small | evidence file of the X-6.4 test and of the X-6.9 test | PART: the X-6.4 part may end on a note; the X-6.9 test is still needed |
| 3 | One junk checkpoint row does not stop signing, and the signer login signs the first checkpoint of an empty chain (X-5.3); under K-A of question 5-K also: no login except `signer` and `k1b` can add a checkpoint row | builder; deployer (the signing program and its secret) | Decision 5: option (i) about 3 to 6 days, (ii) medium, (iii) small, (iv) medium | evidence file of the two tests | YES |
| 4 | The outbox is emptied (X-4, Decision 2); the database-down case is covered | builder | Decision 2: (a) about 1 day, (b) about 2 days; the database-down fallback about 2 to 3 days AFTER its design is reviewed (today only an unreviewed outline exists) | evidence file of the seed-and-drain test | YES, and until the database-down design is reviewed and built a note is the only way; the note must say that a record written while the database is down is lost if the program exits, and that the client was told |
| 5 | Forged audit rows are refused, or accepted in writing (Decision 4) | builder | option 1: none (note); option 2: about 1 week or more | the note, or the evidence file of the refused-INSERT test | YES |
| 6 | Decisions 3 and 7 are BUILT: the drain program exists and runs; each program holds only its own login's secret | builder; deployer | Decision 3 about 2 to 4 days plus the 'does not sign' setting (Decision 29; inside the figure of Decision 5 option (i), about 3 to 6 days); Decision 7 code part about 3 to 5 days, deployer part unknown | raw output of both runs | NO: needs the built programs and passing tests |
| 7 | The agent login cannot read the stored original values (X-6.8) | builder | Decision 8: no figure yet (first figure proposed for the end of 2026-11-19 UTC, which is 05:30 IST on 2026-11-20) | evidence file of the three-login test | YES, until Decision 8 is done |
| 8 | The departures X-6.6a, X-6.6b and X-6.6c are closed or covered by a note, AND the start-up check of the hold sentence exists (proposal: a pilot or production configuration is refused when any criterion (1) to (9) is open and names no existing note file, or is claimed met and names no evidence file for the program being started (its signed subtree hash, Decision 27) with a recorded pass; notes bound to the studio head, see the text of (8)) | builder; deployer for X-6.6c | the start-up check small to medium (assistant judgement, not measured); Decision 21 option (a) small; Decision 8 as in (7); Decision 4 as in (5) | raw output of the two start tests (refused; starts), and the notes | PART: the departures may end on notes; the start-up check must be built and its two start tests must pass |
| 9 | Over-breadth proof (the six gaps, with the baseline and allowlist written in the text of criterion (9), and the shadowing test) | builder; evidence runner; deployer for the revokes of Decisions 9a and 9b | checks for today's three logins and the PUBLIC baseline small to medium, about 1 to 2 days; the per-login checks for the new logins wait for Decisions 3, 5 and 7 (their `migrate()` work is counted there) | raw output of both runs | NO: needs the built checks and passing tests; under Decision 9a 'no' the TEMPORARY departure and under 9b 'no' the lock departure are each covered by a note (the built checks and passing tests are still required) |

Which criteria CANNOT end on an acceptance note: (1), (6) and (9). Criteria (3), (4), (5) and (7) may end on a note alone; (2) only for its X-6.4 part (its X-6.9 test is still needed); (8) only for its departures (its start-up check must be built). The folder for notes does not exist yet and nothing checks a note (see the hold sentence). The text of each criterion:
- (1) X-6.1, X-6.2 and X-6.3 built, proved by: a negative test per right (the agent login is refused UPDATE on `kill_switch`, refused any lowering of `spend` and `spend_month`, refused INSERT on `approval_decisions`, refused a change of an approval request to APPROVED without a decision row; each asserts the exact error class `InsufficientPrivilege` or the guard's own error), AND a positive test per login (the `operator` login can switch the stop on and off; the `receiver` login can record a decision; the agent can still do its own job: create a run, save a plan, add spend), AND the secret-scoping proof of (6), because a refused login can be replaced by a stolen secret that a process next to it can still read. Paper: `evidence-<n>.md` with the raw output and the commit.
- (2) X-6.4: built, proved by a test in which a forged `gate.decide` record with a matching digest, appended by the agent login, is refused or ignored at `execute`; or accepted by an acceptance note (below); and X-6.9 (approved-plan integrity): a test that an UPDATE of the stored plan after the gate decision makes `execute` stop with `plan_changed`, and that the agent login cannot change or replace the gate decision row.
- (3) X-5.3: built, proved by the two tests named in X-5.3 (a junk checkpoint row planted at the head plus 1000 does not stop signing: a valid row exists at the head seq and `signed_through()` equals the head; and the signer login signs the first checkpoint of an empty chain), or accepted by an acceptance note. If question 5-K of Decision 5 is answered K-A (the K1b commands sign), a third test is needed: the `k1b` login can add a checkpoint row and no login except `signer` and `k1b` can; if K-B, a test that a K1b process that holds a signer secret refuses to start.
- (4) X-4 and Decision 2: the chosen option built and proved by a test (seed one outbox row, run the tick or the exit call, assert one chain row and an empty outbox). For the database-down case only an OUTLINE exists (Decision 2; not reviewed, not built), so until it is reviewed and built the only way to meet it is an acceptance note that says plainly 'an audit record written while the database is down is lost if the program exits' and records that the client was told. The design, and then its test (take the database down, write one event, restart, assert the event is found and chained once the database is back), is a work item, not a condition of the note (who does it and the proposed date: 'Work that is possible now and still open').
- (5) Decision 4: an acceptance note with the client told, or the audit-writer function proved by a test that a direct INSERT into `audit_entries` by the agent login is refused.
- (6) Decisions 3 and 7 BUILT, not only made: the drain program exists and runs (and, if Decision 2(b) is chosen, the `k1b` login is the only other login that may DELETE from `audit_outbox`, with its own negative test; in production this needs the setting 'this process does not sign', X-5.3 option (i), Decision 5, because `signer: none` is refused today), and the secret-scoping proof passes: the program that runs client hooks is started in its own process or container holding only its own login's secret; the test asserts that no other login's secret name resolves and that its environment and mounted files hold no other login's value; a second test shows a process that names a second login's secret refuses to start in a pilot or production environment. Paper: the raw output of both runs.
- (7) X-6.8 built, proved by a test (the agent login is refused SELECT on `redaction_maps`, the executor login is allowed, the portal login is refused), or accepted by an acceptance note until Decision 8 is done.
- (8) Departures X-6.6a, X-6.6b and X-6.6c: each is closed or covered by an acceptance note: X-6.6a through criterion (5); X-6.6b through Decision 8 built, or a note; X-6.6c through Decision 21 option (a) done (a named rotation lead and an interval written down) or option (b) built, or a note. ENFORCEMENT (proposed, not built; the builder; size unknown, assistant judgement small to medium, not measured): the start-up refusal of the hold sentence, widened from the departures of this criterion to the criteria (1) to (9) (one range, used everywhere in this file; for (8) itself the check covers only the departures X-6.6a to c, because (8)'s own check part is proved by its two start tests): a start-up check for a pilot or production environment that refuses to start unless the config names, for each open criterion, a note file that exists (it cannot judge the note's content; the studio head reads it); and for each criterion claimed met, an evidence file that NAMES THE COMMIT BEING STARTED (the deployed commit) and records a pass (exit code 0, every guard test green); an evidence file for another commit, or without a recorded pass, is refused; and the notes and evidence are BOUND (see EVIDENCE BINDING in criterion (9) and Decision 27) to something only the studio head controls, because a typed 'signature line' is only text (agents never hold approver credentials, and the builder who creates the notes folder and writes this check must not be able to fill a note): proposal, a commit signed by a key only the studio head holds, or the notes folder under CODEOWNERS with the studio head as the only code owner plus a branch rule that requires that review (a repository-settings change needs the studio head's yes; how the host enforces either was not checked; UNVERIFIED); and the stored result of the criterion (9) checks inside `migrate()` is present and green.
