This is the same check as the one proposed under the hold sentence and the table row of (8) (one check, not three); criterion (8) is therefore met only when that check is built and tested, and only its departures may end on notes (WHICH CRITERIA A NOTE CAN CLOSE, the one table the check uses: (1) none; (2) only the X-6.4 part; (3) yes; (4) yes, and for the database-down case only a note exists today; (5) yes; (6) none; (7) yes; (8) only its departures; (9) only the named TEMPORARY and lock departures, and never alone. The check refuses a note for a criterion marked none). Today no code and no file in the repository refuses a pilot configuration, so until the check exists, the hold is kept only by the studio head not sending the proposal. Paper: raw output of one test that starts a pilot config with an open departure and no note (refused) and one with the note (starts).
- (9) OVER-BREADTH PROOF (proposal, not built): `effective_problems` (`migrate.py:193-262`) already reports every right above the grant map on every relation, column and sequence of the schema, for the runtime and purge logins (it is NOT called for the portal or for any new login). Criterion (9) is the six gaps listed in the paragraph headed 'Over-breadth checks still missing' (in 'Departures from the least-privilege rule in the end-state', after the login table), run for every login of the login table that exists (see WHERE THE CHECKS RUN and WHICH LOGINS MUST EXIST below), PLUS a shadowing negative test (a temporary table named like `approval_decisions`, created by the agent login, must not be read by the guards of X-6.3). WHERE THE CHECKS RUN: twice. (1) On a scratch database, to prove the checks work: each check fails on a planted gap (a missing revoke, a TEMPORARY grant, a default-ACL or membership gap) and passes on a clean database. (2) INSIDE `migrate()` on the REAL target database, in the same transaction as the grants, the way `effective_problems` runs today (`migrate.py:195-199`: 'the grants above are made first and this runs in the same transaction, so a problem rolls them back'), because a scratch run proves the check works, not that THIS client database has its revokes done, TEMPORARY removed and no default-ACL or membership gap. `migrate()` stores the result of its last run (a row or a file; not designed; what it holds and who may write it are under THE STORED RESULT below), and the start-up check of criterion (8) reads that result and refuses to start in a pilot or production environment when it is missing, red or older than the last migration (proposal, not built). WHICH LOGINS MUST EXIST before (9) can be claimed: today's three (runtime, portal, purge) plus every login that a built criterion needs: the `operator`, `receiver`, drain and agent/tick logins (criteria (1) and (6) cannot end on a note, so these must exist), the `signer` login under option (i) of Decision 5, the `k1b` login under Decision 2 option (b), and the `verifier` and dead-man reader logins if they are built. A login that is not created because its criterion ended on a note ((3), (4), (5), (7)) is NOT checked as 'nothing found': the check prints the list of logins it ran for, and (9) is claimed only for that list, with the note saying that the old shared login keeps its rights. Paper: raw output of both runs (the scratch run saved by an evidence runner; the run inside `migrate()` on the real target saved by the deployer), BASELINE (Postgres gives PUBLIC, by default, the right to create temporary tables on a database and the right to execute a function, so every login holds both through PUBLIC unless they are revoked; from the Postgres manual, UNVERIFIED, not run here): a check that simply asks 'can this login create temporary tables' or 'can it execute any function' would flag every login on every database, so each check states its expected result. EXPECTED RESULTS, tied to Decisions 9a and 9b. (1) EXECUTE: the check covers the functions of the app schema and an explicit list of named built-in functions, not every built-in; no login may execute a SECURITY DEFINER function it does not own. THE LIST OF NAMED BUILT-IN FUNCTIONS (from the Postgres manual, UNVERIFIED, not run here; each exists in a one-number and a two-number form, 16 signatures in all): `pg_advisory_lock`, `pg_advisory_lock_shared`, `pg_advisory_xact_lock`, `pg_advisory_xact_lock_shared`, `pg_try_advisory_lock`, `pg_try_advisory_lock_shared`, `pg_try_advisory_xact_lock`, `pg_try_advisory_xact_lock_shared`. The unlock functions only release a session's own locks and are left alone (my reading, UNVERIFIED). Only the one-number `pg_advisory_xact_lock` is called in `src` today (`audit/chain.py:120`, key `_PG_LOCK`; `store/migrate.py:314`, a different key `_LOCK`, run under the migration owner's connection; `store/migration_numbers.py:253`, which is printed text that a database administrator runs by hand); the other seven names are revoked too because a hostile session can call any of them to hold a lock, and the session-level and shared forms hold longer. ALLOWLIST (who may execute them under 9b yes; the code needs only one of the eight names): only the one-number `pg_advisory_xact_lock(bigint)` is given back, and only to the migration owner (runs `migrate()`; a stricter revoke would stop every migration; the same grant covers whoever runs the printed recipe by hand) and to the logins that append audit entries through `audit/chain.py:120` (the agent and tick login, the receiver, `operator`, `k1b` and the drain login). The other 15 signatures (the two-number forms, the session-level forms, the shared forms and the try forms) are given to nobody. `signer` is OFF the list from the start: my reading of `checkpoint()` (`chain.py:295-317`) is that it calls no lock function (UNVERIFIED, not run); its positive test shows whether that is right, and if the signing program then fails for a missing lock right, that is a finding to fix, not a reason to widen the list. THE CHECK asserts, for every login it ran for, the EXACT set of the 16 signatures that login may execute (`has_function_privilege` on each signature, for the effective right, plus the ACL query below): the allowlisted logins hold exactly the one signature, every other login holds none. Every other login is refused. With 9b yes the check demands exactly that; with 9b no it demands only that no login holds EXECUTE through a direct grant or a membership beyond the PUBLIC baseline, and the lock departure is recorded in an acceptance note (the cost sentence of Decision 9b copied exactly). THE LIMIT OF THIS CONTROL: the agent and tick login is on the allowlist and runs client hook code that is hostile under T1, so 'a hostile program can no longer hold the audit lock' is NOT claimed for that login; the claim holds only for logins off the list, and for the agent login only after the audit writer is its own login (X-6.4). (2) TEMPORARY: MODE A (9a yes): no login holds it. MODE B (9a no): every login holds it only through PUBLIC (no direct grant, no membership), the departure is recorded in an acceptance note (the cost sentence of Decision 9a copied exactly), every guard of X-6.3 uses schema-qualified names and a pinned `search_path`, and the shadowing test must still pass. In both modes the built checks and the passing shadowing test are required; the notes cover only the named TEMPORARY and lock departures, so criterion (9) still cannot end on a note alone. (3) default privileges, (4) a call for every new login, (5) memberships (including non-inheriting ones) and (6) the schema scope are expected to find nothing. HOW THE ACL IS READ ( the checks above cannot be done with `has_function_privilege` or `has_database_privilege` alone, because those answer 'can it' and not 'through what'; all of this is from the Postgres manual and the reviewer, UNVERIFIED, not run): the stored right lists are `pg_proc.proacl` for functions and `pg_database.datacl` for the database, read with `aclexplode`; a NULL list means the built-in default, which includes PUBLIC (for a function: EXECUTE for PUBLIC; for a database: TEMPORARY and CONNECT for PUBLIC), so a NULL list is reported as 'default, PUBLIC holds it', never as 'nothing'; a right held through a role membership is read with `pg_has_role`. The check leaves out the roles that hold every right anyway (the object owner, superusers, the database owner), because their rights cannot be removed and must not make the check fail; they are listed by name instead. THE PLANTED-GAP TEST must plant a DIRECT grant to the agent login itself (not only a grant to PUBLIC) and show the check fails on it, and then show it passes once the grant is removed. WHO ELSE CAN LOG IN: criterion (9) is refused, not just reported, while a role that can log in (`pg_roles.rolcanlogin`) and is not on the declared list of logins exists on the database, and the database owner (`pg_database.datdba`) is named in the output; otherwise a login nobody declared would never be checked (today `effective_problems` is called only for the named runtime and purge logins, `migrate.py:298-303`). THE STORED RESULT (not designed; proposal): written only by the migration owner login, and holding the commit it ran for, a hash of the grant map, the list of logins it ran for, the mode answers of 9a and 9b, the result, and the time; the start-up check of criterion (8) reads it and refuses a result that is missing, red, older than the last migration, or for another commit or grant-map hash. EVIDENCE BINDING (build and host behaviour UNVERIFIED): the evidence file is bound by a detached signature over three things (the commit hash or tree hash, the criterion number, and the hash of the result), checked against a public key pinned outside the repository; the signing key is a dedicated approval key of the studio head that is not used for routine commits; a file cannot contain its own commit hash, so the commit is named in the signed text, not inside the evidence file; a sole-owner CODEOWNERS rule may deadlock a repository with one person (UNVERIFIED); the running program cannot see CODEOWNERS or commit signatures, so the check can verify the detached signature only. The criterion (8) text and Decisions 26 to 28 carry the choices. DEPENDENCY AND SIZE: the checks of today's three logins and the PUBLIC baseline are about 1 to 2 days (assistant judgement, not measured); the per-login checks of the new logins cannot run until those logins exist and `migrate()` accepts them (it takes three logins today, `migrate.py:283-292`, `:298-303`; the drain parameter is X-5.6 (b); `operator`, `k1b`, drain and `signer` need column grants, but `Grant` has only `purge_cols`, `migrate.py:37`, and `_apply_grants` takes one column callback, `migrate.py:169`), which is the work of Decisions 3, 5 and 7 and is not in the 1 to 2 days. Without criterion (9) a login can hold a right that the grant map does not show (for example EXECUTE on a function, or TEMPORARY on the database), so criteria (1) to (8) could pass while the logins are still too broad.
- ACCEPTANCE NOTE (one wording wherever this file says 'accepted in writing'): a file, in a folder the studio head names (not created yet; action to create it: the studio head names the folder, assistant suggestion `handover/acceptance-notes/`, and the builder creates it with an empty template of these four parts within one working day, so until then no note exists and none counts), holding four parts: the sentence of the 'what accepting costs' cell copied exactly; 'I, the studio head, accept this for <scope: demos only, or this pilot and the client's name or code> until <date>'; the date the client was told and the document that told them; the studio head's own signature line (name and role, no personal handle). A typed signature line is only text: the binding to the studio head is the one named in criterion (8) (a signed commit or CODEOWNERS). A note missing any of the four parts does not count.
- TIMING: the hold ends only when criteria (1) to (9) are met, so every build they name must be finished before the proposal is sent; that is why the table gives 'before the proposal is sent' as the trigger of Decisions 2, 3, 4 (note), 5, 7, 10, 12, 14 and 21 (assistant suggestion). Alternative that needs the studio head's yes and changes the hold sentence (option 2 of the block of Decision 1): split the hold into a proposal hold (acceptance notes plus a written statement in the proposal of which criteria are open) and a pilot hold (all nine). Not applied. Cost of letting both go ahead before then: the proposal cannot truthfully promise that agents never hold approver or executor rights, or that mistakes cannot cause damage.
- Recommended default (assistant suggestion): keep the hold until criteria (1) to (9) are met; demos on made-up data go on.
2. **X-4: who empties the outbox.** Options: (a) un-defer A-01 (the lead deferred it by default 4 of 2026-11-16; the studio head may veto that default; option (b) un-defers it too): the tick drain step drains the outbox every tick and nothing else changes (X-2 part 1 and A-6 first; size small to medium; interim: Decision 3 later moves the drain into the drain program and switches this step off, see 'drain' in 'Words and scales'); (b) the same drain in a small tick step of our own AND a `reconcile()` call at the end of every short-lived K1b command (`replay`, `score`; NOT the operator command, because option (b) needs outbox DELETE and the `operator` login has none), so rows that a K1b command leaves in the outbox are drained when it exits; that gives the new `k1b` login of the login table the outbox right DELETE, with its own negative test, and makes it a second drain login (size medium); X-5.1 and criterion (6) already name `k1b` for option (b); a K1b command parks an event in the outbox on one path only (the failure of the score report, `cli.py:262-270`; `emit` raises and never parks, `k1b/audit_sink.py:31-35`; replay never parks), so the `k1b` rights rest on that one path and (b) drains only when a K1b command happens to run; and (b) needs the studio head's answer to question 5-K of Decision 5 (does the `k1b` login sign checkpoints; see the `k1b` row of the login table for both alternatives, K-A and K-B); the only difference from (a) is that extra call at the end of each command (the K1b command line needs a `K1bAudit.reconcile()` that calls its sink's; today `K1bAudit` has none, and its docstring at `audit_sink.py:38` only says the SINK keeps a failed event 'for `reconcile`', which is true of the sink and promises nothing about a K1b command: row A-2); (c) accept it. In one plain sentence each: (a) a scheduled job empties the waiting list of audit events into the real audit record on every tick; (b) the same, and every short command-line command also empties the waiting list as it finishes; (c) nobody empties it, so events wait until a person runs the emptying command by hand. `K1bAudit` is the small wrapper (`k1b/audit_sink.py:26`) that the command-line replay tools put around an audit sink. Cost of (c): audit events that reach the outbox wait for a hand call, with no log line or red step; an event written while the database is down is lost at exit, and options (a) and (b) narrow but do not close that case (it needs a durable fallback that does not use the database; only the outline below exists, unreviewed and unbuilt). Recommended default (assistant suggestion): keep A-01 deferred for the demo and the video; decide (a) before the proposal is sent (the one trigger time of Decision 2), and review the database-down fallback and build it after X-2 part 1, whose outline follows. DATABASE-DOWN FALLBACK, DESIGN OUTLINE (written by the assistant; not reviewed, not built, nothing run; UNVERIFIED). IT IS A NEW, UNREVIEWED WRITE PATH: by the 'write path' test in 'Words and scales' a folder that any audit-writing process can write to is a way to write audit data that today's grants do not give, so it must pass the same test as A-01 (X-2: one login, a per-action reason, a negative test) BEFORE it counts as a way to satisfy criterion (4), and it is therefore ordered AFTER X-2 part 1; until then criterion (4) can end only on the acceptance note. OPEN QUESTIONS the review must answer, none answered here: which account is the folder owner; which processes may WRITE to it (the agent and tick run client hook code in the same process, so any of that code could plant files); which may READ it (the drain only?); the file mode; a size or count cap (a full disk must not stop the program, and an unbounded folder is a denial of service); who removes files and when; and how a planted file with a chosen `event_id` is told from a real one (the same problem as X-5.2 (ii), because the files are not hash-chained until drained). The proposed date below is for the DESIGN to reach review, not for it to be reviewed. THE FALLBACK ITSELF: when both the audit record and the outbox table fail, write the event to a spool folder on the local disk of the process BEFORE it is held only in memory (`audit/chain.py:229`): one file per event, written to a temporary file, flushed to disk, then hard-linked into place so a retry makes no second file (the technique described at `audit/local_dir.py:1-4`, read); a later drain reads the spool, chains each event and deletes the file; a repeated `event_id` is treated as already written (`audit/chain.py:219-220`; whether the chain append refuses a repeated id was not re-checked). Limits stated plainly: the folder must be on a disk that survives a restart (a mounted volume, the deployer's job) and if that disk is lost too the event is lost; the files hold audit events that are already masked; only the drain and the writer may touch the folder; the existing local-directory sink is refused in a pilot or production environment (`DEMO_ONLY`, `backends/table.py:593-601`), so this is a new mechanism, not that kind. It needs a negative test (a process without the folder right cannot read the spool) and a crash test (take the database down, write one event, restart, assert one chain row once the database is back). Size: medium, about 2 to 3 days of agent work, not measured; who does it: the builder; proposed date for the design to reach review: the end of 2026-11-18 UTC (05:30 IST on 2026-11-19).
3. **X-5.1: a separate drain login.** Cost of accepting: the agent's login can delete pending audit events with no trace. PREREQUISITE (same as X-5.1 and Decision 5): in production a drain built without a signer is refused today (`config.py:419-420`), so Decision 3 needs the setting 'this process does not sign' of Decision 29 first. Recommended default (assistant suggestion): yes, but only together with running the drain program (a drain inside the tick it protects nothing), with Decision 7 and with Decision 5.
4. **X-5.2 and X-6.4: forged and planted audit rows.** Accept in writing that the runtime login keeps INSERT on the audit tables (after a split, every login that writes audit events), or design one audit-writer function in the database. Cost of accepting: false audit rows can be appended and, narrowly, a real event's content replaced. Cost for a pilot: the same, on a client's real data (a false audit row on real work). Recommended default (assistant suggestion): accept in writing for demos; for a pilot only through exit criterion (5) of Decision 1; design the function before production.
5. **X-5.3: who may write checkpoint rows.** QUESTION 5-K (studio head; also needed for the `k1b` row of the login table): do the K1b commands sign checkpoints themselves (alternative K-A: they hold a signer secret and may INSERT into `audit_checkpoints`) or not (alternative K-B: they use the setting 'this process does not sign' and the signing process signs for them)? Cost of no answer: the `k1b` row cannot be finished and Decision 2(b) cannot be built. Options for the main question: (i) a separate signing process and login; (ii) count only valid rows; (iii) a foreign key `(seq, hash)` as a weak second guard; (iv) a `(seq, signature)` key. In one plain sentence each: (i) only a separate program that holds the signing key may write the signed-head rows, so no other program can write a fake one (under K-A the `k1b` program holds a signing key too, so 'no other program' then means 'no program except `signer` and `k1b`'); (ii) the program that looks for the last signed head ignores any row whose signature does not check out; (iii) the database refuses a signed-head row that does not match a real audit entry (a weak extra guard); (iv) junk rows may sit in the table and the program trusts only the row with a valid signature. Option (i) also needs the setting 'this process does not sign' (X-5.3), which Decisions 3 and 10 need too. Cost of accepting: one junk row silences checkpoint signing with no error. Recommended default (assistant suggestion): (i), with (iii) added later.
6. **X-5.4: lock and statement timeouts.** In one plain sentence: the database cancels any audit write that waits too long for a lock or runs too long, so one stuck program cannot freeze every audit write; it guards against accidents only. Cost of accepting: a held audit lock hangs every audit write. These settings protect against accidents only. Recommended default (assistant suggestion): set them together with Decision 2(a) or 2(b), never before a drain runs.
7. **X-6.6: split the one login into separate logins, or accept the shared login in writing.** Includes the prerequisite per-process secret scoping (not in this repository; size large; the studio head decides, our code builds it, the deployer sets up separate processes and secret mounts). Cost of accepting: kill switch off, spend zeroed, self-approval, silently (X-6.1 to X-6.3). Recommended default (assistant suggestion): build per-process secret scoping FIRST (a split without it protects nothing, X-6.6); then split in two releases (A adds, B narrows), starting with `operator` and `receiver`.
8. **X-6.6: move ALLOW-class execution from the agent loop (`agent_loop.py:768`) to the tick,** so that agent and tick can be separate logins. Cost: ALLOW-class actions wait up to one tick interval. Cost of not doing it: agent and tick stay one login that runs the client's hooks, so the end-state does not meet the rule 'agents never hold executor credentials' and X-6.8 stays open. Recommended default (assistant suggestion): yes if Decision 7 is yes; otherwise agent and tick stay one login and the departure from the rule is accepted in writing.
9. **X-6.5 (finding) and Decisions 9a and 9b: database-level revokes from PUBLIC ).** In one plain sentence each: (9a) take away from every program except the migration owner the right to create short-lived scratch tables in the database (TEMPORARY), because a scratch table with a real table's name could be read instead of the real one; (9b) take away from every program the right to run the advisory-lock functions (the lock whose hold stops every audit write, X-5.4; the full list of eight functions is in criterion (9)) and give back only `pg_advisory_xact_lock(bigint)` to the migration owner and the logins that append audit entries (the agent and tick login, receiver, `operator`, `k1b`, drain); `signer` is not on the list from the start. Postgres gives PUBLIC both rights by default (manual, UNVERIFIED, not run). Both are deployment actions by the deployer; 9a may refuse scratch tables to other tools on that database; 9b breaks every audit writer that is not on the list, so it needs the positive tests of the login table first (wave 3) and probably a superuser (UNVERIFIED). The four combinations of answers, the setting that records them (no default) and who does the revoke are in the plain block 'Decision 9, both parts'. Cost of accepting (not doing 9a): a temporary table can shadow a real table for any login that may create one, so the guards proposed in X-6.3 (the finding is X-6.5) depend on schema-qualified names and a pinned `search_path` being right everywhere (one miss and the guard is bypassed). Cost of accepting (not doing 9b): any login can hold the audit lock (X-5.4). Even with 9b yes, the agent and tick login stays on the list, so a hostile agent can still hold the lock until the audit writer is its own login (X-6.4). WHAT 'NO' DOES TO CRITERION (9): 9a no gives mode B for TEMPORARY and 9b no gives mode B for EXECUTE (each needs an acceptance note). Recommended default (assistant suggestion): 9a yes where only our app uses the database, 9b yes only after the positive tests of every login on the list pass; elsewhere mode B with the notes; each deployment records its own answers.
10. **X-6.3: does the first release already take INSERT on approval decisions from the agent login?** PREREQUISITE (same as X-6.6 and Decision 5): the receiver is its own process that holds no signer, and in production `signer: none` is refused for every process today (`config.py:419-420`), so this needs the setting 'this process does not sign' of Decision 29 and the receiver's own secret (Decision 7). Cost of not doing it: the first release gives no protection against self-approval; cost of doing it: approvals stop until the receiver has its own connection string. Recommended default (assistant suggestion): yes.
11. **X-6.3: a column-level UPDATE (status only) for the receiver in the same release.** In one plain sentence: the receiver may change only the status column of an approval request, not its other columns (such as what the approval points at). Needs the two update builders to set only the changed columns and `Grant` to carry a column list. Cost of accepting (no column-level UPDATE): the receiver login keeps UPDATE on every column of `approval_requests`, so a hijacked receiver can change what an approval points at, and the database guard of X-6.3 is the only limit. Recommended default (assistant suggestion): yes.
12. **X-6.4: where the approved restored values are bound,** in a place the agent cannot write (for example a frozen digest: a fingerprint of the approved values, stored in the approval request, which `execute` computes again and compares before it runs). Cost of accepting: a forged `gate.decide` record with a matching digest lets changed values run. Recommended default (assistant suggestion): the frozen digest (no outside source; my judgement from the rows).
13. **X-6.4: who closes the agent-written approval summary and approver list:** nobody (written down) or the receiver rebuilds them itself from the stored plan (recomputes them) instead of trusting the agent's text. Cost of accepting ('nobody'): the summary and approver list the approver sees are written by the agent, so a hijacked agent can show an approver a text that differs from the stored plan. Recommended default (assistant suggestion): recompute.
14. **X-6.2: spend option A (a database rule, called a trigger, that refuses any lowering of the spend counters and any change of their key columns) or option B (store each spend as an added row and add the rows up, so nothing is overwritten).** Cost of accepting: spend counters can be zeroed. Recommended default (assistant suggestion): A first (no outside source; my judgement from the rows).
15. **X-6.7: timing of the narrowing release.** Cost of narrowing too early: a lock-out. Recommended default (assistant suggestion): after one end-to-end proof on a scratch database, and only once the logins' secrets exist.
21. **Credential lifetime and rotation (X-6.6, departure X-6.6c).** Today each login is one static connection string, so it is long-lived, which breaks item 2 of the least-privilege rule (short-lived credentials only). Options: (a) name a rotation lead (the studio head names him; he may name the deployer once Decision 25 names one, or another person; there is no default person) and an interval for changing each login's secret, as a stop-gap; (b) build short-lived credentials: the existing cloud sign-in (`store/auth_aws.py`, `iam_auth: true`) for the first client on that cloud, or, neutral, a rotating secret from the client's own secrets manager through the secrets kind. Cost of accepting today's state: a stolen connection string works until someone changes it. Recommended default (assistant suggestion): (a) now with an interval the studio head sets (assistant suggestion: 90 days; no source for that number and no measurement), (b) at the first pilot.
22. **Breakglass, CI deploy and human identities (X-6.6, departure X-6.6d).** Decide whether a NEW design for these three identities is written before a pilot. They are NOT designed from `../ROLES-PROPOSAL.md`. That file (434 lines; parked as 'client build / after the video' by lead default 3 of 2026-11-16) proposes only five database roles for the lead-intake table `mf_leads` (`mf_tableowner`, which nobody logs in as, plus the four logins `mf_door`, `mf_sub`, `mf_recover`, `mf_alert`; its table at lines 67-71, which I read). Its line 400 says a human read-only login and a backup login are NOT covered, and my search found no occurrence of 'breakglass', 'break-glass', 'passkey', 'CI deploy' or 'OIDC' in it. So breakglass, CI deploy and the human roles have NO source document and a new design is needed. Its names also do not overlap the core logins of the login table (agent, tick, receiver, `operator`, drain, signer, dead-man reader, portal, purge): it concerns the n8n flows, not the core store. Whether to un-park it is a separate question about the flow logins. Size of the new design: about 3 hours of agent time, borrowed from `../reviews/roles-proposal-summary.md` (that figure is for the five other roles, so it is an order of magnitude only; UNVERIFIED, not measured). Cost of accepting: the end-state table is incomplete against the least-privilege rule (no emergency account design, no deploy identity, no human roles), and the proposal must not claim it is complete (X-6.6d). Recommended default (assistant suggestion): write the new design before the first pilot, not before the video.
25. **Who is the deployer.** The deployer is the person who sets up separate programs and secret stores at a client or on the studio's machines (defined in 'Words and scales'). No file I searched (`handover/`, `.claude/`) names him, so the deployer part of Decisions 3, 5, 7, 9, 10, 15 and 21 has no named doer, Decision 7's deployer part cannot be sized, and Decision 21's rotation lead has no default. Readings, not facts: on the studio's own machines the only person the repository describes is the studio head (a one-person studio); at a client it may be a client person; unknown: studio head answers. Cost of no answer: criteria (1), (6) and (8) have a part with no doer and no end date. Recommended default (assistant suggestion): none; this is a fact only the studio head knows.

### Matter only if a patch is applied, or concern the rating
16. **The `max_tokens` ceiling number (C-2, X-1).** Cost of accepting: none today (the ceiling is cut); if it returns with a wrong number, a valid call is blocked or a ten-times typo passes. Recommended default (assistant suggestion): leave it cut.
17. **A-3: the return contract** (change `AuditSink.reconcile()`, or add a separate `pending_count()`); only if A-01 returns. Cost of no decision: a stuck backlog shows as nothing. Recommended default (assistant suggestion): the separate `pending_count()`.
18. **C-01: shape (answer Question Q1 below first).** Price-only (with Q1 yes: inside `Config` or where cost is computed; with Q1 no: where cost is computed only), rebased by one context line, or drop the patch. The per-row guard of A-6 is not part of this decision (it belongs to A-01, Decision 2). Cost of no decision: no price check exists, and a zero price still disables every spend cap for a route built outside `load_config`. Recommended default (assistant suggestion): price-only, placed where cost is computed and charged (C-3, ONE PLACEMENT: one function called at `llm/common.py:166-169` (the multiplication is at 167-168, the division at 169), `llm/fake.py:51-52`, the demo's reserve and `k1b/replay.py`, plus `Limits.charge` refusing a negative amount, which is the same change as C-7), with the config-load check kept only as an optional early warning; the A-6 guard is a precondition of A-01, not of C-01.
19. **C-8: the price of a self-hosted model.** Accept that only kind `fake` may cost nothing and a self-hosted model gets a positive notional price (an "unmetered" flag would reopen the zero-price hole for hosted endpoints of the same kind). The number is the studio head's. If no: the design as written refuses a keyless local-model config; a zero price reaches a hosted endpoint only if the studio head asks for the exemption to be widened. The number is collected in the plain block of Decision 19 (a number, or 'the builder proposes and I confirm'). Recommended default (assistant suggestion): accept the rule.
20. **The Major rating rule** (see 'Words and scales'). Options, with the counts the script would print as Major / Minor / Nit: (1) keep it as written (3 present and 1 conditional Major): 4 / 13 / 11; (2) 'a conditional or deferred row is Minor' (X-2 becomes Minor): 3 / 14 / 11; (3) 'any gap in a deferred patch is Major' (A-2 and A-3 become Major): 6 / 11 / 11; (4) 'a hostile model endpoint is in the threat model' (T2: only C-7 becomes Major, because an endpoint or a recording supplies token counts while the route price comes from the config file, so C-3 stays Minor): 5 / 12 / 11; (5) 'a defect of today's code that breaks the least-privilege rule is a Blocker' (by that wording EVERY Major part of X-5 and X-6 becomes Blocker in the word on it: X-5.1, X-5.2, X-5.3, X-6.1, X-6.2, X-6.3, X-6.4, X-6.6 and X-6.8, which is 9 parts (the three Major departures X-6.6a to X-6.6c are inside X-6.6, not extra; X-6.6d is Minor and keeps its word); X-4 stays Major because it loses a record and breaks no right; the counted rows stay Major in the count, only the word on them changes, and the hold sentence already has that effect): 4 / 13 / 11; (6) 'a money limit that one bad number switches off is Major' (C-3 and C-7 both become Major; this is NOT T2): 6 / 11 / 11. The conflict of earlier text (6 Major in one place, 5 in another, both called the T2 choice) is settled: T2 alone gives 5, and rating both money-limit rows Major gives 6 and is option 6. Counts for two options at once are not computed. Cost of keeping the rule: two Minor rows on a deferred patch can read as less urgent if A-01 returns, and C-3 (a placement flaw, now Minor) can be missed; both stay in the list of fixes to apply first. Recommended default (assistant suggestion): keep.

26. **Decisions 26 to 29: the notes folder, the binding, the start-up refusal and the 'does not sign' setting.** The earlier text said 'the studio head names the folder', 'bound to the studio head', 'a start-up check' and 'the setting this process does not sign' without a question for each, so no answer could be given. Each is now a question with its own block and table row: 26 the folder name; 27 how notes and evidence are bound (detached signature, or CODEOWNERS with a branch rule; host behaviour UNVERIFIED); 28 whether a start-up refusal is wanted; 29 whether the setting 'this process does not sign' is built, and its size (assistant judgement, not measured). Cost of no answer: no note counts, so no criterion can close on a note, and the hold stays.

23. **`G5-wording.patch`: two separate questions, 23a and 23b.** BACKGROUND: the original patch carries a personal repository handle in two context lines (the handle is not printed in this file). **23a: may a cleaned copy be kept in the repository?** (Why it is asked: the patch text carries the handle in two context lines; a cleaned copy would not show it.) It is not copied in the current tree; a cleaned copy would no longer apply to b4cce5b. Cost of no: whoever applies G5 rebuilds the patch from this checkout. Recommended default (assistant suggestion): no copy; rebuild from the checkout when G5 is applied. Who: the assistant, once answered. **23b: the handle that is already in the branch history.** Earlier text said 'the G5 patch is not copied' without limit; that is true for the current tree only. A fixer copied the file, it was committed (commit `34540bfa2`) and pushed to the branch `cloud/flows-audit`, and then removed from the tree; the commits that hold it remain in the branch history, so the handle is still readable there (`../reviews/incident-g5-patch-copy.md`). Options: (1) REWRITE the branch history so that the file never existed in it; (2) ACCEPT the exposure in writing. WHO REWRITES, WHICH BRANCH, WHEN: the assistant that edits this file will not rewrite a shared branch on its own; NEEDS THE STUDIO HEAD to name who does it (himself, or the lead if he hands it over: they are different people, see 'Who is who' in 'Words and scales'); the branch is `cloud/flows-audit`; proposed time (assistant suggestion): before any pull request is opened from that branch, because a rewrite is cheapest while nobody has built on it. WHAT A REWRITE DOES: every commit from `34540bfa2` to the tip of the branch gets a new commit id (commits before it keep theirs); anyone who already fetched the branch must fetch it again, and any file that quotes a later commit id goes stale (I did not search for such quotes). WHAT 'ACCEPT IN WRITING' MEANS here: an acceptance note in the four-part format of Decision 1 (the cost sentence below copied exactly; the studio head's acceptance line with scope and date; the date on which, and the way in which, everyone who may hold a copy was told; the signature line), naming commit `34540bfa2`. Cost of accepting: a personal repository handle stays readable in the history of a repository that may be shared. Recommended default for 23b (assistant suggestion): rewrite (option 1) before the first pull request is opened from the branch; if the branch has already been fetched by other people, accept in writing instead. Housekeeping, not an exit criterion.

Cost of no answer: none today (the licence test passes, see 'Update: full-repository run'). Recommended default (assistant suggestion): item 11 wins; no LICENSE file is changed.

WHO CHECKS THE FACT: the builder, by one search that takes minutes, before Decision 18 is asked: open the take's model program (defined in 'Words and scales') and search it for `load_config(` and `Route(`; if it calls `load_config`, the price check applies to the take; if it builds `Route(...)` in code, it does not, the patch is not applied as written, and Decision 18 chooses between a check where cost is computed (C-3) and dropping the patch (the ONE STATEMENT in 'Words and scales'). MISSING INPUT: the path of the take's model program; no file I read names it, so I did not do the search. The studio head confirms the reading of the line (and may veto the lead's default itself). ## Plain summary (read this first)
1. What this is: a review of three ready-made changes ("patches") for the Hadavin core code library, plus problems found in today's code that no patch touches. The patches are C-01 (refuses a model price of zero or less), A-01 (a scheduled step that empties the waiting list of audit records) and G5-wording (comment and text changes only). Rows named C-3, X-5 and so on are the findings, listed in the index below.
2. C-01: do not apply the file as written. Keep only the price check, and only after the studio head decides where that one check lives (C-3). It also refuses a self-hosted model priced at zero; the only exemption is the recorded fake model, so such a model needs a positive stand-in price (C-8).
3. A-01: the lead deferred it. If it returns, fix X-2 (which login runs the new step) and the Minor rows on it first, and give it a guard against one bad row (A-6).
4. G5-wording: apply after one wording fix (G-1) and a small rebase, because one of its three files no longer applies to the current checkout.
5. Three serious problems exist in today's code and need no patch. X-4: nothing in this repository ever empties the audit waiting list, and a record written while the database is down is lost when the program exits. X-5: the database login the agent runs under can delete, forge and block audit records. X-6: that same login can switch off the emergency stop, set its spend counters to zero and approve its own requests.
6. Blocker for the proposal and for pilot and production: 2 (X-5, X-6), because they break the studio rule that agents never hold approver or executor credentials. The table below counts 0 Blocker only because it uses the reviewers' scale; both lines are recorded and the studio head may overrule the scale (choice (6) at the end).
7. Counts after this round (by script, Counts section): 28 rows, 0 Blocker on the table scale, 5 Major, 12 Minor, 11 Nit.
8. Rating rule used for every row (see 'Rating rule'): Major means a shown defect or a broken written studio rule, never a guess.
9. What was run: only `git apply --check`, `py_compile`, greps and line counts. No test, lint or Postgres statement was run, so every statement about Postgres or about how a test behaves is UNVERIFIED unless it says it was read from source.
10. The studio head decides nine things, listed with what accepting today's state costs under 'Open for the studio head'; two matter for today's code (X-5 and X-6).


Old 'Labels used in this file':
## Labels used in this file
- Round N: the Nth review round of this file; round 16 is the latest. Each round has two reviewers: the design reviewer (`review-design-rN.md`) and the codebase reviewer (`review-codebase-rN.md`). Their points are numbered inside those files: F1, F2 (findings), B1 (a Blocker), M1 to M7 (Majors), Nit 3, fact 5 (a confirmed fact). R1-05 and similar are round-1 findings. Reviewer words are unverified until the row says I opened the source.
- (a), (b), (c), (i): sub-points inside one row. Choice (5)(g): sub-choice (g) of numbered choice (5) under 'Open for the studio head'.
- src: a line of this checkout, opened by me. Patched copy: a line of `/tmp/core-b4p` (commit b4cce5b plus the patches). Three line bases (A, B, C) are explained under 'Inputs read'.
- UNVERIFIED: not run and not checked against a real database; read from source or from reviewer knowledge only.
- The round-by-round process log, and old counts, are under 'History' at the end; the log of round 16 is in `fix-review12.md`, section 'Round 16'.

Old 'Rating rule':
## Rating rule (applied to every row since round 16)
A row is rated Major only if it is (a) a defect SHOWN by a cited source line or a run, which loses a record or lets someone get around a money limit, an approval, the emergency stop or tamper evidence, in today's code or in the text of a patch someone may apply; or (b) a broken written studio rule (for example hard rule 11, least privilege). A risk that needs a producer or sink nobody showed, a missing test, or a missing log line is Minor at most. A patch being deferred only decides whether its row is counted as live; it does not change the rating. Each Major row says what is SHOWN and what is NOT shown. Applied: C-3 (a, shown placement flaw), X-2 (b), X-4 (a, shown missing caller and read loss path), X-5 and X-6 (a and b). A-2 and A-3 do not meet (a) or (b) and are Minor. If the studio head prefers 'any gap in a deferred patch is Major', A-2 and A-3 go back to Major (7 Majors); that is choice (9).

Old 'Verdicts by patch' (including the old counts paragraph):
## Verdicts by patch, and problems in today's code (details)
- Price check (C-01): DO NOT APPLY THE FILE AS WRITTEN (round 15). The file has four parts: (1) the hunk in `config.py` at `Route.max_tokens` (`le=defaults.MAX_ROUTE_TOKENS`) and (2) the hunk in `defaults.py` (`MAX_ROUTE_TOKENS = 200_000`, a guessed number): both are the ceiling the lead cut, remove both; (3) the price check inside `Config` (config.py hunk 2): keep only after the choke-point decision in C-3, because a `Route(...)` built directly never reaches `Config` (the demo, `fixtures.py`, `tests/contracts/kinds/builtin.py`); it does not apply to this checkout as shipped (exit 1) and needs one context line cut (`-C1`, exit 0); (4) `tests/test_c01_route_limits.py`: keep the price tests (the six price cases, `test_a_priced_live_route_still_loads`, `test_the_fake_model_may_cost_nothing`), remove the two ceiling tests (`test_max_tokens_above_the_ceiling_is_refused`, `test_max_tokens_at_the_ceiling_loads_and_zero_is_still_refused`) and the `defaults` import with them. The price-only version applies only if the take's model program loads Route config (not verified; not in the video path). The lead cut the extra token ceiling, so that part (C-2) is moot. Of the 13 new tests that fail on the old code, only the 6 price tests belong to work that stays in; the other 7 belong to the deferred A-01 (5) or the cut ceiling (2). ROUND 16: hunk 2 also refuses a self-hosted model priced at zero (row C-8): the only exemption is the kind `fake`, and such a model needs a positive notional price.
- Outbox step (A-01): deferred by the lead (decision 2026-11-16 item 4). If it is ever applied: apply after fixes (X-2, the one Major, for the new step; A-2 and A-3 are Minor under the rating rule since round 16, A-1 and A-5 since round 15, reasons in their rows; A-6 is a Minor, but its per-row guard becomes a PRECONDITION of applying A-01 (round 15): once the step runs every tick, one stuck row delays every row after it on every tick, loudly on the chain kind and silently on the local-directory kind, though no audit event is lost).
- Wording patch (G5): apply after fixes (one wording finding, G-1, re-rated Minor in round 15: the new header may understate the protection that exists, its strength was not compared; the lead kept the patch, text only); its test-file hunk needs a rebase on this checkout (exit 1). DEAD-MAN-CHECK.md: keep as a note; corrections in D-1, D-2 and D-3 (text only).
- Problems in today's code that need no patch (read from source, not run). X-4: nothing in this repository calls the audit `reconcile()`, so audit records that reached the outbox wait there until someone calls it by hand, and a record written while the database is down is lost when the program exits. X-5: the database login the agent runs under can delete waiting audit records, add fake rows to the outbox and the audit chain, add a fake signing checkpoint that stops signing for good, hold the audit lock so every audit write waits, and add a row that cannot be read, which stops a chosen run; a separate login for the drain fixes only the first of these. X-6: one database login serves the agent, the tick and the approval receiver, so the agent's identity can switch off its own kill switch, set its spend counters to zero, add approval decisions, and set an approval request to APPROVED directly, after which the tick runs the plan. The guard proposed earlier for that last hole must NOT be built as written (a temporary table or function can forge what the guard reads; part X-6.5); X-6 stays PROPOSE FIX and its parts are in the table X-6.1 to X-6.7. Nothing was built or tested.
- A missing guard, not a live defect: C-7. A negative token count or a negative usd amount would move spend down. No known source produces one (see the row), so it is counted apart from the live problems; it does not depend on the Route-config condition and is not part of patch C-01. G-1 (the G5 patch's new header says nothing re-scans text before it leaves; the approval channel does run one final redaction pass, so the header may understate the protection, whose strength was not compared) exists only once the G5 patch is applied. C-7 is rated Minor since round 15.
Counts (by script, see the Counts section): 28 rows, 0 Blocker, 5 Major, 12 Minor, 11 Nit. BLOCKER FOR THE PROPOSAL AND FOR PILOT AND PRODUCTION: 2 (X-5, X-6). The two lines do not disagree: the table counts 0 Blocker because it uses the reviewers' scale (a Blocker is a defect in today's source or in a patch that must be fixed before anything is applied), and the second line uses the studio rule about credentials; both are recorded and the studio head may overrule the choice of scale (choice (6)). Reason for the second line: X-5 and X-6 break the studio rule 'agents never hold approver or executor credentials' (least privilege, items 1 and 3): the one shared runtime database login can approve, switch off the kill switch, set the spend counters to zero and forge audit rows, so a sales proposal that promises 'mistakes cannot cause damage' cannot be sent, and no pilot or production build may ship, while that login stays shared. For demos on made-up data the same two rows stay Major. Majors: X-4, X-5, X-6 are live in today's code (3); C-3 counts only if the take's model program loads Route config (1); X-2 returns only if A-01 returns (1): 3 + 1 + 1 = 5. Round 16 changes: A-2 and A-3 Major to Minor (rating rule), one row added (C-8, Minor): 27 rows became 28, Major 7 became 5, Minor 9 became 12, PROPOSE FIX 24 became 25. Choices for the studio head are listed at the end, each with what accepting today's state costs: nine choices, of which only two matter for today's code (X-5 and X-6; A-2 and A-3 matter only if A-01 returns, C-2 only if the ceiling returns).

Old first bullet of 'What the evidence shows':
- Restated in round 12 (codebase reviewer M2, confirmed against evidence-13.md:23-24, :43-58, :63-73, :99): of the 13 new tests that fail on the old code, only the 6 price cases belong to work that stays in (the C-01 price check); the other 7 belong to the deferred A-01 (5 tests) or the cut token ceiling (2 tests, which fail with `AttributeError: MAX_ROUTE_TOKENS` at setup, evidence-13.md:43-58, not by behaviour). The failure reason is shown for the A-01 list test only (:63-73); the A-01 direct-call tests still passed with the step removed (mutation (c), :99). So read the next bullet as history: Confirms for 13 new tests: they fail on the old code (evidence-13.md line 23-24: 15 failed, 55 passed on old source. Split of the 15 (round 7, names read from evidence-13.md lines 23-24): 13 patch-attributable new tests (C-01: 6 price cases + 2 ceiling cases = 8; A-01: 5 new tests) plus 2 others that are not new tests of a patch's own logic: the CHANGED existing test `test_p7_tick::test_steps_order_and_summary_line_is_not_a_step` and `test_p7_tick::test_slack_app_manifest_is_least_privilege` (the manifest test, which also fails on the patched tree because a docs file is missing in the scratch tree, see below, so it does not prove the patch); the test file test_c01_route_limits.py holds 10 new tests, of which 8 fail on old code and the rest pass on both trees as guards (fake kind, a ceiling-sized load; the codebase reviewer counted three such guards, the evidence names two, not itemised), and A-01 adds 5 new tests that all fail on old code; plus 1 changed existing test (the step-order test in test_p7_tick) and 1 manifest test that also fails on the patched tree because a docs file is missing in the scratch tree). The raw output shows the failure REASON for only 3 groups: price cases (DID NOT RAISE ConfigError), the two ceiling cases (no attribute MAX_ROUTE_TOKENS) and the step-list tests (step missing from the list). The reasons for the other A-01 tests are not shown in the output. By reading `A-01.patch` (round 16): the drain test (line 145) and the skip test (line 159) call `tick.audit_reconcile`, which does not exist on the old code; the two log tests (lines 165 and 175) call `ChainAudit.write_outcome`, which does exist, and fail on the old code because the log line is missing. That is my reading of the patch, not evidence output. `ruff check`, `ruff format --check` and `bandit -ll` return 0 on the patched tree. All three patches apply alone and in order on the b4cce5b copies (the runner's claim; I re-ran `git apply --check` on b4cce5b in round 15 with the same result; on the current checkout C-01 and G5 do not apply as shipped, see Patch bases). Mutation checks: removing the price check, dropping the output price, changing `le` to `lt`, removing the ceiling and removing the step from STEPS were each caught by a test.

Old 'Open for the studio head' (nine choices):
Nine choices (five from earlier rounds; (6) and (7) added in round 15; (8) and (9) added in round 16), of which two are live today: (4) X-5 and (5) X-6. Choice (1) matters only if the ceiling returns, and choices (2) and (3) only if A-01 is un-deferred (this agrees with 'Verdicts by patch'; the earlier 'four are live' was wrong). For each: what to decide, and what accepting the current state costs. (1) The ceiling number for max_tokens (C-2). Cost of accepting: none today (the ceiling is cut); if it returns with a wrong number, a valid call is blocked or a 10x typo passes. (2) Whether K1bAudit gets a `reconcile()` (A-2; it does not block the tick-side call, which uses the tick's own `app.audit`). Cost of accepting: events the K1b command line leaves in its outbox wait for a hand call; matters only if A-01 is un-deferred. (3) The return contract for A-3 (change `AuditSink.reconcile()`, or add a separate `pending_count()`). Cost of accepting no choice: the tick cannot say how many events wait, so a stuck backlog shows as nothing; matters only with A-01. (4) X-5, a separate database identity for the audit drain, or accepted over-breadth with the lead's yes. Cost of accepting the current state: the agent identity can delete pending audit events, plant outbox and chain rows, plant a checkpoint row that stops signing, hold the audit lock so every audit write hangs, and make reads of a chosen run fail. The drain identity fixes only the first of those five problems (the DELETE); the other four need the further steps in (c), (f) and (h). Also inside it (round 12; round 13 lists the minimum rules for the function in X-6, AUDIT WRITES): whether every identity keeps INSERT on the audit tables (accepted over-breadth, written down) or one audit-writer function is designed (X-6, AUDIT WRITES). Sub-choices inside it: who may INSERT checkpoint rows (option (i): a separate signing process and credential; (ii): not cheap and does not close the head-seq case; (iii): the foreign key, proposed as a second guard; or the `(seq, signature)` key), and whether the connections get lock and statement timeouts, which turns a hang into unbounded outbox growth while nothing drains the outbox (X-4), so only with a drain running. (5) X-6, the split of the one runtime database role into the identities of the table in the row, or the one shared role accepted with the lead's yes in writing; inside it (round 12): whether ALLOW-class execution moves from the agent loop to the tick (`agent_loop.py:768`), so that the two can be separate identities at all. Round 13 sub-choices: (a) the three-part fix for the temporary-table bypass (schema-qualified names or a pinned search path, the revoke of TEMPORARY, the check in `effective_problems`): the first and third parts are code in our own repository; the studio head decides only the database-level `REVOKE`, which needs the database owner or a superuser and may refuse temporary tables to any other tool on that database (not checked); (b) who closes the agent-written approval summary and approver list (X-6, INSERT rule): nobody, as accepted over-breadth written down, or the receiver recomputes them from the stored plan (needs SELECT on `plans` for the receiver and the detector that `summary()` uses; not checked); (c) whether a flaw found in a proposed fix counts as a Blocker in the table (the scale used in this round is in the Counts section; I kept B1 out of the Blocker count and inside X-6). Round 14 sub-choices (none decided here): (d) whether the approval receiver gets a column-level UPDATE (status only) in the same release as the identity split, which needs the two update builders to set only the changed columns and `Grant` to carry an UPDATE column list; (e) the `REVOKE TEMPORARY` step is an operator deployment step, not a migration (it needs the database owner or a superuser), checked afterwards by `effective_problems` and a start-up refusal; (f) whether release A already revokes INSERT on `approval_decisions` from the agent role (otherwise release A gives NO protection against self-approval; cost: approvals stop until the receiver has its own DSN deployed); (g) how the approved restored values are bound in a place the agent cannot write (ROUND 14 point 3 in X-6), for example a digest stored in the approval request and frozen, or another value the agent role cannot write. Cost of accepting the current state: any process holding the runtime DSN (the agent, and the tick that runs client hooks) can switch off the kill switch with no audit event, set its spend to zero, insert approval decisions and set an approval request to APPROVED so that the tick runs the plan; `execute` does not require a decision row (see X-6 (4)), so a self-approved plan reaches the executor hook whenever the run is waiting; how far it gets after that depends on the client's hook and was not run. ROUND 15 additions: (6) The severity scale for X-5 and X-6: the table keeps them Major (the studio scale: a Blocker is a defect that must be fixed before anything is applied), and a separate line counts them as 'Blocker for the proposal and for pilot and production' (2). The design reviewer said this scale choice is the reviewer's call and not the studio head's; I recorded both and changed neither the table nor the studio scale. Decide whether the proposal may be sent, and whether a pilot may start, while one runtime role is shared (cost of accepting: the proposal cannot truthfully say agents never hold approver or executor rights). (7) The shape of patch C-01 (price-only, after the choke-point decision in C-3, rebased with one context line cut) or dropping it; and, if A-01 returns, whether the per-row guard of A-6 ships with it (my recommendation: yes, as a precondition). Cost of accepting no choice: the price check does not exist, a zero price still disables every spend cap for a live route built outside `load_config`. ROUND 16 additions: (8) The price of a self-hosted model (row C-8): accept the rule that only the kind `fake` may cost nothing and that a self-hosted model gets a positive notional price (my recommendation), or name another way; an 'unmetered' flag is possible but reopens the zero-price hole for hosted endpoints of the same kind (cost: a new field and a gap in the money rule). Cost of accepting no choice: either C-01 refuses to load a keyless local-model config, or, if the exemption is widened, a zero price reaches a hosted endpoint. (9) The Major rating rule (section 'Rating rule'): Major means a shown loss or bypass, or a broken written rule. If you prefer 'any gap in a deferred patch is Major', A-2 and A-3 go back to Major (7 Majors); no other row changes. Cost of accepting the rule: two rows on a deferred patch are Minor, which can read as less urgent if A-01 returns; they stay in the list of fixes to apply first.
