### X-5 full working text (kept unchanged)
Map from its old labels to the parts: the first paragraph (DELETE on the outbox and the drain login) -> X-5.1; (a) signer and anchor for the drain, and (b) a second handle inside the tick protects nothing -> X-5.1 and X-5.6; (c) planted outbox and chain rows -> X-5.2, and its read-side denial (iii) -> X-5.5; (d) what the fix breaks, and (e) the link to A-6 -> X-5.6 and X-5.2; (f) the planted checkpoint row -> X-5.3; (g) the drain's exact rights -> X-5.1; (h) the advisory lock -> X-5.4; (i) `migrate()` for a drain login, and (j) the test-breakage list -> X-5.6.

> | X-5 | Major | PROPOSE FIX | In plain words: the one database login the agent uses may delete audit records that wait for the chain, and (below) forge, plant and block audit records. RATING BASIS (round 16): rating rule parts (a) and (b), shown by the cited grants in `store/migrate.py`; Postgres behaviour UNVERIFIED. Least privilege, missing finding (round 6; owner: the lead decides; live today, independent of A-01). The runtime database role holds `DELETE` on `audit_outbox` (src `store/migrate.py:59`: `Grant("table", "SELECT, INSERT, DELETE")`; the one `runtime_role` is used by the agent AND the tick, `migrate.py:286` and `:340-342`). Rows in that table are audit events not yet in the chain; the chain table itself is protected (triggers refuse UPDATE, DELETE and TRUNCATE to the runtime, portal and purge roles, `migrate.py:42-45`; the table owner or a superuser can disable them), the outbox is not. So the identity that runs the agent can erase pending audit events (a bug or a hijacked agent: audit loss with no trace). DELETE is needed only by the drain (`chain.py:244`, and the file removal in the local-directory kind). Fix: the drain gets its own identity with `SELECT, DELETE` on `audit_outbox` plus `INSERT` and a column `SELECT (seq, hash, event_id)` on `audit_entries` (the column grant is chosen, round 11; see (g)) and, with `signer=None`, only `SELECT` on the columns `alg` and `key_label` of `audit_checkpoints` and no INSERT there (round 8 correction: the first version listed INSERT on `audit_checkpoints`; see round 8 (g) for why SELECT is still needed); the agent role keeps `SELECT, INSERT` on `audit_outbox` (INSERT is the last resort in `write_outcome`, `chain.py:223-227`; whether INSERT alone is enough with `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` was not run, so keep SELECT too). Do it in the grant map (`migrate.py:46-68`, a new role next to `runtime_role`, `portal_role` and `purge_role`; precedent: `app.purge_store or app.store`, `tick.py:196`, `app.py:151-160`, where the purge store is split off; `app.py:87` is only a comment) and prove it with `effective_problems` (`migrate.py:193`) plus a negative test: the agent role is refused DELETE on `audit_outbox`; the drain role is refused INSERT on `audit_checkpoints` and DELETE on every other table (round 8: the earlier 'drain refused UPDATE on `audit_entries`' is kept only as a regression guard, see round 8 (g)). Not designed yet: how the tick holds a second database handle for the drain (the audit sink is built with the store at `app.py:161`). If the lead refuses, record it as accepted over-breadth with the lead's yes. X-2 now points here: "add nothing" in X-2 is the cheap choice, not a clean one. Read from source, not run. ROUND 7 ADDITIONS (src, each opened by me; nothing run). (a) The drain identity needs MORE than the rights listed above: `reconcile` -> `_drain` -> `_append` ends with the inline checkpoint when a signer is set (`chain.py:203-204`, `checkpoint()` `:295-317`), and the checkpoint signs with `self.signer` and hands the head to the anchor (the signing call is `chain.py:309`, the anchor call is `:318-319`, `_anchor` is `:322-329`). So the drain process either holds the signer credential and the anchor credential (two more secrets for the drain identity, to be named in the policy with a per-action reason) or is built with `signer=None`, in which case `checkpoint()` returns None at once (`chain.py:299-300`) and the inline checkpoint is skipped; the existing `checkpoint` step (`tick.py:177`) on the runtime identity then still signs, so signing stays where it is. Pick one and say it; the cheaper one is `signer=None`. (b) Not designed, and a limit of the fix: a second database handle inside the tick (the audit sink is built with the one store at `app.py:161`) protects the outbox DELETE only if the agent and the tick run as SEPARATE processes. The tick runs the client's `execute`, `verify` and `rollback` hooks in its own process (`agent_loop.py:886`, `:901`, `:909`), so client code can reach any handle the tick holds, including the drain handle; inside one process, a second DB handle protects nothing. Also, a second `ChainAudit` object (the drain's) has its OWN `_pending` list (`chain.py:173`), so the tick's own memory events and the drain object's list are two lists; the tick's `write_outcome` must go through the object whose `_pending` the drain drains, or the drain drains only what it wrote itself. (c) The agent role KEEPS `INSERT` on `audit_outbox` (the last resort at `chain.py:223-227`), so a hijacked agent or a buggy hook can still PLANT outbox rows. Two effects, both read from code: (i) a row whose body is not a valid `AuditEvent` makes `AuditEvent.model_validate_json(body)` at `chain.py:243` raise, unguarded, so `reconcile` stops there and every row after it (ordered by `event_id`, `chain.py:239`) waits: head-of-line blocking that any holder of INSERT can create on purpose; (ii) a planted row with a CHOSEN `event_id` makes the real event's later outbox INSERT do nothing (`ON CONFLICT (event_id) DO NOTHING`, `chain.py:225`), so the planted body is what gets drained into the chain under the real id, and the real body is dropped. That needs the real chain append to fail first (otherwise the real event is already in the chain and a later duplicate is absorbed by `DuplicateEvent`), so it is narrow, but it is a loss of the real event's content, not only a delay. Neither is new in kind: the agent role also holds INSERT on `audit_entries` (`migrate.py:57`, `_APPEND`), so it can still APPEND forged audit rows directly (the chain's triggers refuse UPDATE, DELETE and TRUNCATE, `migrate.py:42-45`, so REWRITING is blocked and APPENDING is not; not run). X-5 as written removes outbox DELETE (erasing pending events); it does not remove forged appends or planted outbox rows from an identity that is allowed to write audit at all. Say that plainly in the policy text so nobody reads "the agent cannot touch the audit" into it. ROUND 10 ADDITION to (c), a read-side denial (iii) (src, opened by me; not run): the agent role can also INSERT into `audit_entries` (`migrate.py:57`), and `ChainAudit.events()` (`chain.py:279-286`) turns every stored `body` into an event with `AuditEvent.model_validate_json(body)` with no guard. A row whose `run_id` is a chosen run and whose body is not valid JSON for `AuditEvent` makes `events(run_id)` raise for that run (`verify()` flags a row whose hash does not match, but a row an agent builds with the public hash function passes the hash check and still breaks the read; either way reads fail). Round 13 (codebase Nit 1, confirmed): `verify()` has a SECOND check, `chain.py:397-400`, which compares the event id column with the event id inside the body (`_body_event_id`, `chain.py:406-410`); a body that is not JSON gives None there, so that row IS flagged, but a body that is valid JSON, is not an `AuditEvent` and carries the matching `event_id` is NOT flagged by either check, while it still breaks `events()`. Read, not run). The only caller in `src` is `runtime/agent_loop.py:847`, inside the restored-values recheck of `execute`: it sits in a `try` whose `except Exception` (`agent_loop.py:853-854`) returns `self._to(run, S.NEEDS_PERSON, "audit_unavailable")`. So the effect, read from the code, is that the run is stopped and handed to a person, not a crash: a planted row can deny execution of a chosen run (fail-closed, with a misleading reason 'audit_unavailable'); what a person then sees was not run. The fix, with the per-row guard for (c)(i) at the same time: in `events()` and in `reconcile()` (`chain.py:243`) parse each row inside its own `try`, on `ValidationError` skip that row, count it and log one ERROR line naming the `seq` (never the body), so one bad row never denies the rest; `verify()` keeps reporting the row. Test (fails on the current code): insert a row for run R with an unparseable body (and one with a body that is valid JSON but not an `AuditEvent`), write a valid `gate.decide` event for R, assert `events(R)` returns the valid event, does not raise, and the log line holds the seq and not the body; and an `execute`-level test that the recheck still finds the bound hash instead of ending in `audit_unavailable`. Risk to check in the same change: skipping a row must not let a forged row hide a real one, so the guard only skips rows that fail to parse; it never skips a row that parses. (d) What the fix BREAKS or must change: the test `tests/warden_port/test_audit_chain.py:643-677` (`test_the_runtime_role_writes_and_reads_the_chain_but_cannot_rewrite_it`) builds the chain as the RUNTIME role and asserts `log.reconcile() == 1` (comment: "SELECT + DELETE on the outbox"), so it fails once the runtime role loses DELETE and must be split (runtime role: append, checkpoint, outbox INSERT; drain role: reconcile); `migrate()` accepts only the three roles `runtime_role`, `portal_role`, `purge_role` (`migrate.py:283-292` signature, `:298-303` name check and the "must be different roles" check, `:333-339` the not-members-of-each-other check, `:340-355` one `_apply_grants` plus `effective_problems` block per role), so a drain role needs a fourth parameter, to be added to `named`, and its own block; `Grant` (`migrate.py:23-37`) has fields `runtime`, `portal`, `purge`, `purge_cols` and no drain field, so a new field (for example `drain`) and a per-relation value in `GRANTS` are needed, and the `audit_outbox` runtime value changes from `SELECT, INSERT, DELETE` to `SELECT, INSERT` (`migrate.py:59`); docstrings that state the old grant must change: the comment `migrate.py:42-45` ("reconcile deletes an outbox row once it is in the chain", written for the runtime role) and the `migrate` docstring `migrate.py:294-295` ("DELETE only on the outbox and the purged evidence tables"); on EXISTING deployments `_apply_grants` runs `REVOKE ALL` on each relation (`migrate.py:179`) and then re-grants, so the next `migrate` run with the new map takes DELETE away from the runtime role (intended), and a deployment with no drain role yet then has a drain nobody can run: the CHANGELOG upgrade steps must say the drain role and its DSN secret must exist before the runtime role is migrated, and the tick must show a missing drain handle as a red step; a live-profile check like `app.py:86-89` (live and Postgres store and no `purge_dsn_secret_name` raises `ConfigError`) is needed so a pilot or prod deployment cannot start without the drain DSN secret name, with the setting next to `purge_dsn_secret_name` (`backends/table.py:120`) and the second connection built like the purge store (`app.py:151-157`); the local-directory kind (`audit/local_dir.py`) has no database role at all, so the same split there is a directory permission question (agent may create files in `outbox/`, only the drain may delete them), which is not designed, and Postgres grants do not cover it (see the single-dependency section). KEEP `SELECT` on `audit_outbox` for the agent role: `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT (event_id) DO NOTHING` probably needs SELECT on the conflict column in Postgres (not run; prove it with a negative test on a real database before removing it). (e) Link to A-6: see A-6, round 7 note. ROUND 8 ADDITIONS (src, tests and migrations opened by me; nothing run; I checked each of the reviewer's points and mark below where I corrected it). (f) PLANTED CHECKPOINT ROW (confirmed, a fourth way to damage the trail, live today, independent of A-01 and of the drain). The runtime role holds `SELECT, INSERT` on `audit_checkpoints` (`migrate.py:58`, `_APPEND`). The table's only constraint is `seq BIGINT PRIMARY KEY` with `hash` and `signature` text (`migrations/0002_audit_chain.sql:14-18`; `0004_audit_checkpoint_meta.sql:6-7` later adds the nullable `alg` and `key_label` columns); there is no link to `audit_entries` and no check of the signature. `checkpoint()` decides what is signed from `signed_through()`, which is `SELECT MAX(seq) FROM audit_checkpoints` (`chain.py:290-293`): at `chain.py:307` `if self.signed_through() >= seq: return seq` returns 'already signed' without signing, and the signing INSERT is `ON CONFLICT (seq) DO NOTHING RETURNING seq` (`chain.py:310-314`), so `new` is empty and `_anchor` is skipped (`:318`). So one junk row at a seq at or above the chain head makes the signing step quietly stop: no error is logged, the tick step `checkpoint` (`tick.py:177-181`) stays green, and no new head is signed or anchored until the chain grows past the planted seq (never, for a very large seq). The row cannot be removed: the append-only trigger refuses DELETE and UPDATE to the runtime, portal and purge roles (`0002_audit_chain.sql:30-31`, trigger on `audit_checkpoints`; the table owner or a superuser can disable it, and they are not the identities in this finding). What does notice: `verify()` reports `checkpoint N has an invalid signature` for the junk row (`chain.py:413-436`) and `unsigned_tail` grows; nothing runs `verify()` on a schedule in this repository (not searched beyond `cli.py`). Fix options (the lead decides); ROUND 11 corrects the round-10 version of this block, including one mistake of mine. (i) Only an identity that holds the signer may INSERT into `audit_checkpoints`: the agent and tick are built with `signer=None` (no inline checkpoint, so they lose INSERT there) and the `checkpoint` step runs in a separate signing process. Cost, stated plainly (reviewer M2): the tick runs the client's `execute`, `verify` and `rollback` hooks in its own process (`agent_loop.py:806-909`), so the signer credential must NOT sit in the tick process next to those hooks; a separate signing process or job is therefore needed, with its own database identity `signer` (INSERT on `audit_checkpoints`, SELECT on `audit_entries` `hash` and `seq`) and its own secret; this is the same single-process limit as the drain (see (b)), and it is the only option that stops the junk row at its source. (ii) Count only rows that verify in `signed_through()` and `checkpoint()` (signature against the public key, `hash` equal to the chain hash at that seq) and ignore rows above the head. It is NOT the cheap option I first called it: it needs new code in the signing step and a verify routine, and a writer has the public key only if it holds a signer (`self.signer.public_key()`, `chain.py:352`), so a `signer=None` writer cannot do it; and on its own it closes only the above-head case (below). (iii) FOREIGN KEY, weighed in round 11 (reviewer M2, the option nobody had costed): a foreign key `(seq, hash)` from `audit_checkpoints` to `audit_entries`, which needs a UNIQUE `(seq, hash)` on `audit_entries` (trivially true, `seq` is already the primary key). It makes a junk row ABOVE the head and a junk row with a WRONG hash impossible to insert (no matching entry), at the price of one migration and one extra unique index; the signer inserts the checkpoint after the entry exists (already true: `chain.py:305-312`). It does NOT stop a junk row with the TRUE head hash and a bad signature: the runtime role must read `hash` to append (`chain.py:187`), so the attacker knows it. Verdict: adopt the foreign key as a cheap second guard (reason: it removes the above-head case at the database, so the (2) test below becomes a test of the constraint), and do not count it as a fix of the head-seq case. ROUND 12 (design M5, confirmed; weaker than it reads): the attacker role can INSERT a chain entry itself (`audit_entries` INSERT is in its grant) and so create the seq the foreign key needs, so the key stops only a checkpoint for a seq with no entry at all; a `(seq, signature)` key allows unbounded junk rows. It also breaks three existing tests that insert checkpoint rows with no matching entry: `tests/test_p2_postgres.py:289`, `:296`, `:299` (they must insert the entry first). Net: a weak second guard, adopted only with those test changes. Unverified: Postgres behaviour of the constraint. CORRECTION OF MY ROUND-10 TEXT: I proposed a primary key `(seq, hash)` for the head-seq case. That does not work either, for the same reason: the attacker copies the true `hash`, so the junk row has the same `(seq, hash)` as the real one and the real row still conflicts. A key that lets the real row in must include the signature, `(seq, signature)`: real and junk rows then coexist at one seq, `signed_through()` and `verify()` must accept one valid row per seq, and `_check_anchors` (`chain.py:443`, `stored = {seq: (head, signature) ...}`) must be rewritten because it assumes one row per seq. Cost: junk rows cannot be removed (append-only trigger), so the table can grow with junk at the agent's write rate; `verify()` must skip them and report them. So for the head-seq case there are two real options, (i) (stop the INSERT right) and the `(seq, signature)` key (accept junk but keep signing); (ii) and the foreign key cannot close it. ROUND 10 and 11 REWORDING of what (ii) and its negative test can achieve (src, opened by me; not run). (1) A junk row at exactly the HEAD seq: `audit_checkpoints.seq` is `BIGINT PRIMARY KEY` (`0002_audit_chain.sql:15`), the signing INSERT is `ON CONFLICT (seq) DO NOTHING` (`chain.py:312`), the append-only trigger refuses DELETE and UPDATE (`0002_audit_chain.sql:30-31`), so the junk row holds that seq for good; counting only valid rows in `signed_through()` does not help, because the INSERT itself does nothing. Needs (i) or the `(seq, signature)` key, as above; the migration is proposed as 0009 (see the number list in X-6). (2) What a test CAN show now: a junk row ABOVE the head. On the current code such a row suppresses signing: `signed_through()` is `MAX(seq)` (`chain.py:290-293`), so `if self.signed_through() >= seq: return seq` (`chain.py:307-308`) returns the head seq without writing anything. Test: plant a junk row at head plus 1000, call `checkpoint()`, then assert (A) it returns the head seq, (B) a row with a valid signature exists at the head seq, (C) `signed_through()` equals the head seq. ROUND 11 CORRECTION (reviewer M3, confirmed): only (B) and (C) fail on the current code; (A) PASSES on the current code, because the early return at `:308` returns the head seq too (and on the current code `signed_through()` is head plus 1000, so (C) fails). Write (A) as a regression check, not as a failing assertion; the test as a whole still fails on the old code, through (B) and (C). Limit that must be said: the junk row still occupies its own seq, so when the chain later grows to exactly that seq the head-seq case in (1) applies there (one checkpoint cannot be written); heads above that seq are signed normally and each signature covers all earlier rows through the hash chain, so only that one checkpoint is lost, and `verify()` still reports the junk row. (3) Negative test for (i): a role without INSERT on `audit_checkpoints` is refused (Postgres, assert the class `InsufficientPrivilege`). A head-seq test (junk row with the true hash at the head seq, then `checkpoint()` must still write a valid row) is written only together with (i) or the `(seq, signature)` key; until one is chosen the head-seq case stays OPEN in this row (next step: the lead's choice). Add this to (c): the planted-row list is now outbox rows, chain rows, checkpoint rows and (round 10) chain rows with an unreadable body, which deny READS. (g) DRAIN RIGHTS, corrected (the reviewer said the drain needs no `audit_checkpoints` at all; that is half wrong). With `signer=None`, `checkpoint()` returns at `chain.py:299-300` before it touches the table, and `_append` runs the inline checkpoint only `if self.signer is not None` (`chain.py:203-204`), so the drain needs no INSERT there and no signer or anchor credential. But `build()` runs `SELECT alg, key_label FROM audit_checkpoints LIMIT 1` when it constructs the sink on a Postgres store (`chain.py:529`) and turns any failure into 'the audit tables are missing' (`:530-541`), so a drain role with no SELECT on those two columns cannot even build its sink, and the error would blame a missing migration. Also `build()` refuses an anchor without a signer (`chain.py:544-545`), so the drain's `make('audit', ...)` is called with `signer=None` and `anchor=None`. Drain rights, complete for this repository's code (Postgres, not run): `INSERT` plus a COLUMN `SELECT (seq, hash, event_id)` on `audit_entries` (round 10, src: `_append`, `chain.py:180-206`, reads only `event_id` at `:185`, and `seq` and `hash` at `:187`, so the drain needs no read of `body`, `at`, `run_id`, `kind` or `prev`; the precedent for a column grant is `Grant.purge_cols`, `migrate.py:34-37`; DECISION (round 11): use the column grant, and do not give the drain the full `SELECT` (it would let a hijacked drain read every event body, which it never needs); Postgres not run, unverified); `SELECT, DELETE` on `audit_outbox` (SELECT is needed by the DELETE's WHERE and by the drain's own read); `SELECT (alg, key_label)` on `audit_checkpoints` (column grants have a precedent in `Grant.purge_cols`, `migrate.py:34-37`). The negative tests above follow: the agent role refused DELETE on `audit_outbox`; the drain role refused INSERT on `audit_checkpoints` and DELETE on every other table; a drain role that is a member of the runtime role refused by `migrate()` ((i)). The reviewer is right that 'drain refused UPDATE on `audit_entries`' does not test the split, but wrong that it 'proves nothing' when the assertion names `InsufficientPrivilege` (see X-2, round 8 note): keep it only as a regression guard. (h) ADVISORY LOCK (confirmed in source; Postgres behaviour not run). Every append takes `pg_advisory_xact_lock(_PG_LOCK)` before its SELECT (`chain.py:120`); the key is a constant derived from the bytes `hadvnaud` (`chain.py:47`), public in the source. As far as I know any database role may call that function (the default privilege of PUBLIC on it; not checked against a live database), so a hijacked agent identity can hold the lock inside an open transaction and every `write_intent` and `write_outcome` of every writer waits: with no timeout configured the wait is unbounded, which is a hang, not a fallback to the outbox (`write_outcome` only falls back when `_append` RAISES, `chain.py:219-229`). A separate drain role does not remove this; neither does any grant change. The mitigation against ACCIDENTS (not against a hostile session; see the round 15 correction in this paragraph) is on the connection, not in the grant map: set `lock_timeout` and `statement_timeout` on the writers' DSNs (or `ALTER ROLE ... SET` for the runtime and drain roles) so a blocked append raises and the existing outbox fallback runs, and `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` on the agent role so a held lock is released (ROUND 15 CORRECTION, design reviewer F9, UNVERIFIED: not run against Postgres; from my knowledge of Postgres, `lock_timeout`, `statement_timeout` and `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` are ordinary user-settable parameters, so a hijacked agent session can `SET` them back to 0 for itself: these settings are ACCIDENT controls only, for a bug that leaves a transaction open, and must not be listed as a control against a hostile agent. The real control for the hostile case is that the agent identity cannot call the advisory-lock function at all. That cannot be done while the audit writer is the same role, because the writer must call `pg_advisory_xact_lock` (`chain.py:120`); it needs the audit writer as its own identity (a function owned by an audit-writer role, X-6 AUDIT WRITES) and then `REVOKE EXECUTE` on the advisory-lock functions from PUBLIC with a grant to that role only, which needs the database owner or a superuser; or the advisory lock is replaced by a row lock on a one-row table the agent role has no right to lock. Both are proposals, neither was checked against a live database); ROUND 11 TIE TO X-4: a short `lock_timeout` turns a hang into a fallback to the outbox, and nothing drains the outbox today (X-4), so with the timeout on and no drain running the result is an outbox that grows without a signal; set the timeouts only once a drain (A-01, or an equivalent job run by hand on a schedule) is live; whether `lock_timeout` applies to advisory-lock waits as documented, and which values suit, were not checked or run. The same key idea applies to `migrate`'s own lock, which only the migration role runs (`migrate.py:69`, taken at `:314`). This is a loss of availability, not of data; it is why fail-closed `write_intent` (raises `AuditUnavailable`) is the safe side. (i) `migrate()` for a drain role (src `migrate.py:283-355`, opened by me; the reviewer's cites are close: the role name check is `:298-300`, `named` is `:301`, the 'different roles' check is `:302-303`, the pairwise not-members check is `:333-339` and the per-role grant blocks are `:340-355`). A drain role must be added to `named`, or the not-members check never sees it and it can be a member of the runtime role and inherit its DELETE; it also needs its own `_apply_grants` and `effective_problems` block with expectation `lambda g: g.drain if g else ""` (and a column expectation for `audit_checkpoints`); the error text at `:336-338` ('the runtime, portal and purge roles must not be members of each other') and the message at `:303` must name the fourth role. Compatibility (the reviewer's item 2, confirmed): the new parameter must be OPTIONAL with default `None` (`def migrate(...)` at `:283-292` is called with `runtime_role` and `purge_role` only by `tests/test_k5_round10_postgres.py:104` and `tests/contracts/kinds/store_postgres.py:44`, and with `runtime_role` only by `tests/test_p2_postgres.py:181`, `tests/test_p7_store.py:143` and `tests/test_k2_migrations.py:218,234`). ROUND 14 CORRECTION: the round 13 list below is NOT complete (the word 'completes' is withdrawn); four more kinds of caller exist (src, opened by me): `tests/test_k5_fixes2.py:302` (`mig.migrate(dsn)`, no role), `tests/test_p3b_neutral_audit.py:324` (`migrate_mod.migrate(dsn, iam_auth=True, region=..., sslrootcert=...)`, no role) and the error-path calls with a dummy DSN, `tests/test_k2_migrations.py:115`, `:120`, `:125` and `tests/test_p2_postgres.py:78`, which fail before they connect. The conclusion is unchanged: the new parameter is optional with default `None`, so none of them breaks. ROUND 13 LISTED (codebase Nit 2; grep over `src` and `tests`, opened by me): `runtime_role` only also at `tests/warden_port/test_audit_chain.py:659`; `runtime_role` and `portal_role` at `tests/test_k2_migrations.py:253` and `:384`; `portal_role` only at `tests/test_k2_migrations.py:280`, `:286` and `:441`; no role at all at `tests/test_p2_postgres.py:161`, `:170` and `:290`, `tests/test_p7_store.py:175`, `tests/test_k5_round10_postgres.py:102`, `tests/contracts/kinds/store_postgres.py:36` and `tests/test_k2_migrations.py:394`; and the one production caller, `cli.py:330`, which passes all three roles by keyword (`_migrate`, `cli.py:312`), and the new `Grant` field `drain: str = ""` must default to nothing, so every existing `GRANTS` entry keeps working and gives the drain role no rights (deny by default). What the optional default means for a deployment with no drain role is open: either the runtime role keeps outbox DELETE (today's behaviour, X-5 stays open without anybody noticing) or it loses it and nobody can drain; the live-profile refusal in (d) (no drain DSN secret name, no start) is what makes the first choice safe, so keep both together. (j) TEST-BREAKAGE LIST for the split (the reviewer's item 2, completed by grep over `tests/`): `tests/warden_port/test_audit_chain.py:643-679` `test_the_runtime_role_writes_and_reads_the_chain_but_cannot_rewrite_it` breaks twice, at `:671` (`signed_through() == 2`, an inline checkpoint written by the RUNTIME role: if (f) option (i) removes INSERT on `audit_checkpoints` from that role, `checkpoint()` catches the refusal at `chain.py:315-317`, logs it and returns None, so the assertion fails) and at `:677` (`reconcile() == 1` as the runtime role, which loses DELETE); split it into a runtime-role test (append, outbox INSERT, refused DELETE on the outbox) and a drain-role test (reconcile). Checked and NOT broken: `tests/test_k2_migrations.py:214-225` and `:249-` read `grant.runtime` from the map, so they follow it; `tests/test_p2_postgres.py:181` and `tests/test_p7_store.py:143` check `runs`, `approval_decisions`, `dead_letters` and similar, not the outbox; ROUND 10 correction of this last claim: `tests/contracts/test_capabilities.py:45` and `:59` use the `audit` fixture, which runs over EVERY registered audit contract kind, including `chain_postgres` (`tests/contracts/kinds/audit_chain.py:128-138`), which builds its store from `pg_schema()` with the default `runtime=False`, that is the OWNER DSN, not a runtime role. So those two assertions run on Postgres (when `HADAVIN_TEST_PG_DSN` is set) and the A-3 return-type change touches Postgres runs, though they do not use a restricted role and so do not break from the grant split. ### X-6 row as it stood before round 17 (verbatim)

> | X-6 | Major | PROPOSE FIX | In plain words: one database login (the runtime role) serves the agent, the scheduled tick and the approval receiver, so the agent's identity can switch off its own kill switch, set its own spend counters to zero, add approval decisions, and set an approval request to APPROVED directly, after which the tick runs the plan. LIVE in today's source, independent of A-01; read from source, not run; every Postgres statement is UNVERIFIED. The lead decides. The finding is split into seven parts in the table X-6.1 to X-6.7 directly below (each part: what is wrong, who can do it, what it costs). VERDICT (rounds 13 and 14, still current): the approval-request guard proposed in earlier rounds must NOT be built as written; the gaps found in it are in part X-6.5. RATING BASIS (round 16, rating rule): Major because parts X-6.1 to X-6.3 are defects shown in today's source (the cited grants) and because the shared login breaks hard rule 11 (agents never hold approver or executor credentials); the gaps in the proposed guard are gaps in a proposal, not new defects in today's source or in the three patches. NOT shown: that any deployment really uses one login for all three jobs today (no second login setting exists except the purge one, `backends/table.py:120`), and how far a self-approved plan gets, which depends on the client's hook. The full working text of rounds 10 to 14 (every cite, test and Postgres statement) is kept unchanged in the section 'X-6 full working text' at the end of this file. |

### X-6 full working text (rounds 10 to 14, kept unchanged)
This is the complete text of the row X-6 as it stood before round 16, moved here so the table stays readable. It contains the round labels (B1, M1 to M7, Nit, POINT 1 to 9), every cite, the proposed grants, tests and rollout, and every Postgres statement, all UNVERIFIED. The seven parts X-6.1 to X-6.7 above summarise it.

Least privilege, missing finding (round 10, reworked in round 11; the lead decides; live today, independent of A-01). ROUND 14 STATUS: same verdict; five Majors and four Minors against the proposed guard are answered in the ROUND 14 block at the end of this row (severity stays Major: they are gaps in the proposal, not new defects in today's source or in the three patches). ROUND 13 STATUS: PROPOSE FIX, DO NOT BUILD THE GUARD AS WRITTEN. The design review found one Blocker (B1, a temporary table lets the agent forge the record the guard reads) and five Majors (M1 to M5) in the guard and the audit-writer proposal; each is answered below, and every answer is written text only: nothing was built, run or tested, and the Postgres behaviour is the reviewer's knowledge, not checked by me. Severity of this row stays Major: the live defect it records (one database role for the agent, the tick and the approval receiver) is in today's source; B1 is a flaw in the proposed fix, not in today's source or in the three patches (Counts section, scale note). ONE database role serves several identities. `migrate()` knows three roles, `runtime_role`, `portal_role` and `purge_role` (`store/migrate.py:286-288`, name checks `:298-303`), and the app builds one store for everything (`app.py:146`; only the purge store is split off, `app.py:151-160`). The runtime role holds (src, `migrate.py:47-59`): `SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE` on `kill_switch` (`:55`), `spend` (`:53`), `spend_month` (`:54`), `runs` (`:47`), `plans` (`:48`) and `approval_requests` (`:50`), and `SELECT, INSERT` on `approval_decisions` (`:51`). Consequences at DATABASE level, read from code, not run, all unverified on Postgres: (1) the role can UPDATE `kill_switch` to off, with no `Limits` path and so no audit event; the code's only writer is the operator command (`cli.py:383-408` through `Limits.stop` and `Limits.reset`, `limits.py:43-52`) and the agent loop only reads it (`agent_loop.py:252`, `:817`); (2) it can UPDATE `spend` and `spend_month` to zero (the code only adds, `store/postgres.py:416-431`); (3) it can INSERT `approval_decisions` (the code's only writer is the receiver path, `postgres.py:337-345`, `requests.py:170`); (4) ROUND 11, reviewer M1, confirmed: it can also set an approval request to APPROVED directly, because it holds INSERT (the agent's `request`, `requests.py:107`) and UPDATE (`sweep` and `resend_unsent`, `requests.py:126`, `:210`) on `approval_requests`, and the tick step `reconcile` (`tick.py:85-95`) lists every APPROVED request and calls `runtime.on_decided`, which calls `execute` and so runs the plan (`agent_loop.py:799-806`). So removing INSERT on `approval_decisions` alone (3) does NOT stop self-approval; the negative test 'agent role refused INSERT on `approval_decisions`' would pass while this hole stays open. Narrowed in round 12 (codebase N3, confirmed): `reconcile` needs the run to be WAITING_FOR_APPROVAL (`tick.py:91-92`), and `get_approval_decision` returns None, not an error, when there is no decision row (`store/postgres.py:389-397`, `store/memory.py:175-180`), so a self-set APPROVED request reaches `execute(approval=None)`; `execute` loads the run's gate decision at `agent_loop.py:809` (that line only loads it; round 14 corrected the earlier word 'demands') and `_recheck` at `:814` compares its plan hash (`:783`), and, for EVERY plan, a `gate.decide` audit event that carries a restored hash (round 13, codebase M2, confirmed: `agent_loop.py:742` sets `restored_hash` on every such event, the digest is always a non-empty SHA-256 or HMAC hex string, `:267-278`, and `execute` reads the stored events at `:845-852` and stops with NEEDS_PERSON `restored_unbound` at `:855-866` when none carries one; the earlier qualifier 'only for plans with restored values' was wrong); a run that is waiting already has the gate decision and that audit event; so nothing in `execute` requires a decision row (the conclusion is unchanged). The hole is real (round 12: and retarget, reset and replay, and upsert-over-APPROVED make it wider, see the guard) Correction to X-2 is now written in X-2. IDENTITY TABLE (round 11, reviewer B1; code facts are src-checked, the deployment is PROPOSED and not built). Rule: each process holds exactly ONE database identity, built from the secret name of that identity only; no process holds two. (a) Agent loop (plan, gate, `Approvals.request`, `requests.py:95-109`): identity `agent`. ROUND 12 CORRECTION (design M1, confirmed): the agent loop ALSO executes: for every ALLOW-class plan it calls `self.execute(run.run_id)` (`agent_loop.py:768`), and `execute` calls the client's `execute`, `verify` and `rollback` hooks (`agent_loop.py:886-909`), so client code runs in the agent process as well as in the tick. The earlier sentence that placed client hooks only in the tick was wrong. Choice for the lead (added to choice (5)): move ALLOW-class execution to the tick (cost: ALLOW-class actions wait up to one tick interval, a code change at `agent_loop.py:768`, and the agent process then holds no executor hooks, so `agent` and `tick` could really be two identities), or accept that both processes reach client hooks, in which case `agent` and `tick` are ONE identity in practice, as the next sentence already allows. Not done. (b) Tick and executor (`runtime/tick.py` steps; its `reconcile` step runs `on_decided`, and `execute` calls the client's hooks here too, `agent_loop.py:806-909`): identity `tick`. The repository does not say whether (a) and (b) are one process or two; if they are one, `agent` and `tick` are ONE identity, and this row says so instead of claiming a split it cannot make: the rights below that do not need that split (kill switch, approval decisions, the approval status guard, the spend trigger) still hold, provided that process holds none of the DSNs of (c) to (g). (c) Approval receiver (`approvals/receiver.py:30`, always `record_only=True`): identity `receiver`. (d) Operator command, stop and reset (`cli.py:383-408`): identity `operator`. (e) Audit drain (X-5): identity `drain`. (f) Signing step (X-5 (f) option (i)): identity `signer`, the only one with INSERT on `audit_checkpoints` and the signer key. (g) Migration: the owner DSN, used only at deploy time, never inside a running process. Existing: `portal` (read only), `purge`. Live-profile refusal (like `app.py:86-89`, which refuses a live Postgres store with no `purge_dsn_secret_name`): in a live profile each of (c) to (f) needs its own DSN secret-name setting, and a process whose config names the secret of a second identity is refused with `ConfigError`. Negative test (shape; the secrets kinds were not read, so whether they can be scoped to a list of names is unverified and not designed): build the app as the agent-and-tick process with its secrets scoped to its own names, and assert that resolving the receiver, operator, drain or signer secret name is refused, and that building it with two identities' names set raises `ConfigError`. Why it matters: the tick runs client hooks, so any DSN in the tick process is reachable by client code (X-5 (b)); a role split without this table protects nothing. GRANTS (exact; all Postgres, unverified, each to be proved with `effective_problems`, `migrate.py:193`, and a negative test). `agent` and `tick` (or the one runtime identity): as today MINUS: no INSERT or UPDATE on `kill_switch` (SELECT only), no INSERT on `approval_decisions` (SELECT only), `spend` and `spend_month` keep SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE but behind the trigger below, and `approval_requests` behind the guard below. `operator`: `SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE` on `kill_switch` (the row is seeded by `0003_approvals_tick.sql:25-27`; the code's upsert is INSERT .. ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE, `postgres.py:453-458`, so it needs both); for the audit it must write (`reset` writes the audit FIRST and fails closed, `limits.py:48-52`; `stop` writes it second, `:43-46`): `INSERT` plus column `SELECT (seq, hash, event_id)` on `audit_entries` and `INSERT` on `audit_outbox` (`SELECT` on it only if the `ON CONFLICT` needs it, not run), `SELECT (alg, key_label)` on `audit_checkpoints` for the sink's build probe (`chain.py:529`); NO DELETE on any table. Reviewer G2 asked for 'INSERT only on `audit_entries` and `audit_outbox`': half right: no DELETE and no full SELECT, but INSERT alone is not enough, because `_append` reads `event_id`, `seq` and `hash` (`chain.py:185`, `:187`), so without those three columns `write_intent` fails and `reset` is refused with exit 3 (`cli.py:383-388`). `receiver` (round 11, reviewer M2, the 'to be read when built' hedge is closed): `decide` calls `_audit` at `requests.py:163` and `:173`, so it needs the SAME audit rights as the operator (`INSERT` plus column `SELECT (seq, hash, event_id)` on `audit_entries`, `INSERT` on `audit_outbox`, `SELECT (alg, key_label)` on `audit_checkpoints`, no signer, so no INSERT there); plus `SELECT, UPDATE` on `approval_requests` (the guarded compare-and-set, `postgres.py:349-352`), `SELECT, INSERT` on `approval_decisions` with `USAGE` on `approval_decisions_seq_seq` (`migrate.py:66`). It needs NO write on `runs` or `plans`: the receiver always calls `receive(..., record_only=True)` (`receiver.py:30`), and `decide` returns at `requests.py:177` before `update_message` and `on_decided`. Whether anything at start-up reads other tables was not traced. GUARD FOR THE APPROVAL REQUEST (closes (4); round 12 rebuilt it after design B1, whose three holes I confirmed in src; Postgres behaviour unverified). The round-11 guard (status to APPROVED only with a decision row) left three holes. (a) RETARGET: the decision row has `approval_id`, `plan_hash`, `step`, `decision` and no `run_id` (`models.py:182-190`); `reconcile` takes `run_id` from the REQUEST row (`tick.py:88-95`); `_recheck` compares the plan hash with the run's own gate decision only, never the approval's `run_id` or `plan_hash` (`agent_loop.py:776-797`); the agent holds whole-row UPDATE on `approval_requests` (`migrate.py:50`; both UPDATE builders set every column, `postgres.py:316-326`, `:349-355`); so it can wait for a human to approve a harmless request and then UPDATE its `run_id` to another run that is WAITING_FOR_APPROVAL, and `reconcile` runs that run. (b) RESET AND REPLAY: nothing forbade APPROVED to PENDING to APPROVED, and `save_approval_request` is `INSERT .. ON CONFLICT (approval_id) DO UPDATE SET <every column>` (`postgres.py:311`, `_insert` at `:204-218`), so a fresh PENDING INSERT over an APPROVED row passes a rule that says 'an INSERT must be PENDING' and resets it; the only caller is `requests.py:107`, which never overwrites a request. (c) 'a matching decision' was undefined. THE GUARD, a trigger on `approval_requests`, BEFORE INSERT OR UPDATE: INSERT (round 13, design M2, confirmed: the agent writes `run_id`, `plan_hash`, `steps`, `requested_by`, `risk_class`, `chain_id` and `summary` of the request itself, `requests.py:97-107`, and `_recheck`, `agent_loop.py:776-797`, never reads the approval, so a request that only says PENDING can be a decoy for any run): status must be PENDING AND `current_step` must be 0 AND a `gate_decisions` row exists for `NEW.run_id` with outcome `needs_approval` and the same `plan_hash` as `NEW.plan_hash` (the agent loop saves that row first, `agent_loop.py:747`) AND the run `NEW.run_id` is WAITING_FOR_APPROVAL (the loop sets that state before it asks for approval, `agent_loop.py:762-763`) AND no other request of the same run is still PENDING. This needs SELECT on `gate_decisions` and `runs` (the agent holds both) and schema-qualified names (B1 below). WHAT SQL CANNOT CHECK, said plainly: that `summary` truly describes the plan and that `steps` (the list of approvers) is the right list, because both are text the agent wrote; the person who approves still trusts agent-written words. That is a residual risk, not a fix; who closes it is the studio head's choice (end of file, choice (5)). And the code change that goes with it: `save_approval_request` becomes a PLAIN INSERT, no upsert (`postgres.py:311`; the memory store the same), so an existing approval id is refused by the primary key. UPDATE: (1) FROZEN columns, any change refused: `run_id`, `plan_hash`, `steps`, `requested_by`, `chain_id`, `risk_class`, `created_at`, and (round 13, design M3, confirmed) `summary` and `step_expires_at`. `summary`: its only write is the INSERT (`requests.py:97-107`); the other updates (`requests.py:124`, `:166`, `:198`, `:207`) change only `sent_at` and `channel_ref`, `status`, or `current_step`, `step_expires_at` and `sent_at`, and the update builders (`postgres.py:316-326`, `:349-355`) write `summary` back unchanged, which a freeze accepts; this answers the earlier 'whether anything changes summary was not checked'. `step_expires_at`: left writable, the agent could push an expiry out for ever or into the past; it may change only in the escalation (rule 4). `sent_at` and `channel_ref` stay writable (the resend and message update change them). (2) Any change of `status` needs OLD.status = 'PENDING'; APPROVED, REJECTED and EXPIRED are terminal and immutable (only `sent_at` and `channel_ref` may still change on them). (3) A change to APPROVED or REJECTED needs an `approval_decisions` row with `approval_id` = NEW.approval_id, `step` = NEW.current_step (the step cannot change in the same statement), `plan_hash` = NEW.plan_hash, and `decision` = 'approve' for APPROVED or 'reject' for REJECTED (columns from `models.py:182-190`; the SQL column names were not opened). (4) EXPIRED only from PENDING and only when `OLD.step_expires_at` is not later than `now()` plus a stated clock-skew allowance (a setting, no number is fixed here): the sweep decides what is due with the application clock (`tick.py:81` passes `ctx.now`) while `now()` is the database clock, so a check with no allowance would refuse a legitimate sweep; an escalation (the only change allowed to `current_step` and `step_expires_at`) only PENDING to PENDING with `NEW.current_step = OLD.current_step + 1` exactly, `NEW.current_step < jsonb_array_length(NEW.steps)` (the sweep's own test, `requests.py:196-197`), `NEW.step_expires_at` later than `OLD.step_expires_at`, and the same due condition on `OLD.step_expires_at` (design M3, confirmed). The trigger runs as the invoking role (a function that uses the rights of whoever fires it, as opposed to a SECURITY DEFINER function, which runs with the rights of the role that owns it), so it needs SELECT on the tables it reads (`approval_decisions`, and with the INSERT rule `gate_decisions` and `runs`): the agent, tick and receiver roles all keep it. ROUND 13 BLOCKER (design B1; the src facts were opened by me, the Postgres behaviour is the reviewer's knowledge and was not run): the earlier sentence 'the roles cannot CREATE in the schema, so they cannot shadow the names' is FALSE for temporary tables. A temporary table exists only inside one database session and vanishes when the session ends; when a table name is written with no schema in front of it, Postgres looks in the session's temporary schema (`pg_temp`) BEFORE the normal schema, and every role may create temporary tables by default (the `TEMPORARY` right on the database is held through PUBLIC unless revoked). What src does: `effective_problems` checks CREATE on `current_schema()` only (`migrate.py:210-212`), and a search of `src/hadavin_sdk` for `TEMP`, `search_path`, `SECURITY DEFINER`, `ENABLE ALWAYS` and `session_replication_role` finds hits that are MIGRATION-TIME only (round 14: the earlier wording 'finds nothing relevant' was an over-claim and is withdrawn): `store/migrate.py:126` (docstring of `_scan`: the migration file scanner refuses UNLOGGED and TEMP tables written inside a migration file), `store/migration_numbers.py:12-14` (a planned read-back of `search_path` after each migration file, 'a later change') and `:251` (a `SET search_path TO` line inside the written recipe for a late migration file); the header of `migration_numbers.py` (`:5-7`; round 15 fixed from `:3-5`) says that nothing calls it yet, and `migrate()` does not. None of these is a run-time defence for a database session of the agent role, so nothing refuses or checks temporary tables at run time today, and B1 stands. Once wired in, `migration_numbers.refused_statements` (`:633-639`) refuses `SET` and `RESET` (except two `SET LOCAL` timeouts), `DO`, `EXECUTE`, `ALTER ROLE`, `ALTER USER`, `ALTER DATABASE`, `CREATE ROLE` and `GRANT` or `REVOKE` without `ON`. My reading of those lines (Postgres behaviour unverified): creating the owner role inside a migration file is refused, and `REVOKE TEMPORARY ON DATABASE` cannot live in a migration file either (it needs the database name, so it needs `DO` or `EXECUTE`, and it needs database-owner rights); part (2) below is therefore an OPERATOR DEPLOYMENT STEP, checked afterwards by `effective_problems` and by the start-up refusal. THE ATTACK: the agent role runs `CREATE TEMP TABLE approval_decisions (...)`, inserts one forged 'approve' row for the target request into it, then runs the UPDATE that sets the request to APPROVED; the guard, written with the bare name `approval_decisions`, reads the agent's temporary table first, finds the forged row and lets the update through. The claim 'cannot shadow the names' is therefore withdrawn, and the guard of rounds 11 and 12 is NOT sound as written. REQUIRED FIX, all three together (none built): (1) the trigger function never uses a bare table name: it builds every query from the schema of the table that fired it (`TG_TABLE_SCHEMA`, a value Postgres gives a trigger function) with `format('%I.%I', TG_TABLE_SCHEMA, 'approval_decisions')` run through `EXECUTE`, AND the function carries `SET search_path = pg_catalog, <the schema>, pg_temp` with `pg_temp` written LAST, and calls its pg_catalog functions with the schema written (`pg_catalog.now()`). ROUND 14 CORRECTION (design Major 1): this is NOT an either-or. Schema-qualified table names alone leave every function, operator and cast in the trigger body (`jsonb_array_length`, `now()`, `=`) resolved through the caller's `search_path`; a session can set its own `search_path` so that its temporary schema comes first and define a temporary function or operator that wins (the same class as CVE-2018-1058), and a shadow placed in `public` works too where PUBLIC may CREATE there (Postgres before 15, or a database upgraded from one). The round 13 sentence that only tables, views and types are exposed is withdrawn. All of this is the reviewer's knowledge, unverified on Postgres; (2) `REVOKE TEMPORARY ON DATABASE <db> FROM PUBLIC` and from the agent, tick, receiver, operator, drain and signer roles (the right is held through PUBLIC by default, so revoking it from a role alone changes nothing; the migration role is not in this list); this is a database-level statement, which `_apply_grants` (one relation at a time, `migrate.py:170-186`) does not issue today, and it needs a role that owns the database or is a superuser (not checked), and any other tool that uses temporary tables on the same database would be refused (not checked); (3) `effective_problems` gets `has_database_privilege(role, current_database(), 'TEMP')` and reports a role that still has it, and (round 14) reports CREATE on EVERY schema on the role's search path, not only `current_schema()` (today `migrate.py:210-212` checks that one schema), so a later change cannot bring the hole back unseen. The same rule (schema-qualified names, or a pinned `search_path` ending in `pg_temp`) binds every new function in this file, the audit-writer function included (X-6, AUDIT WRITES). The owner or a superuser can disable the trigger; they are not the identities above. ROUND 13 (design M4, confirmed in src; Postgres behaviour not run): two more ways to switch a trigger off exist. (a) `session_replication_role = replica` makes Postgres skip triggers that are in their default mode, and the audit triggers in `0002_audit_chain.sql:28-35` (`audit_entries_append_only`, `audit_checkpoints_append_only` and the two no-truncate triggers) are in that default mode (plain `CREATE OR REPLACE TRIGGER`); in older Postgres only a superuser may set that parameter, in newer versions it can be granted to any role (reviewer's knowledge, unverified). `effective_problems` refuses superusers (`migrate.py:206-209`) but looks for no granted parameter right, and src never mentions `session_replication_role`. Fix: every new trigger AND the four audit triggers are created `ENABLE ALWAYS` (they then fire in every mode), and `effective_problems` reports a role that may set that parameter. (b) A role that is a MEMBER of a table's owner role can run `ALTER TABLE ... DISABLE TRIGGER`; `effective_problems` tests `owner == role` only (`migrate.py:219`), not membership. Fix: add `pg_has_role(role, owner, 'MEMBER')` for every relation owner. Negative tests (Postgres, not run): the runtime role cannot `SET session_replication_role = replica`; it cannot `ALTER TABLE ... DISABLE TRIGGER`; and a superuser test session that sets the parameter still sees the guard refuse the forbidden update (this proves ENABLE ALWAYS). Also (M4): `migrate()` takes its role names as OPTIONAL parameters (`migrate.py:285-288`), so a run with none applies the migrations and no grants, and `cli.py:341-343` only prints a warning. The application cannot see how `migrate` was called, so the refusal belongs at start-up: a live profile connects as its runtime role, runs `effective_problems` for it and refuses to start on any problem (new code; the existing precedent for refusing a live Postgres profile is `app.py:86-89`, which checks only that a purge secret name is set, so the reviewer's 'like app.py:86-89' names the pattern, not a ready-made check). Not built. ROUND 13 ADDITIONS TO THE GUARD (design Nits 1 to 4, each opened in src; nothing built): (i) a second guard on the decision table (Nit 3): a BEFORE INSERT trigger on `approval_decisions` that refuses a decision unless its request is PENDING, `NEW.step` equals the request's `current_step`, `NEW.plan_hash` equals the request's `plan_hash`, `NEW.approver_id` is in `steps[current_step].approver_ids` and differs from `requested_by` (the same tests `decide` makes in code, `requests.py:152-159`). It checks that the record is consistent, not who the person really is: `approver_id` is text the receiver supplies, so a hijacked receiver identity can still name any listed approver (limit, plainly). It breaks `tests/contracts/test_store.py:131-135`, which insert decisions for an approval id (`apr-x`) that has no request (not run). (ii) One writer (Nit 2): `Store.save_approval_decision` (`store/postgres.py:383-385`, Protocol `store/protocol.py:94`, memory store `store/memory.py:168`) is a SECOND writer of `approval_decisions` besides `record_decision`; src has no caller of it (only `tests/contracts/test_store.py:131`, `:132`, `:135`), so the identity table names `record_decision` (`postgres.py:345`) as the receiver's only write path; remove `save_approval_decision` from the Protocol and the stores so that is true in src, and change those test lines (not done). (iii) One state machine in code as well (Nit 1): today the allowed status changes exist only as the Postgres trigger, so the memory store and any other store kind have no equivalent and a test on them proves nothing; put rules (1) to (4) in ONE Python function in the store layer that every store kind calls before it writes, plus one contract test over every store kind that tries each forbidden change; the Postgres trigger stays as the guard against a process that bypasses the code. (iv) Races (Nit 4): the reviewer found none and I did not look for one independently; keep negative test (c) on the real upsert statement (`postgres.py:311`), not a stand-in, because whether a BEFORE UPDATE trigger fires on the DO UPDATE branch of an upsert is the unverified point. Alternative considered and rejected as the only fix: move the tick's UPDATE to another identity (it still holds UPDATE on the same column). NEGATIVE TESTS (prove each fails on the old code): (a) retarget: a human decision (receiver role) approves request A for run R1, then the agent role's `UPDATE approval_requests SET run_id = R2` is refused, and `reconcile` does not execute R2; (b) reset and replay: `UPDATE ... SET status = 'PENDING'` on an APPROVED row is refused; (c) upsert over APPROVED: the old upsert statement with a PENDING row over an APPROVED approval id is refused (after the code change, the plain INSERT fails on the primary key); (d) a direct UPDATE to APPROVED with no decision row is refused, and with a decision row for the wrong step, the wrong `plan_hash` or `reject` is refused; (e) the receiver's `record_decision`, the sweep's escalation and expiry and the resend's UPDATE still work; (f) round 13, B1: as the agent role, `CREATE TEMP TABLE approval_decisions (LIKE approval_decisions)` is refused once TEMPORARY is revoked, and, in a separate test with the right granted back to a test role so the trigger is tested on its own, the agent creates that temporary table with a forged approve row for a PENDING request and `UPDATE approval_requests SET status = 'APPROVED'` is STILL refused with the guard's error (it fails on a guard that uses bare names); round 14 addition to (f): in a second case the agent role creates a temporary function or operator with the name of one the trigger calls (for example `jsonb_array_length`) or sets its own `search_path`, and the forbidden UPDATE is STILL refused (it fails on a function with no pinned `search_path`); (g) round 13, M2: an INSERT of a request for a run with no `needs_approval` gate decision, for a run that is not WAITING_FOR_APPROVAL, with another PENDING request for the same run, or with a `plan_hash` that differs from the gate decision's, is refused, and the normal `request()` path still works; (h) round 13, M3: an UPDATE of `summary`, or of `step_expires_at` outside an escalation, is refused; an escalation by exactly one step works and one by two steps is refused; an EXPIRED change before the due time is refused and one after it works; (i) round 13, Nit 3: a decision for a request that is not PENDING, for the wrong step, or by the requester is refused by the second guard. BREAKAGE LIST (reviewer minor, partly confirmed): the guard breaks fixtures that set APPROVED without a decision: `tests/contracts/test_store.py:103` (UPDATE to APPROVED), `:114` (`_request(status="APPROVED")`, an INSERT of an APPROVED request), `:166` (UPDATE to APPROVED) and `:237` (round 13 read it, codebase Nit 3: inside `test_record_decision_is_atomic` the decision is 'reject' while the request copy is set to APPROVED, so rule (3), which wants 'approve' for APPROVED, refuses it; the earlier reason 'must record a decision first' was wrong for this line, the pair must match); they run on the Postgres contract kind and must record a matching decision first or use the decision path. `:114` is already in this list (design Nit 5, confirmed present). ONE MORE BREAKAGE (codebase Nit 3): `tests/test_p2_postgres.py:289`, `:296` and `:299` (a `migrate` call and two inserts into `audit_checkpoints`; the earlier cite :290 held nothing, found by the citation check of round 25 and re-read by me) asserts that `migrate(fresh_dsn)` returns exactly `["0004_audit_checkpoint_meta", "0005_evidence"]`; any new migration file (0006 and later) is applied by that same call and makes the list longer, so that test changes with the first new migration (the list in the foreign-key note names `:289`, `:296` and `:299`, which are inserts, not this assertion). The reviewer counted 5 and 1 hits by search; I opened the four in `test_store.py` and found none in `tests/test_p7_tick.py` that touches the database directly (`:328` goes to a fake channel). COLUMN-LEVEL UPDATE (design M4, confirmed; the stronger option, the trigger above is the minimum and the backstop): agent and tick `UPDATE (status, current_step, step_expires_at, sent_at, channel_ref)`, receiver `UPDATE (status)` only, nobody `UPDATE (run_id, plan_hash, ...)`. This needs the two UPDATE builders (`postgres.py:316-326` and `:349-355`) to SET only the changed columns (today they SET every column except `approval_id`, so a column grant would make them fail even with unchanged values), a pin test that the agent role has no UPDATE on `run_id`, and `Grant` to carry a column list for UPDATE (today only `purge_cols`, a SELECT list, `migrate.py:34-37`). ROUND 13 (design M5, confirmed in src): `effective_problems` excuses a column-level right only for SELECT (`migrate.py:256`: `if p in allowed or (p == "SELECT" and col in allowed_cols)`), so a role granted `UPDATE (status, ...)` would be reported as a problem for each of those columns. Change `Grant` (a list of UPDATE columns per role) and `effective_problems` (excuse UPDATE for a column in that list) TOGETHER, and add two tests: a role with exactly the listed columns passes, a role with one extra column is reported. Not built. SPEND (reviewer M1, weighed). The agent must be able to add to spend (`add_spend` is an upsert that adds, `postgres.py:416-431`), and a Postgres grant cannot say 'increase only'. Option A, trigger on `spend` and `spend_month`, BEFORE UPDATE: refuse a row whose `usd` is lower than the old value, plus `CHECK (usd >= 0)` so a fresh row cannot be negative. In Postgres the BEFORE UPDATE trigger also fires for the DO UPDATE branch of INSERT .. ON CONFLICT (unverified; the test must run the real upsert path), a fresh INSERT of a key the code never reads is harmless to the cap (it cannot lower an existing row; the cap reads `(agent_id, day)` and `(agent_id, month)`), and the owner or a superuser can disable the trigger and drop the CHECK. Option B, append-only spend: the agent role gets INSERT only (a delta row per charge, `CHECK (delta >= 0)`) and the total is `SUM` on read; no UPDATE exists to abuse and no trigger is needed. Costs of B: it rewrites `add_spend`, `spend_totals` and the memory store (`postgres.py:416-443`, `memory.py:196`), the table grows one row per charge, and the cap check must read the sum and insert the delta atomically (reserve-then-charge is on the money path: uncapped review rounds). Decision proposed: A now (small, keeps the store code), B named as the stronger option if the lead wants no UPDATE right at all; reason for A first: B touches the money path in more places. NOT A SPEND GUARANTEE (design M6, confirmed): the trigger only stops a stored total from being lowered. A hijacked agent that never calls `Limits.charge`, or charges less than the real cost, is not caught; the guard for real spend remains the reserve-before-call budget in code. I confirmed there is no legitimate decrease (`add_spend` only adds, `store/postgres.py:416-431`). BEFORE UPDATE row triggers fire on the DO UPDATE branch of an upsert: unverified; the test must run the real upsert. Relation to C-7: the code-level refusal of a negative amount (`Limits.charge`, both stores) fires first with a clear error, and the trigger is the backstop; they overlap on purpose and are tested separately. AUDIT WRITES (design M7, confirmed): every identity above keeps INSERT on `audit_entries` and `audit_outbox` (each audits its own acts; `migrate.py:57`, `:59`), so any of them can append a forged event, while the project's own rule lists an 'audit writer (append-only)' as an identity of its own. Choices, the lead's (choice (4) at the end): (1) accept it as over-breadth, written down (ROUND 14: this framing understates the harm in one case: a forged `gate.decide` event is a concrete bypass of the approval binding, see ROUND 14 point 3, so (1) alone does not close that case): the chain is tamper-evident (`verify()` flags rewrites) but an identity that may audit can still append a false event; or (2) design one audit-writer function: a Postgres function owned by an `audit_writer` role that computes `seq`, `prev` and `hash` and does the append, with EXECUTE granted to each identity and no direct INSERT; cost: `_append` (`chain.py:180-206`) moves into SQL (Postgres only), a hijacked identity can still append an event with false content (it only loses the ability to forge hashes and rows), and more code on the audit path (uncapped review). ROUND 13 (design M1, confirmed as reasoning; nothing built): option (2) is not written yet, and a function that appends to the audit chain for every identity is where a mistake costs most, so its minimum is: (a) a SECURITY DEFINER function (it runs with its owner's rights, so callers need no INSERT on the audit tables) owned by a role nobody can log in as (a non-LOGIN role) that holds only what the append needs (INSERT and SELECT of `seq`, `hash`, `event_id` on `audit_entries`, INSERT on `audit_outbox`), never a superuser and never the table owner; (b) a `search_path` pinned on the function with `pg_temp` last, or table names built from a fixed schema (the B1 technique; a SECURITY DEFINER function without it runs attacker-named temporary tables with the owner's rights); (c) `REVOKE EXECUTE ... FROM PUBLIC`, then `GRANT EXECUTE` to each identity by name (a new function is executable by everyone by default); (d) the advisory lock taken INSIDE the function, as `chain.py:120` does today, so lock and append are one unit; (e) the hash computed INSIDE the function and equal to what Python's `_hash` makes (`chain.py:68-72`: the JSON list `[prev, seq, at, run_id, kind, body]` written by `json.dumps` with `ensure_ascii=False` and compact separators, then SHA-256), because a hash passed in by the caller adds nothing (a hijacked identity would pass one it made itself); byte-exact agreement with Python's JSON text for every unicode and escape case is a real cost in SQL and needs a test that feeds the same events to both and compares hashes (not designed); (f) negative tests (Postgres, exact class `InsufficientPrivilege`): a role without EXECUTE cannot append; a role with EXECUTE cannot INSERT into `audit_entries` directly; a temporary table named like an audit table, made by the caller, does not change where the function writes; the function refuses a caller-supplied hash. Migration note: `_SAFE_CREATE` (`migrate.py:80-85`) accepts `CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION` and refuses a plain `CREATE FUNCTION`; creating the owner role, `ALTER FUNCTION ... OWNER TO` and `GRANT EXECUTE` inside a migration file were not checked against the guard (not read). Not designed further. ROLLOUT ORDER (reviewer G5; `_apply_grants` runs `REVOKE ALL` on every relation before it re-grants, `migrate.py:170-186`, so a narrowed map takes rights away on the next `migrate`). Two releases: release A adds the new roles, DSN secret names, grant fields (`operator`, `receiver`, `drain`, default empty) and triggers while the `runtime` values stay as they are today (expand); then create the roles and their DSN secrets, deploy each process with its own DSN, and prove end to end: stop and reset by the operator role, an approval by the receiver role, a drain run, the negative tests. Only then release B narrows the `runtime` values (contract), and `migrate` is re-run. Rollback (round 12, design M2, confirmed; the round-11 sentence was wrong): re-running `migrate` with release A's map restores GRANTS only. It does not remove the triggers or constraints of `0006` to `0008`; there is no down-migration (`migrate.py:5`: 'Rollback = restore the snapshot taken before migrating'), and an older release run against the newer database is refused (`migrate.py:320-326`: 'applied migrations have no file in this release'). So the rollback is the snapshot taken before `migrate`, and the CHANGELOG says so. Two more rollout facts: `0009` (the `(seq, signature)` key) breaks `ON CONFLICT (seq) DO NOTHING` at `chain.py:312`, and `checkpoint()` catches the error (`chain.py:315-317`) so signing would stop silently; therefore the code change to `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` (no target) ships one release BEFORE `0009`, or both in the same release. New constraints (`CHECK (usd >= 0)`, the foreign key, the UNIQUE `(seq, hash)`, the trigger rules) can fail on rows already in a live database, and `migrate` applies all files in one transaction and stops (fails closed): pre-check the data with a query that lists offending rows, or add the constraint `NOT VALID` and `VALIDATE CONSTRAINT` in a later step, and list both in the CHANGELOG upgrade notes. Never narrow the runtime map before the operator and receiver roles and DSNs are tested, or `kill switch` writes and approvals stop working at the next `migrate`. Release notes (CHANGELOG upgrade steps) say this. MIGRATION NUMBERS (reviewer N5; `0001` to `0005` exist; the ledger check wants no gaps, `migrate.py:97-107`; the numbers below are PROPOSED, all distinct, and are assigned when each change MERGES, not now: `0009` is unusable if `0006` to `0008` slip): `0006` foreign key and unique `(seq, hash)` for `audit_checkpoints` (X-5 (f) (iii)); `0007` spend trigger and `CHECK (usd >= 0)` (this row); `0008` approval status guard (this row); `0009` checkpoint key `(seq, signature)` only if the lead chooses it (X-5 (f)). The round-10 text gave `0006` to two different changes; that is corrected here. CODE SHAPE (round 12 adds, design M3, confirmed): a role-specific `build()` profile. `app.build` creates the signer, anchor, llm, executor and channel for every process (`app.py:144-145`, `:161-162`, `:170-174`), so an operator or receiver process built through it would hold the signer and the model credentials. Add one build profile per process kind (operator: store, audit and limits only, no signer, anchor, llm or executor; receiver: store, audit, channel and approvals only) and a negative test: the operator process cannot resolve the signer secret or an llm secret. Also: new `Grant` fields default to nothing; optional new parameters on `migrate()`; each new role in `named` so the not-members check (`migrate.py:333-339`) sees it; own `_apply_grants` plus `effective_problems` block; the `cli.py` changes listed in the single-dependency section; a non-Postgres outline is in the single-dependency section. Negative tests (Postgres, assert `InsufficientPrivilege` or the trigger's error, and prove each against the OLD grant map): (a) agent refused INSERT and UPDATE on `kill_switch`, operator allowed; (b) agent cannot lower `spend` or `spend_month` (the real upsert, and a plain UPDATE), and an UPDATE that adds still works; (c) agent refused INSERT on `approval_decisions`, receiver allowed; (d) the status guard tests above; (e) receiver and operator refused DELETE everywhere and every write outside the lists; (f) `effective_problems` returns nothing for each role and the roles are refused as members of each other. Over-breadth review: the receiver still holds UPDATE on `approval_requests` (guarded by the trigger) and the operator holds `kill_switch` writes; both are the minimum the code uses. Not checked: whether a deployment really uses one DSN today (`app.py:146`; no second DSN setting exists except `purge_dsn_secret_name`, `backends/table.py:120`), whether the receiver runs in the agent's process, and all Postgres behaviour. ROUND 14 (design review r14 against the proposed guard; every src fact below was opened by me, every Postgres statement is the reviewer's knowledge and UNVERIFIED; nothing built or run; severity of this row stays Major, see the status above). POINT 1 (Major, function and operator lookup): written into fix (1), (3) and test (f) above. POINT 2 (Major, spend option A can be sidestepped), confirmed in src: `spend` has the key (`agent_id`, `day`) (`migrations/0001_core.sql:87-91`) and `spend_month` the key (`agent_id`, `month`) (`0003_approvals_tick.sql:15-19`); the runtime role holds SELECT, INSERT and UPDATE on both (`migrate.py:53-54`). Option A refuses a LOWER `usd` but not a changed key, so `UPDATE spend SET day = <another date>` or `SET agent_id = 'x'` passes; `spend_totals` (`postgres.py:433-442`) then finds no row for today and reads 0, and `Limits.blocked` (`limits.py:24-29`) never fires. The per-run cap has the same shape: it reads `store.get_run(...).cost_usd >= cap` (`agent_loop.py:256-257`) and the runtime role has UPDATE on `runs` (`migrate.py:47`), so lowering `runs.cost_usd` defeats it. Fix, so option A means something: freeze the key columns (`agent_id`, `day`, `month`) on both spend tables, refuse any decrease of `usd` on both (as written), and make `runs.cost_usd` monotonic (never lower); I did not open the other `runs` columns, so a full frozen-or-monotonic list for `runs` is still to be written. Negative tests: an UPDATE that changes a key column is refused; an UPDATE that lowers `usd` is refused; an UPDATE that lowers `runs.cost_usd` is refused; an UPDATE that adds still works; the real upsert path is run. POINT 3 (Major, the approval binding rests on rows the agent role can write; the earlier framing 'an event with false content, accept as over-breadth' understated it), confirmed in src: `execute` takes the approved digest of the restored values from the LAST `gate.decide` audit event of the plan (`bound[-1]`, `agent_loop.py:845-867`); every identity keeps INSERT on `audit_entries` (`migrate.py:57`); the restored values come from the `redaction_maps` row (read at `agent_loop.py:292`), which the runtime role may INSERT and UPDATE (`migrate.py:65`). The approved `plan_hash` covers placeholders, not the restored values (the reviewer's statement; I did not open how the plan hash is built). So a hijacked identity can rewrite the map, append a forged `gate.decide` event whose digest matches the new values, and `execute` then runs the altered values. If `restored_key` is set the identity also needs that key (`agent_loop.py:273-278`); with no key the digest is a plain SHA-256 that anyone can compute. This is a concrete bypass of the approval binding, not a harmless false event, and the status guard does NOT close it. Needed (not decided, not built; studio head choice (5)(g)): fix the digest of the approved restored values in a place the agent role cannot write, for example in the approval request at INSERT, frozen by the guard, and compare it at `execute`, or in another value the agent cannot write. Negative test: change the redaction map after approval and, even with a forged `gate.decide` event appended, the run must stop. POINT 4 (Major, rollout): in release A the agent still holds INSERT on `approval_decisions` (`migrate.py:51`); it can insert a forged approve row, the list of approvers (`steps`) is written by the agent itself when it inserts the request (`requests.py:97-107`) and can name any approver id, the consistency trigger of round 13 (Nit 3) then passes, and the agent sets the request to APPROVED. So release A gives NO protection against self-approval, and the UPDATE-path negative tests pass in release A while self-approval stays open. Say so in the CHANGELOG upgrade steps, run the negative tests against the release B grant map, and consider revoking INSERT on `approval_decisions` from the agent role already in release A (choice (5)(f); cost: approvals stop until the receiver has its own DSN). POINT 5 (Major, INSERT rule and UPDATE rule). (a) The INSERT rule reads `gate_decisions` and `runs`, which the same identity can write (`migrate.py:47`, `:49`), so against a hijacked identity it is circular: it protects against bugs and partial misuse, not against that identity. `step_expires_at` is unconstrained at INSERT (the agent sets it, `requests.py:103`): a value in the past is due at once, so the next sweep expires or escalates the request immediately; bound it to about `now()` plus `steps[0].timeout_s`, with the same stated clock allowance as rule (4). (b) The frozen-column list is a denylist and so open by default: it omits `approval_id` and `schema_version`, and any column a later migration adds (`sent_at` and `channel_ref` came with `0003`). Checked in src: both update builders SET every column except `approval_id` (`postgres.py:316-326`, `:349-355`), so `schema_version` is written back on every update (with its old value), while `approval_id` is only the WHERE key there; the reviewer's phrase 'written by update builders' is therefore true for `schema_version` and not for `approval_id`, but a direct UPDATE of `approval_id` by a hijacked identity is still not stopped by a denylist, so the point stands. A changed `schema_version` may make `model_validate` fail in the `list_approvals` scan of `tick.reconcile` (`tick.py:88`); UNVERIFIED. Fix: invert it to an allowlist of MUTABLE columns (`status`, `current_step`, `step_expires_at`, `sent_at`, `channel_ref`) and compare `to_jsonb(NEW)` minus those columns with `to_jsonb(OLD)` minus those columns, so every other column, present and future, is frozen; this replaces the frozen list in UPDATE rule (1). Add BEFORE DELETE and BEFORE TRUNCATE refusal triggers, as `0002_audit_chain.sql:28-35` has for the audit tables. POINT 6 (Minor, error mapping and clock): `decide` (`requests.py:171`) and the sweep (`requests.py:211`) catch only `StaleState`; a trigger refusal is another database error, escapes the sweep loop, and later due requests wait. Give the guard's refusals a distinct SQLSTATE, map it to a refusal result in both places, decide between `clock_timestamp()` and `now()` (`now()` is the start of the transaction) or say why, and test clock skew in both directions (application clock ahead and behind). POINT 7 (Minor): `ENABLE ALWAYS` for the four existing audit triggers needs a new migration of its own, because `0002` is released and a released file may not change (`migration_numbers.py` header); the numbers proposed above (`0006` to `0009`) list none, so add one (number assigned when it merges). From the source text neither the CREATE scan (`migrate.py:80-85`, `:134`) nor the refusal list (`migration_numbers.py:633-639`) names `ALTER TABLE`, so it is probably not refused by the scanner; whether Postgres and the scanner accept `ALTER TABLE ... ENABLE ALWAYS TRIGGER` is UNVERIFIED and needs a test. POINT 8 (Minor, audit-writer function): a caller that supplies `at`, `run_id` and `kind` can backdate an event and can pass a `run_id` or `kind` that disagrees with the body; `verify()` compares only the event id with the body (`chain.py:397-410`). Rules to add: take `at` from the database clock, written like Python's `isoformat(timespec='microseconds')`; derive `run_id`, `kind` and the event id from the body and refuse a mismatch. Hash parity (`chain.py:68-72`): the SQL text must equal `json.dumps(..., ensure_ascii=False, separators=(',', ':'))` byte for byte, needs a UTF-8 database, and Postgres JSON text uses a comma plus a space; a NUL character cannot be stored and a lone surrogate fails on the Python side (UNVERIFIED). Add a cross-implementation hash test with non-ASCII, control characters and quotes. POINT 9 (Minor, receiver rights wider than implied): the receiver holds whole-row UPDATE on `approval_requests` (its update builder sets every column, `postgres.py:349-355`) and the trigger is the only stop, so a hijacked receiver can still name any listed approver id. Make its UPDATE column-level (`status` only) in the same release as the identity split (studio head choice (5)(d)). UNVERIFIED on Postgres (reviewer list, kept): whether a temporary schema is searched for functions and operators only when named; whether `session_replication_role` can be granted to a non-superuser; whether BEFORE UPDATE fires on the DO UPDATE branch of an upsert; `TG_TABLE_SCHEMA` with `EXECUTE format()`; `ENABLE ALWAYS` against the scanner; a shape check that refuses a changed `schema_version`; hash parity. Confirmed sound by the reviewer: B1 is a real Blocker for the guard as written; the three-part repair has the right shape once point 1 is added; the retarget, reset-and-replay, upsert and summary-freeze analysis matches the source; the `session_replication_role` point is real; the owner-membership gap is real (`migrate.py:219` tests owner equals role only). Read from source, not run.
